“Positive. Always like that in the evening, moreover, she wear her jewels.”

“You noticed what they were?”

“Same like always﻿—same necklace out of pearls, same rings, diamond and sapphire, two on one hand, one the other﻿—I see them when she open that bag.”

“Mr. Bellamy was a person of moderate means, wasn’t he, as far as you know?”

“Oh, everybody what there is around here knows he wasn’t no John P. Rockfeller, I guess.”

“Do you believe that the stones were genuine?”

Mr. Orsini, thus appealed to as an expert, waxed eloquent and expansive. “Oh, positive. That I know for one absolute sure thing.”

“Tell us just how, won’t you?”

“Well, that house girl, Nellie, one night she tell me that Miz’ Bell’my have left one of her rings at the club where she wash her hands, but that Miz’ Bell’my just laugh and say she should worry herself, because all those rings and her pearls they are insure big, and if she lose those, she go out and buy herself a new house and a auto car, and maybe a police dog too.”

“I see. Had it ever occurred to you that Mrs. Bellamy was using the cottage at Orchards for other purposes than piano practice, Mr. Orsini?”

===Chinese===

奥尔西尼把那双锃亮的眼睛转向兰伯特先生；兰伯特红润的脸已摆出一副极不待客的神情，不过他还是往那副洪亮的嗓音里注入了一种不祥的和蔼。“凭您那双警觉的、什么都逃不过的眼睛，呃——奥尔西尼先生，您可曾留意到贝拉米太太经过大门时穿的衣裳？”

“哦，那可不。一条白裙子，毛茸茸的，一件黑披肩，薄得很，几乎看得透——兴许差一点，可差不多。”

“戴帽子了吗？”

“头上是一条小黑纱巾，她还在脖子上绕了两圈，兴许三圈。路易吉这双眼睛——”

“好了。您看得出她戴没戴珠宝吗？”

“看得出。再说，晚上她总是那样子，浑身戴满珠宝。”

“您留意到是些什么了吗？”

“跟平常一样——还是那条珍珠项链，还是那几枚戒指，钻石的、蓝宝石的，一只手两枚，另一只一枚——她开包的时候我瞧见的。”

“就您所知，贝拉米先生家境平平，对吧？”

“哦，这一带的人谁都知道，他可不是什么约翰·P·洛克菲勒，我琢磨。”

“您相信那些宝石是真的吗？”

奥尔西尼先生被人当专家请教，顿时健谈豪迈起来。“哦，那可不。这个我知道得千真万确。”

“可否赐告您是怎么知道的？”

“嗯，那个女仆内莉，有天夜里告诉我，说贝拉米太太在俱乐部洗手时落下一枚戒指，可贝拉米太太只笑笑，说她操哪门子心，那些戒指和珍珠全上了大额保险，真丢了，她就出去给自己买栋新房子、一辆汽车，兴许再添条警犬。”

“我明白了。奥尔西尼先生，您可曾想到过，贝拉米太太用奥查兹（Orchards）那间小屋，为的并不是练琴？”

===Original===

Orsini’s smile flashed so generously that it revealed three really extravagant gold fillings. “Well, me, I don’t miss many things, maybe you guess. After she get that key three-four times, I think to myself, ‘Luigi, it is funny thing that nevair she give you back that key until the day after, and always those evenings she go out by herself﻿—most generally when Mr. Bell’my he stay in town to work.’ So one of those nights when she ask for that key I permit myself to take a small little stroll up the road in Orchards, and sure thing, there is a light in that cottage and a auto car outside the door. Sufficient! I look no further. Me, I am a man of the world, you comprehend.”

“Obviously.”

“Just a moment, Mr. Lambert,” interrupted Judge Carver. “Is your cross-examination going to take some time?”

“Quite a time, I believe, Your Honour.”

“Then I think it best that we adjourn for the noon recess, as it is already after twelve. The Court stands adjourned until one-ten.”

---

“Well, here’s where we get our comic relief,” said the reporter with unction. “That son of sunny Italy is going to give us an enviable imitation of a three-ringed circus and a bag of monkeys before he and Lambert get through with each other, or I miss my guess. He’s got a look in his eye that is worth the price of admission alone. What’s your mature opinion of him?”

“I think that he’s beguiling,” said the redheaded girl somewhat listlessly. Little shadows were under her gray eyes, and she curled small limp paws about a neglected notebook. Something in the drooping shoulders under the efficient jacket suggested an exhausted baby in need of a crib and a bottle of hot milk and a firm and friendly tucking in. She made a halfhearted effort to overtake an enormous yawn that was about to engulf her, and then surrendered plaintively.

“Bored?” inquired the real reporter, his countenance illuminated by an expression of agreeable surprise.

===Chinese===

奥尔西尼的微笑挥洒得那么大方，露出三处着实铺张的金牙。“嗯，我这个人，漏掉的事不多，你们兴许猜得到。她来取那把钥匙取了三四回之后，我心里就琢廑：‘路易吉，这事怪得很，她回回都到第二天才还你钥匙，而且那几个晚上她总是独自出门——十有八九是贝拉米先生进城加班的时候。’于是有一晚她又来取钥匙，我就准许自己到奥查兹那条路上小小溜达了一趟，千真万确，小屋里亮着灯，门外停着一辆汽车。够了！我不再往下看了。我嘛，是个见过世面的人，你们懂的。”

“可想而知。”

“且慢，兰伯特先生，”卡弗法官打断道，“您的盘问还要花不少时间吗？”

“想必不少，庭上。”

“那我看最好休午庭，已经过十二点了。本庭休庭至一点十分。”

---

“好啦，咱们的插科打译来了。”记者煞有介事地说。“等他和兰伯特交完手，这位阳光意大利之子准会给我们表演一场三环马戏外加一袋猴子，错不了。他眼里那副神气，单凭这个就值回票价。您对他有何高见？”

“我觉得他挺迷人。”红头发姑娘有点没精打采地说。她的灰眼睛下挂着淡淡的黑影，一双绵软的小爪子蜷在冷落的笔记本上。那件干练外套下耷拉的双肩，透着一股倦极的娃娃气，正需要一张小床、一瓶热牛奶，外加一次坚定而友好的掖被。她有气无力地想把即将吞没她的一个大呵欠赶回去，随即哀怨地投降了。

“腻烦了？”真记者问，脸上焕发出愉快的惊奇。

===Original===

“Bored?” cried the lady beside him in a voice at once scornful and outraged. “Bored? I’m half destroyed with excitement. I can’t sleep anymore. I go back to the boarding house every night and sit up in front of a gas stove with an orange-and-magenta comforter over my shoulders that ought to warm the dead, writing up my notes until all hours; and then I put a purple comforter over my knees and a muffler over my nose, and get an apple and sit there alternately gnawing the apple and my fingers and trying to work out who did it until even the cats stop singing under my window and the sky begins to get that nice, appealing slate colour that’s so prettily referred to as dawn. And even then I don’t know who did it.”

“Don’t you, indeed?” inquired the reporter severely, looking irritated and anxious. “Haven’t you any sense at all, you little idiot? Listen, I know a place just two blocks down where you can get some fairly decent hot soup. You go and drink about a quart of it and then trot along home and turn in, and I’ll do your notes for you tonight so well that your boss will double your salary in the morning﻿—and if you’re very good and sleep eighteen hours, I may tell you who did the murder.”

The redheaded girl, who had shuddered fastidiously at the offer of fairly decent soup, eyed him ungratefully as she extracted a packet of salted peanuts from the capacious pouch that served her as handbag, commissary, and dressing table.

“Thank you kindly,” she said. “My boss wrote me two special-delivery letters yesterday to say that I was doing far the best stuff that was coming up out of Redfield﻿—far. He said that the three clippings that I sent him of your stuff showed promise﻿—he did, honestly.﻿ ﻿… I think that soup’s terrible, and this is the first time in my life that I’ve been able to stay up as late as I pleased without anyone sending me to bed. I’m mad about it.﻿ ﻿… Have some peanuts?”

“No, thanks,” said the reporter, rising abruptly. “Anything I can get you outside?”

===Chinese===

“腻烦？”他身旁这位姑娘喊起来，声音又鄙夷又愤慨。“腻烦？我激动得半条命都没了。我根本睡不着觉了。我每天夜里回到供膳宿舍，坐在煤气炉前，肩上裹一条橘红夹紫的毯子——那毯子暖得能焐热死人——赶笔记赶到深更半夜；然后我膝盖上再盖一条紫毯子，鼻子上围条围巾，拿一只苹果，坐在那儿把苹果和手指头轮着啃，琢廑到底是谁干的，一直琢廑到我窗下的猫都不叫了，天开始泛出那种好看、动人的石板色——人们美其名曰‘黎明’。可到了那会儿我还是不知道是谁干的。”

“是吗，还真不知道？”记者声色俱厉地问，又急又恼。“你到底有没有一点脑子，小傻瓜？听着，往下过两个街口有家店，热汤还算像样。你去喝上一夸脱，然后小跑回家钻被窝，今晚的笔记我替你写，写得让你老板明早给你薪水翻倍——你要是乖乖睡上十八个钟头，我兴许还告诉你凶手是谁。”

红头发姑娘——听到“还算像样的热汤”就已矜持地打了个寒战——一面不知好歹地白他一眼，一面从那只兼作手袋、食品柜和梳妆台的大口袋里摸出一包咸花生米。

“多谢好意，”她说，“我老板昨天给我写了两封快递信，说雷德菲尔德发出去的稿子里，我写的远远是最好的——远远。他还说，我寄给他的三段你的剪报颇有前途——真的，不骗你。……我觉得那汤糟透了；再说，这辈子头一回，我想熬到多晚就熬到多晚，没人撞我上床。我迷这个迷得要命。……来点花生米？”

“不了，谢了。”记者说着霍地起身。“要我出去给你捎点什么吗？”

===Original===

“You’re cross!” wailed the redheaded girl, her eyes round with panic and contrition. “You are﻿—you are﻿—you’re absolutely furious. Wait, please﻿—please, or I’ll hang on to your coattails and make a scene. The real reason I don’t go out and get soup is because I don’t dare. If I went away even for a minute, something might happen, and then I wouldn’t ever sleep again. Someone might get my seat﻿—didn’t you see that fat, sinful-looking old lady who got the *Gazette* girl’s place yesterday? She wouldn’t go even when three officers and the sheriff told her she had to, and the *Gazette* girl had to sit on a stool in the gallery, and she said she had such a rushing of rage in her ears that she couldn’t hear anything that anyone said all afternoon. So, you see﻿—And I would like a ham sandwich and I think that you write better than Conrad, and I apologize, and if you’ll tell me who did the murder, I’ll tell you. And please hurry, because I hope you won’t be gone long.”

“You’re a nice little nut,” said the reporter, and he beamed on her forgivingly, “and I like you. I like the way your nose turns up and your mouth turns down, and I like that funny little hat you wear.﻿ ﻿… I’ll make it in two jumps. Watch me!”

The redheaded girl watched him obediently, her face pink and her eyes bright under the funny little hat. When the door opened to let him out, she plunged her eyes apprehensively for a moment into the silent, pushing, heaving mob behind the policeman’s broad blue shoulders, shivered, and turned them resolutely away.

“If I were convicted of murder tomorrow,” thought the redheaded girl passionately, “they’d shove just like that to see me hanged. Ugh! What’s the matter with us?”

She eyed with an expression of profound distaste the plump lady just beyond her, conscientiously eating stuffed eggs out of a shoe box. So smug, so virtuous, so pompadoured and lynx-eyed﻿—Her eyes moved hastily on to the pair of giggling flappers exchanging powder puffs and anecdotes over a box of maple caramels; on to the round-shouldered youth with the unattractive complexion and unpleasant tie; on to the pretty thing with overflushed cheeks and overbright eyes above her sable scarf and beneath her Paris hat. The redheaded girl wrenched her eyes back to the empty space where there sat, tranquil and aloof, the memory of the prisoner at the bar.

===Chinese===

“你生气了！”红头发姑娘哀声叫道，眼睛瞪得溜圆，又是慌张又是悔恨。“你生气了——生气了——你气坏了。等等，求你——求你，不然我就揪住你的衣摆大闹一场。我不出去喝汤的真正原因，是我不敢。哪怕走开一分钟，都可能出事，那我就再也睡不着了。座位可能被人抢——你没瞧见昨天那个肥头大耳、一脸罪愆的老太太占了《公报》姑娘的位子吗？三名警官加治安官叫她走她都不走，《公报》姑娘只好坐到旁听席的凳子上，她说耳朵里血往头上涨，一下午别人说什么都听不见。所以，你瞧——我想要一只火腿三明治，还有我觉得你写得比康拉德好，我道歉，还有你要是告诉我凶手是谁，我也告诉你。请你快点，因为我希望你别去太久。”

“你是个可爱的小疯子。”记者说着，宽宏大量地朝她堆下笑来，“我喜欢你。我喜欢你鼻子朝上翘、嘴巴往下弯的样子，我喜欢你戴的那顶滑稽小帽。……我两蹦就到。瞧好了！”

红头发姑娘听话地望着他，滑稽小帽下脸颊粉红，眼睛发亮。门开处送他出去，她忧心忡忡地把目光探进警察宽阔蓝肩后那一片默默推挤翻涌的人群，打了个寒战，毅然把目光收了回来。

“要是明天我被判了谋杀罪，”红头发姑娘激愤地想，“他们也会这么推推掳掳地挤着看我上绞架。呸！我们这是怎么了？”

她带着深恶痛绝的神情打量紧挨着她的那位胖太太，此人正一丝不苟地从鞋盒里掏夹心蛋吃。那么自得，那么道貌岸然，那么发髻高耸、目光如猞猁——她赶紧把眼睛挪开，挪到正围着一盒枫糖太妃交换粉扑和八卦、咯咯傻笑的两个轻佻姑娘身上；再挪到那个面色晦暗、领带恶俗的驼背青年身上；再挪到貂皮围巾之上、巴黎帽檐之下那张双颊过红、双眼过亮的俏脸蛋上。红头发姑娘猛地把眼睛拽回来，落在那片空座位上——被告席上囚徒的身影，曾在那里安然而孤傲地坐着，此刻只剩记忆。

===Original===

It was good to be able to forget those hot, hungry, cruel faces, so sleek and safe and triumphant, and to remember that other face under the shadow of the small felt hat, cool and controlled and gay﻿—yes, gay, for all the shadows that beset it. Only﻿—what thoughts were weaving behind that bright brow, those steady lips? Thoughts of terror, of remorse, of bitterness and horror and despair? If you were strong enough to strike down a laughing girl who barred your path, you would be strong enough to keep your lips steady, wouldn’t you?

The redheaded girl stared about her wildly; she felt suddenly small and cold and terrified. Where was the reporter? What a long time﻿—Oh, someone had opened a window. It was only the wind of autumn that was blowing so cold then, not the wind of death. What was it those little newsboys were calling outside, yelping like puppies in the gray square?

“Extra, Extra! All about the mysterious﻿—”

“Well,” said the reporter’s voice at her elbow, tense with some suppressed excitement, “this is the time he did it! No enterprising Filipino and housemaid around this time. Read that and weep!”

Across the flimsy sheet of the Redfield *Home News* it ran in letters three inches high: Ex-fiancé of Murdered Girl Blows Out Brains. Prominent Clubman Found Dead in Garden at Eleven Forty-five This Morning.

“I’ve got a peach of a story started over the wires this minute,” said the reporter exultantly. “Here, boy, rush this stuff and beat it back for more. I couldn’t get your sandwich.”

“Well,” said the redheaded girl in a small awed voice﻿—“well, then, that means that he did it himself, doesn’t it? That means that he couldn’t stand it any longer because he killed her, doesn’t it?”

“Or it means that he good and damn well knew that Susan Ives did it,” muttered the reporter, shaken from Olympian calm to frenzied activity. “Here, boy! Boy! Hi, you, rush this﻿—and take off the ear muffs. It’s a hundred-to-one bet that he knew that Sue’d done it, and that he’d as good as put the knife in her hand by telling her where, when, and why it should be managed.﻿ ﻿… Here, boy!”

“He didn’t!” said the redheaded girl fiercely. “He didn’t know it. How could﻿—”

“The Court!” sang Ben Potts.

“How could he know whether she﻿—”

“Silence!” intoned Ben reprovingly.

Mr. Orsini and Mr. Lambert were both heading purposefully for the witness box.

===Chinese===

能忘掉那些滚烫、饥饿、残忍的面孔——那么光鲜、安稳、得意——而记起小毡帽帽檐阴影下那张另样的脸，冷静、克持、快活——是的，快活，任凭阴影四围——总是好的。只是——那明亮的前额、那坚定的嘴唇后面，正在编织什么样的念头？是恐惧、悔恨，还是怨毒、恐怖与绝望？你若有力气打倒一个挡路的欢笑姑娘，也就有力气让嘴唇保持坚定，对不对？

红头发姑娘发狂似的四下张望；她突然觉得自己渺小、冰冷、惊恐。记者去哪儿了？怎么这么久——哦，有人开了一扇窗。方才吹得这么冷的原来是秋风，不是死亡之风。外头灰色广场上，那些小报童像小狗似的尖声叫卖的是什么？

“号外，号外！神秘案的内幕——”

“嘿，”记者的声音在她肘边响起，绷着某种压抑的激动，“这回可是他自己干的！这回可没有上心的菲律宾人和女仆了。看了哭去吧！”

雷德菲尔德《家庭新闻》那页薄纸上，三英寸高的大字赫然横贯：被害姑娘的前未婚夫饮弹自尽。知名俱乐部人士今晨十一时四十五分被发现陈尸花园。

“我这会儿已经把一条绝好的稿子发上报线了。”记者喜不自胜。“喂，小子，火速把这个送去，再跑回来拿。你的三明治没买着。”

“那么，”红头发姑娘用小小的、敬畏的声音说——“那么，这就说明是他自己干的，是不是？这说明他因为杀了她，再也扛不住了，是不是？”

“要么，就说明他清清楚楚、明明白白地知道是苏珊·艾夫斯干的。”记者咕哝道，从奥林匹斯山上的镇定被震成了疯狂的忙乱。“喂，小子！小子！嘿，说你呢，火速送这个——把耳罩摘了。十赌一，他知道是苏干的，而且他把时间、地点、缘由一五一十告诉她，就等于亲手把刀塞进了她手里。……喂，小子！”

“他才没有！”红头发姑娘激烈地说。“他不知道。他怎么可能——”

“法官驾到！”本·波茨唱道。

“他怎么可能知道她是不是——”

“肃静！”本板着脸吟诵。

奥尔西尼先生和兰伯特先生都目标明确地朝证人席走去。

===Original===

“Now you’ve just told us, Mr. Orsini, that you were able to see Mrs. Ives’s face when you looked down from your window in the garage as clearly as you see mine. Can you give us an idea of the approximate distance from the garage to the house?”

“Positive. The distance from the middle of the garage door to the middle of the front porch step, it is”﻿—he glanced earnestly at a small slip of paper hitherto concealed in one massive paw, and divulged a portion of its contents to his astounded interrogator﻿—“it is forty-seven feet five inches and one half inch.”

“What?”

Mr. Orsini contemplated with pardonable gratification the unfeigned stupor that adorned the massive countenance now thrust incredulously forward. “Also I can now tell you the space between the front gate and the door﻿—one hunnerd forty-three feet and a quarter of a inch,” he announced rapidly and benevolently. “Also from the fence out to the road﻿—eleven feet nine inch and a﻿—”

Judge Carver’s gavel fell with a crash over the enraptured roar that swept the courtroom. “One more demonstration of this kind and I clear the Court. This is a trial for murder, not a burlesque performance. You, sir, answer the questions that are put to you, when they are put. What’s that object in your hand?”

Mr. Orsini dangled the limp yellow article hopefully under the judge’s fine nose. “The instrument with which I make the measure,” he explained, all modest pride. “What you call a measure of tape. The card on which I make the notes as well.”

Judge Carver schooled his momentarily shaken countenance to its customary rigidity and turned a lion tamer’s eye on the smothered hilarity of the press. The demoralized Lambert pulled himself together with a mighty effort; a junior counsel emitted a convulsive snort; only Mr. Farr remained entirely unmoved. Pensive, nonchalant and mildly sardonic, he bestowed a perfunctory glance on the measure of tape and returned to a critical perusal of some notes of his own, which he had been studying intently since he had surrendered his witness to his adversary. The adversary, his eyes still bulging, returned once more to the charge.

“May I ask you what caused you to burden yourself with this invaluable mass of information?”

===Chinese===

“奥尔西尼先生，您刚才告诉我们，您从车库的窗子往下看，能把艾夫斯太太的脸看得跟我这张一样清楚。那您能否告诉我们，从车库到宅子大概有多远？”

“那可不。从车库门中间到前廊台阶中间，那段距离是”——他恳切地瞟了一眼先前藏在肥厚大掌里的小纸条，向惊呆了的盘问者泄露了其中一部分——“是四十七英尺五英寸又半英寸。”

“什么？”

奥尔西尼先生带着情有可原的得意，端详着那张此刻难以置信地凑上前的宽脸上毫不掩饰的目瞪口呆。“我如今还能告诉你们前大门到房门的距离——一百四十三英尺又四分之一英寸。”他语速飞快、和蔼可亲地宣布。“还有栅栏到路边的——十一英尺九英寸又——”

满庭爆出如醉如痴的轰笑，卡弗法官的法槌轰然砸下。“再这么表演一次，我就清场。这里是谋杀案审判，不是滑稽戏。你，先生，问什么答什么，什么时候问什么时候答。你手里拿的是什么东西？”

奥尔西尼先生满怀希望地把那软塌塌的黄物件悬到法官高贵的鼻子底下。“我用来测量的仪器。”他解释道，满心谦逊的骄傲。“就是你们说的卷尺。还有我记数字用的卡片。”

卡弗法官把霎时动摇的脸色重新扳回惯常的僵硬，朝报席那边强忍的欢腾甩去驯狮人的目光。军心已乱的兰伯特费了九牛二虎之力才稳住自己；一位初级律师迸出一声抽气般的哼笑；唯有法尔先生全然不为所动。他若有所思、漫不经心，又略带讥诮，朝那卷尺草草投去一瞥，便重新细读起自己的几页笔记来——自打把证人交给对手，他就一直在专心研读。而对手，眼睛还鼓着，再一次发起进攻。

“请问，是什么促使您给自己背上这一大堆宝贵情报的？”

===Original===

“Surest thing you may ask. I do it because me, I am well familiar with the questions what all smart high-grade lawyers put when in the court﻿—like, could you then tell us how high were those steps, and how many were those minutes, and how far were those walls﻿—all things like that they like to go and ask, every time, sure like shooting.”

“I see. A careful student of our little eccentricities. How has it happened that your crowded life has afforded you the leisure to make so exhaustive a study of our habits?”

“Once again, more slow?” suggested the student affably.

“How have you happened to become so familiar with court life?”

“Oh, me, I am not so familiar with it as that. Once﻿—twice﻿—that is enough for one who know how to use his eyes and ear﻿—more is not necessary.”

“No, as you say, once or twice ought to be enough; it’s a pity that you’ve found it necessary to extend your experience. Orsini, have you ever been in jail?”

“Who﻿—me?” The glittering smile with which Mr. Orsini was in the habit of decorating his periods was not completely withdrawn, but it became slightly more reticent. His lambent eyes roved reproachfully in the direction of Mr. Farr, who seemed more absorbed than ever in his notes. “In what kind of a jail you mean?”

Mr. Lambert looked obviously disconcerted. “I mean jail﻿—any kind of a jail.”

“Was it up on a hill, perhaps, this jail?” inquired his victim helpfully.

“On a hill? What’s that got to do with it? How should I know whether it was on a hill?”

“A high hill, mebbe, with trees all about it?” Once more Orsini’s hands were eloquent.

“All right, all right, were you ever in a jail on a hill with trees around it?”

Orsini gazed blandly into the irate and contemptuous countenance thrust toward him. “No, sair,” he replied regretfully. “If that jail was up on a hill with trees around it, then I was not in that jail.”

===Chinese===

“当然可以问。我这么做，是因为我太熟悉那些聪明的高级律师在法庭上爱问的问题了——比如，那你能告诉我们那些台阶有多高吗，那些分钟有几个吗，那些墙有多远吗——这类事他们就爱问，回回必问，百发百中。”

“明白了。是我们这些小怪癖的细心研究者。您那么忙的人生，怎么偏偏有闲工夫对我们的习惯做这么彻底的研究？”

“再说一遍，慢点？”这位研究者客气地建议。

“您怎么会这么熟悉法庭生活？”

“哦，我，也没那么熟。一回——两回——对一个会用眼睛耳朵的人来说，这就够了——多了不必。”

“是啊，如您所说，一两回应该够了；可惜您觉得有必要扩展经验。奥尔西尼，你坐过牢吗？”

“谁——我？”奥尔西尼先生惯于用来装点语气的闪亮微笑并未完全收回，只是稍稍含蓄了些。他那双流光的眼睛哀怨地飘向法尔先生，后者似乎比刚才更专注于笔记。“您说的是哪种牢？”

兰伯特先生明显慌了神。“我说的是牢——随便哪种牢。”

“那牢，兴许是在山头上？”他的猎物热心地反问。

“山头？这有什么关系？我怎么知道它在不在山头上？”

“高山头，兴许是，四面全是树？”奥尔西尼的双手又一次滔滔不绝。

“好，好，你可曾坐过一座四面有树的山头上的牢？”

奥尔西尼安详地望着朝他凑过来的那张怒气冲冲、满脸鄙夷的脸。“没有，长官。”他遗憾地答道。“那牢要真是在四面有树的山头上，那我可没坐过那牢。”

===Original===

Once more the courtroom, reckless of the gavel, yielded to helpless and hilarious uproar, and for this time they were spared. One look at Mr. Lambert’s countenance, a full moon in the throes of apoplexy, had undermined even Judge Carver’s iron reserves. The gavel remained idle while he indulged himself in a severe attack of coughing behind a large and protective handkerchief. The redheaded girl was using a more minute one to mop her eyes when she paused, startled and incredulous. Across the courtroom, Patrick and his wife Susan were laughing into each other’s eyes, for one miraculous moment the gay and carefree comrades of old; for one moment﻿—and then, abruptly, memory swept back her lifted veil and they sat staring blankly at the dreadful havoc that lay between them, who had been wont to seek each other in laughter. Slowly, painfully, Sue Ives wrenched her eyes back to their schooled vigilance, and after an interminable breath, Pat Ives turned his haunted ones back to the window, beyond which the sky was still blue. Only in that second’s wait the redheaded girl had seen the dark flush sweep across his pallor, and the hunger in those imploring eyes, frantic and despairing as those of a small boy who had watched a beloved hand slam a heavy door in his face.

“Why, he loves her!” thought the redheaded girl. “He loves her dreadfully!” Those few scattered seconds when laughter and hope and despair had swept across a court﻿—how long﻿—how long they seemed! And yet they would have scantily sufficed to turn a pretty phrase or a platitude on the weather. They had just barely served to give the portly Lambert time to recover his breath, his voice, and his venom, all three of which he was now proceeding to utilize simultaneously and vigorously.

“I see, I see. You’re particular about your jails﻿—like them in valleys, do you? Now be good enough to answer my question without any further trifling.”

“What question is that?”

“Have you ever been in jail?”

Mr. Orsini’s expression became faintly tinged with caution, but its affability did not diminish. “When?” he inquired impartially.

“When? Any time! Will﻿—you﻿—answer﻿—my﻿—question?”

Thus rudely adjured, his victim yielded to the inevitable with philosophy, humour, and grace. “Not any time﻿—no, no! That is too exaggerate’. But sometimes﻿—yes﻿—I do not deny that sometimes I have been in jail.”

===Chinese===

满庭再一次不顾法槌，屈服于无可奈何的哄堂大笑，而这一回他们被饶过了。只消看一眼兰伯特先生那张脸——一轮中风发作中的满月——连卡弗法官的铁石储备也告瓦解。法槌闲着，他躲在一方遮脸大手帕后痛痛快快咳了一阵。红头发姑娘正用一块小些的手帕擦眼睛，忽然顿住，又惊又疑。法庭那头，帕特里克和他的妻子苏珊正笑望着彼此的眼睛，有那奇迹般的一瞬，他们又成了旧日那对快活无间的伴侣；只那一瞬——随即，记忆猛地掠回她掠起的面纱，两人呆呆对坐，望着横在他们之间的那片废墟——他们本是惯于在笑声中寻访彼此的人。缓慢地、痛苦地，苏·艾夫斯把眼睛硬扳回训练有素的警觉；隔了长长的一口气，帕特·艾夫斯也把他那双忧魂不散的眼睛转回窗外，窗外天还蓝着。只在那一秒的等待里，红头发姑娘看见一层黑红掠过他的苍白，看见那双恳求的眼睛里的渴慕——狂乱而绝望，像一个小男孩眼睁睁看着一只亲爱的手在他脸上砰地关上沉重的门。

“哎呀，他爱她！”红头发姑娘想，“他爱她爱得要命！”笑声、希望与绝望扫过法庭的那零星几秒——多长——显得多长啊！可这点时间连雕琢一句俏皮话、一句关于天气的套话都嫌不够。它们勉强够让肥硕的兰伯特喘回这口气、这条嗓音、这份毒液——此刻这三样他正打算同时大力派上用场。

“我懂了，我懂了。您挑牢还挑三拣四——喜欢谷地里的，是吧？现在请好好回答我的问题，别再打岔。”

“什么问题？”

“您坐过牢没有？”

奥尔西尼先生的神情微微添了点谨慎，和蔼却没有减少。“什么时候？”他不偏不倚地反问。

“什么时候？任何时候！你——到底——回——不——回答——我的——问题？”

受到如此粗鲁的敦促，他的猎物以豁达、幽默和风度向必然低头。“任何时候——不，不！那太夸张了。可有些时候——是的——我不否认，有些时候我坐过牢。”

===Original===

Under the eyes of the entranced spectators, Mr. Lambert’s rosy jowls darkened to a fine, deep, full-bodied maroon. “You don’t deny it, hey? Well, that’s very magnanimous and gratifying﻿—very gratifying indeed. Now will you continue to gratify us by telling us just why you went to jail?”

Mr. Orsini dismissed his penal career with an eloquent shrug. “Ah, well, for what thing do you not go to jail in these days? If you do not have money to pay for fine, it is jail for you! You drink beer what is two and three quarter, you shake up some dice where you think nobody care, you drive nine and one-half mile over a bridge where it say eight and one half﻿—”

“That will do, Orsini. In 1911 did you or did you not serve eight months in jail for stealing some rings from a hotel room?”

“Ah, that﻿—that is one dirty lie﻿—one dirty plant is put on me! I get that﻿—”

From under the swarthy skin of the erstwhile suave citizen of the world there leaped, sallow with fury, livid with fear, the Calabrian peasant, ugly and vengeful, chattering with incoherent rage. Lambert eyed him with profound satisfaction.

“Yes, yes﻿—naturally. It always is. Very unfortunate; our jails are crowded with these errors. It’s true, too, isn’t it, Orsini, that less than three weeks before the murder you told Mr. Bellamy that the reason you hadn’t asked your little Milanese friend to marry you was that you couldn’t afford to buy her an engagement ring?”

“You﻿—you﻿—”

“Just one moment, Orsini.” The prosecutor’s low voice cut sharply across the thick, violent stammering. “Don’t answer that question.﻿ ﻿… Your Honour, I once more respectfully inquire as to whether this is the trial of Mr. Bellamy and Mrs. Ives or of my witnesses, individually and en masse?”

“And the Court has told you once before that it does not reply to purely rhetorical questions, Mr. Farr. You are perfectly aware as to whose trial this is, and while the Court is inclined to agree as to the impropriety of the last question, it does not believe that it is in error in stating that it is some time since you have seen fit to object to any of the questions put by Mr. Lambert to your witness.”

===Chinese===

在如痴如醉的观众眼前，兰伯特先生红润的腮帮子加深成一种漂亮、浓重、醇厚的褐红。“您不否认，嘿？好啊，真是宽宏大量，令人欣慰——确实非常欣慰。那您可愿继续让我们欣慰一下，说说您到底为什么坐牢？”

奥尔西尼先生耸了耸会说话的肩膀，把他服刑的履历一笔带过。“唉，如今这年月，什么事不叫人坐牢？交不起罚款，就得坐牢！你喝了度数二点七五的啤酒，在自以为没人在意的地方摇了摇骰子，你开车过桥走了九英里半，桥上写着八英里半——”

“够了，奥尔西尼。一九一一年，你有没有因为从旅馆房间偷了几枚戒指而坐了八个月牢？”

“啊，那个——那是个脏瞎话——是有人给我下的脏套！我那次——”

昔日那位八面玲珑的世界公民的黝黑皮肤底下，猛地跳出个卡拉布里亚庄稼汉，气得蜡黄，怕得铁青，丑陋、狠毒，语无伦次地哇哇乱骂。兰伯特心满意足地打量着他。

“是啊，是啊——当然。向来如此。很不幸；我们的牢房里挤满了这类冤案。还有一件事也是真的吧，奥尔西尼——谋杀案发生前不到三星期，你告诉贝拉米先生，你之所以没向你的米兰小情人求婚，是因为买不起订婚戒指？”

“你——你——”

“稍等，奥尔西尼。”检察官低沉的声音犀利地切断那浓浊狂乱的结巴。“不要回答这个问题。……庭上，我再一次恭敬地问一句：这里审的是贝拉米先生和艾夫斯太太，还是我的证人——逐个地审，还是一锅端地审？”

“本庭早告诉过你，它不回答纯修辞性的问题，法尔先生。你完全清楚审的是谁；虽然本庭倾向于同意，最后一个问题确有不当，但本庭不认为它说错了：兰伯特先生向你证人提的种种问题，你已有相当时间没觉得有异议的必要了。”

===Original===

“Your Honour is quite correct. It being my profound conviction that I have an absolutely unshakable case, I have studiously refrained from injecting the usual note of acrimonious bickering into these proceedings that is supposed to be the legal prerogative. This kind of thing causes me profoundly to regret my forbearance, I may state. About two out of three witnesses that I’ve put on the stand have been practically accused of committing or abetting this murder. Whether they’re all supposed to be in one gigantic conspiracy or to have played lone hands is still a trifle hazy, but there’s no doubt whatever about the implications. Miss Page, Miss Cordier, Mr. Farwell, Mr. Ives, Mr. Orsini﻿—it’ll be getting around to me in a minute.”

“I object to this, Your Honour, I object!” The choked and impassioned voice of Mr. Dudley Lambert went down before the clear, metallic clang of the prosecutor’s, roused at last from lethargy.

“And I object, too﻿—I object to a great many things! I object to the appalling gravity of a trial for murder being turned into a farce by the kind of thing that’s been going on here this morning. I’m entirely serious in saying that Mr. Lambert might just as well select me as a target for his insinuations. I used to live in Rosemont. I have a good sharp pocket knife﻿—my wife hasn’t a sapphire ring to her name﻿—I’ve been arrested three times﻿—twice for exceeding a speed limit of twenty-two miles an hour and once for trying to reason with a traffic cop who had delusions of grandeur and a﻿—”

“That will do, Mr. Farr.” There was a highly peremptory note in Judge Carver’s voice. “The Court has exercised possibly undue liberality in permitting you to extend your observations on this point, because it seemed well taken. It does not believe that you will gain anything by further elaboration. Mr. Lambert your last question is overruled. Have you any further ones to put to the witness?”

Mr. Lambert, looking a striking combination of a cross baby and a bulldog, did not take these observations kindly. “Am I denied the opportunity of attacking the credibility of the extraordinary collection of individuals that Mr. Farr chooses to produce as witnesses?”

“You are not. In what way does your inquiry as to Mr. Orsini’s inability to provide a young woman with an engagement ring purport to attack his credibility?”

===Chinese===

“庭上所言极是。由于我深信自己握有一桩绝对动摇不了的案子，我便刻意没有往这场审理里注入那种通常被视为律师特权的刻薄斗嘴。这种事让我深深后悔自己的忍让，我可以这么说。我请上证人席的证人，三个里约有两个实际被指控犯下或协从了这桩谋杀。他们究竟是被认为结成了一个庞大的阴谋集团，还是各自单干，眼下还略有些模糊，但那些含沙射影是明明白白的。佩奇小姐、科迪埃小姐、法威尔先生、艾夫斯先生、奥尔西尼先生——转眼就要轮到我了。”

“对此我抗议，庭上，我抗议！”达德利·兰伯特先生那哽咽激动的声音，被检察官终于从怠惰中惊醒的、清亮如金属的锤锵压了下去。

“我也抗议——我抗议的事情多了！我抗议一桩人命关天的谋杀审判，被今天上午这里的这套把戏闹成闹剧。我完全不是开玩笑：兰伯特先生大可把我也选作他影射的靶子。我从前住在罗斯蒙特。我有一把锋利好使的小折刀——我妻子名下一枚蓝宝石戒指也没有——我被捕过三次——两次是因为超速，超过了时速二十二英里；还有一次是想跟一位满脑妄自尊大幻想的交通警察讲道理，他还带着一把——”

“够了，法尔先生。”卡弗法官的声音里透着高度专断的调子。“本庭允许你就此点继续发挥，或许已属过分宽纵，只因它似乎言之成理。本庭不认为你再发挥下去会于事有补。兰伯特先生，你最后那个问题被驳回。还有别的问题要问这位证人吗？”

兰伯特先生——那模样活像一个闹脾气的娃娃跟一条斗牛犬的惊人合体——对这番话可不领情。“难道我连质疑法尔先生挑来当证人的这批非凡人物的可信度的机会都被剥夺了吗？”

“没有剥夺。那么，你问奥尔西尼先生买不起订婚戒指这件事，又是如何意图攻击他的可信度的？”

===Original===

“It purports to show that Orsini had a distinct motive for robbery and﻿—”

“Precisely. And precisely for that reason, since Mr. Orsini is not on trial here, the Court considers the question irrelevant and incompetent, as well as improper. Have you any further ones to put?”

“No.” The rage that was consuming the unchastened Mr. Lambert choked his utterance and bulged his eyes. “No further questions. May I have an exception from Your Honour’s ruling?”

“Certainly.”

Orsini, stepping briskly down from the witness box, lingered long enough to bestow on his late inquisitor a glance in which knives flashed and blood flowed freely﻿—a glance which Mr. Lambert, goaded by frustrated rage, returned with interest. The violence remained purely ocular, however, and the obviously disappointed spectators began to crawl laboriously to their feet.

“Call for Turner.”

“Joseph Turner!”

A bright-eyed, brown-faced, friendly-looking boy swung alertly into the box and fired a pair of earnest young eyes on the prosecutor.

“What was your occupation on June nineteenth of this year, Mr. Turner?”

“I was bus driver over the Perrytown route.”

“Still are?”

“No, sir; driving for the same outfit, but over a new route﻿—Redfield to Glenvale.”

“Ever see these before, Turner?”

The prosecutor lifted a black chiffon cape and lace scarf from the pasteboard box beside him and extended them casually toward the witness.

The boy eyed them soberly. “Yes, sir.”

“When?”

“Two or three times, sir; the last time was the night of the nineteenth of June.”

“At what time?”

“At about eight-thirty-five.”

“Where did you pick Mrs. Bellamy up?”

“At about a quarter of a mile beyond her house, toward the club. There’s a bus stop there, and she stepped out from some deep shadows at the side of the road and signalled me to stop.”

“Did you know Mrs. Bellamy by name at that time?”

“No, sir; I found out later. That’s when I learned where her house was too.”

“Was yours the first bus that she could have caught?”

“If she missed the eight o’clock bus. Mine was the next.”

“Did anything particularly draw your attention to her?”

===Chinese===

“这是为了表明奥尔西尼有明确的抢劫动机，而且——”

“正是。也正因为如此，既然此地受审的不是奥尔西尼先生，本庭认为该问题不相关、不合格，亦不正当。还有别的问题吗？”

“没有了。”肆虐的怒火烧得这位不思悔改的兰伯特先生话都说不清，眼睛都鼓了出来。“没有其他问题了。可否请庭上准许记录异议？”

“当然可以。”

奥尔西尼轻快地走下证人席，临去前特意赏了他那位刚下场的审讯者一瞥——那一瞥里刀光闪烁、血流成河；兰伯特先生被挫败的怒火刺激着，连本带利地回敬了一瞥。不过暴力仅止于目光，显然大失所望的观众们这才吃力地爬起身来。

“传特纳。”

“约瑟夫·特纳！”

一个眼睛明亮、棕色脸膛、模样和气的少年敏捷地跨进证人席，把一双诚挚的年轻眼睛对准检察官。

“特纳先生，今年六月十九日你从事什么职业？”

“我是佩里敦线路的公交车司机。”

“现在还是？”

“不是了，先生；还在同一家公司，但换了一条新线——雷德菲尔德到格伦维尔。”

“以前见过这两样吗，特纳？”

检察官从身旁的纸板箱里提起一件黑色雪纺披肩和一条蕾丝纱巾，随手递向证人。

少年神情严肃地看了看。“见过，先生。”

“什么时候？”

“两三次吧，先生；最后一次是六月十九日夜里。”

“几点？”

“八点三十五分左右。”

“你在哪儿让贝拉米太太上的车？”

“她家过去约四分之一英里，朝俱乐部方向。那儿有个公交站，她从路边很深的阴影里跨出来，招呼我停车。”

“你当时知道贝拉米太太的名字吗？”

“不知道，先生；后来才知道的。也是那时候我才知道她家在哪儿。”

“她能赶上的头一班就是你这班吗？”

“如果她误了八点那班的话。我的是下一班。”

“有什么特别引起你对她注意的吗？”

===Original===

“Yes, sir. She had her face all muffled up in her veil, the way she always did, but I specially noticed her slippers. They were awfully pretty shiny silver slippers, and when I let her out at the corner before Orchards it was sort of muddy, and I thought they sure were foolish little things to walk in, but that it was a terrible pity to spoil ’em like that.”

“How long did it take you to cover the distance between the point from which you picked Mrs. Bellamy up to the point at which you set her down?”

“About eight minutes, I should say. It’s a little over two miles﻿—nearer two and a half, I guess.”

“Did she seem in a hurry?”

“Yes, sir, she surely did; when she got out at the Orchards corner she started off almost at a run. I pretty nearly called to her to look out or she’d trip herself, but then I decided that it wasn’t none of my business, and of course it wasn’t.”

“How do you fix the date and the time, Turner?”

“Well, that’s easy. It was my last trip that night to Perrytown, see? And about the date, next morning I saw how there had been the﻿—a﻿—well, a murder at Orchards, and I remembered her and those silver slippers, and that black cloak, so I dropped in at headquarters to tell ’em what I knew﻿—and it was her all right. They made me go over and look at her, and I won’t forget that in a hurry, either﻿—no, sir.”

The boy who had driven her to Orchards set his lips hard, turning his eyes resolutely from the little black cloak. “I got ’em to change my route the next day,” he said, his pleasant young voice suddenly shaken.

“You say that you had driven her over several times before?”

“Well, two or three times, I guess﻿—all in that last month too. I only had the route a month.”

“Same time﻿—half-past eight?”

“That’s right﻿—eight-thirty.”

“Anything in particular call your attention to her?”

“Well, I should think she’d have called anyone’s attention to her,” said Joe Turner gently. “Even all wrapped up like that, she was prettier than anything I ever saw in my whole life.” And he added, more gently still: “About twenty times prettier.”

The prosecutor stood silent for a moment, letting the hushed voice evoke once more that radiant image, lace-scarfed, silver-slippered, slipping off into the shadows. “That will be all,” he said. “Cross-examine.”

“No questions.” Even Lambert’s voice boomed less roundly.

“Next witness﻿—Sergeant Johnson.”

===Chinese===

“有的，先生。她照她一贯的做派，把脸整个蒙在面纱里，可我特别留意了她的鞋。那是一双银光闪亮、漂亮极了的银色便鞋；我在奥查兹前的拐角让她下车时，地上有点泥泞，我心想穿着这么走路真是对傻乎乎的小玩意儿，可又觉得把它们糟蹋成那样实在可惜。”

“从你让贝拉米太太上车的地方到让她下车的地方，开了多长时间？”

“大概八分钟吧。两英里出头——我琢磨着快到两英里半。”

“她显得匆忙吗？”

“是的，先生，确实匆忙；她在奥查兹拐角下车后，几乎是一路小跑走掉的。我差一点喊她当心别绊着，后来一想这不关我的事，当然也确实不关。”

“你怎么确定日期和时间的，特纳？”

“嗯，这容易。那是我当晚去佩里敦的末班车，明白吗？至于日子，第二天早晨我得知奥查兹出了——呃——出了桩谋杀案，我想起了她和那双银便鞋、那件黑斗篷，就顺路去总部把我知道的说了——果然是她。他们让我过去认了认她，那个我也一时半会儿忘不了——忘不了，先生。”

这个曾载她去奥查兹的少年抿紧了嘴唇，毅然把眼睛从那件小小的黑斗篷上移开。“第二天我就让他们给我调了线路。”他说，那副讨人喜欢的年轻嗓音突然发颤。

“你说你以前载过她好几次？”

“嗯，两三次吧，我琢磨——全在那最后一个月里。我跑这条线才一个月。”

“都是同一时间——八点半？”

“没错——八点半。”

“有什么特别让你注意她的吗？”

“嗯，我看她想让谁不注意都难。”乔·特纳轻声说。“哪怕裹成那样，她也是我这辈子见过的最美的人。”他又补了一句，声音更轻了：“美上二十倍。”

检察官静立了片刻，让这轻悄的嗓音再一次唤起那个光彩照人的身影——蕾丝纱巾、银色便鞋，悄悄没入阴影。“没有问题了。”他说。“盘问。”

“没有问题。”连兰伯特的声音都嗡得不那么圆了。

“下一位证人——约翰逊中士。”

===Original===

“Sergeant Hendrick Johnson!”

Obedient to Ben Pott’s lyric summons, a young gentleman who looked like a Norse god inappropriately clothed in gray whipcord and a Sam Browne belt strode promptly down the aisle and into the witness box.

“Sergeant Johnson, what was your occupation on the nineteenth of June, 1926?”

“State trooper﻿—sergeant.”

“When did you first receive notification of the murder at Orchards?”

“At a little before ten on the morning of the twentieth of June. I’d just dropped in at headquarters when Mr. Conroy came in to report what he’d discovered at the cottage.”

“Please tell us what happened then.”

“I was detailed to accompany Mr. Dutton, the coroner, Dr. Stanley and another trooper, Dan Wilkins, to the cottage. Mr. Dutton took Dr. Stanley along with him in his roadster, and Wilkins rode with me in my side car. We left headquarters at a little after ten and got to the cottage about quarter past.”

“Just one moment. Do I understand that the state troopers have headquarters in Rosemont?”

“That’s correct, sir.”

“Of which you are in charge?”

“That’s correct too.”

“Who had the key to the cottage?”

“I had it; Mr. Conroy had turned it over to me. I unlocked the door of the cottage myself, and we all went in together.” The crisp, assured young voice implied that a murder more or less was all in the day’s work to the state police.

“Did you drive directly up to the cottage door?”

“No; we left the motorcycle and the car just short of the spot where the little dirt road to the cottage hits the gravel road to the main house and went in on foot, using the grass strip that edges the road.”

“Any special reason for that?”

“There certainly was. We didn’t want to mix up footprints and other marks any more than they’d been mixed already.”

“What happened after you got in the house?”

===Chinese===

“亨德里克·约翰逊中士！”

应着本·波茨咏叹调般的传唤，一位年轻绅士大步流星走过过道，跨进证人席——他活像一位错穿了灰斜纹布制服和山姆·布朗武装带的北欧之神。

“约翰逊中士，一九二六年六月十九日你担任什么职务？”

“州警——中士。”

“你最早是何时接到奥查兹谋杀案通知的？”

“六月二十日上午十点差一点。我刚进总部，康罗伊先生就进来报告他在小屋的发现。”

“请告诉我们随后发生了什么。”

“我奉命陪同验尸官达顿先生、斯坦利医生和另一名州警丹·威尔金斯前往小屋。达顿先生让斯坦利医生坐他的跑车，威尔金斯坐我的边车。我们十点过一点离开总部，一刻钟左右到达小屋。”

“稍等。我没听错吧——州警的总部设在罗斯蒙特？”

“没错，先生。”

“由你负责？”

“也没错。”

“小屋的钥匙在谁手里？”

“在我这儿；康罗伊先生交给我的。我亲手开了小屋的门，我们一起进去的。”那干脆、笃定的年轻嗓音暗示：多一桩少一桩谋杀，对州警来说不过是家常便饭。

“你们是直接把车开到小屋门口的吗？”

“不是；我们把摩托车和汽车停在通小屋的土路与通宅子的碎石路交会处之前，下车步行，走的是路边的草带。”

“有什么特别原因吗？”

“当然有。我们不想让脚印和其他痕迹再乱上加乱——它们已经被搅得够乱了。”

“进屋之后发生了什么？”

===Original===

“Well, Mr. Dutton and the doctor took charge of the body, and we helped them to move it into the dining room across the hall, after a careful inspection had been made of the position of the body. As a matter of fact, a chalk outline was made of it for further analysis, if necessary, and I took a flash light or so of it so that we’d have that, too, to check up with later. I helped to carry the body to the other room and place it on the table, where it was decided to keep it until the autopsy could be performed. I then locked the door of the parlour so that nothing could be disturbed there, put the key in my pocket, and went out to inspect the marks in the dirt road. I left Mr. Dutton and Dr. Stanley with the body and sent Wilkins down the road to a gas station to telephone Mr. Bellamy that his wife had been found in the cottage. There was no telephone in the cottage, and the one at the main house had been disconnected.”

“Sergeant, was Mr. Bellamy under suspicion at the time that you telephoned him?”

“I didn’t do the telephoning,” corrected Sergeant Johnson dispassionately; and added more dispassionately still; “Everyone was under suspicion.”

“Mr. Bellamy no more than another?”

“What I said was,” remarked the sergeant with professional reticence, “that everyone was under suspicion.”

Mr. Farr met the imperturbable blue eye of his witness with an expression in which irritation and discretion were struggling for supremacy. Discretion triumphed. “Did you discover any tracks on the cottage road?”

“I surely did.”

“Footprints?”

“No; there were some prints, but they were too cut up and blurred to make much out of. What I found were tire tracks.”

“More than one set?”

“There were traces of at least four sets, two of them made by the same car.”

“All equally distinct?”

“No, they varied considerably. The ground in the cottage road is of a distinctly clayey character, which under the proper conditions would act almost as a cast.”

“What would be a proper condition?”

“A damp state following a rainstorm, followed in turn by sufficient fair weather to permit the impression to dry out.”

“Was such a state in existence?”

===Chinese===

“是这样：达顿先生和医生负责尸体。在对尸体位置仔细查验之后，我们帮他们把尸体移到门厅对面的餐厅。事实上，我们还用粉笔勾了尸体的轮廓，以备日后必要时分析，又拍了一两张闪光照片，好日后核对。我帮着把尸体抬进另一间屋，放上桌子，决定在那儿停放，等验尸。然后我锁上客厅的门，免得里面的东西被扰动，把钥匙揣进口袋，出去查看土路上的痕迹。我留下达顿先生和斯坦利医生守着尸体，派威尔金斯顺路去加油站打电话通知贝拉米先生，说他的妻子在小屋里被找到了。小屋里没有电话，宅子那部的线也掐了。”

“中士，你们打电话时，贝拉米先生是否已受怀疑？”

“打电话的不是我。”约翰逊中士不动声色地纠正；接着更不动声色地补充：“人人都受怀疑。”

“贝拉米先生不比别人更多？”

“我说的是，”中士以职业性的缄默说道，“人人都受怀疑。”

法尔先生迎着证人那只波澜不惊的蓝眼睛，脸上的神情里恼怒与审慎正争夺上风。审慎获胜了。“你在小屋的路上发现车辙了吗？”

“当然发现了。”

“脚印呢？”

“没有；印子倒是有一些，但被践踏得模糊不清，辨不出什么名堂。我找到的是轮胎印。”

“不止一组？”

“至少四组，其中两组是同一辆车留下的。”

“都同样清晰？”

“不，差别很大。小屋那条路的土质黏性很重，条件合适时几乎能像模子一样把印子浇铸下来。”

“什么算合适的条件？”

“一场大雨后的湿态，再跟上足够的晴天，让印子干透。”

“当时正是这种状态吗？”

===Original===

“In one case﻿—yes. There was a storm between one and three on the afternoon of the nineteenth. We’ll call the tire impressions A, B 1 and 2, and C. A showed only very vague traces of a very broad, massive tire on a heavy car. It was almost obliterated, showing that it must have been there either before or during the downpour.”

“Would those tracks have corresponded to the ones on Mr. Farwell’s car?”

“There were absolutely no distinguishing tire marks left; it could have been Mr. Farwell’s or any other large car. C had come much later, when the ground had had time to dry out considerably. They were the traces of a medium-sized tire on fairly dry ground. They cut across the tracks left by both A and B.”

“Could they have been made by Mr. Conroy’s car?”

“I think that very likely they were. I checked up as well as possible under the conditions, and they corresponded all right.”

“What about the B impressions?”

“Both the B impressions were as sharp and distinct as though they had been made in wax. They were made by the same car; judging from the soil conditions, at an interval of an hour or so. We made a series of tests later to see how long it retained moisture.”

“Of what nature were these impressions, sergeant?”

“They were narrow tires, such as are used on the smaller, lighter cars,” said Sergeant Johnson, a slight tinge of gravity touching the curtness of his unemotional young voice. “Two of the tires﻿—the ones on the front right and rear left wheels had the tread so worn off that it would be risky to hazard a guess as to their manufacture. The ones on the front left and rear right were brand new, and the impressions in both cases were as clear cut as though you’d carved them. The impressions of B 2 were even deeper than B 1, showing that the car must have stood much longer at one time than at another. We experimented with that, too, but the results weren’t definite enough to report on positively.”

“What makes you so clear as to which were B 2?”

“At one spot B 2 was superimposed on B 1 very distinctly.”

“What were the makes of the rear right and left front tires, sergeant?”

“The rear right was a new Ajax tire; the front left was a practically new Silvertown cord.”

“Did they correspond with any of the cars mentioned so far in this case?”

===Chinese===

“有一组——是的。十九日下午一点到三点之间下过一场暴雨。我们把轮胎印称为A、B1、B2和C。A只剩下一辆重型车宽阔厚重的轮胎的模糊痕迹，几乎被抹平了，说明它必定是在暴雨之前或暴雨之中留下的。”

“那些辙印与法威尔先生车上的轮胎相符吗？”

“没有留下任何可供分辨的轮胎纹；可能是法威尔先生的，也可能是任何别的大车。C来得晚得多，那时地面已经干了许多。那是中型轮胎在相当干燥的地面上的痕迹。它斜切过A和B留下的辙印。”

“它们可能是康罗伊先生的车留下的吗？”

“我认为很可能就是。我在条件允许的范围内做了核对，是相符的。”

“那B组的印子呢？”

“B组两处印子都锋利清晰，如同蜡上压出来的一般。它们是同一辆车留下的；根据土质状况判断，前后间隔一小时上下。我们后来做了一系列试验，看那土的湿气能保持多久。”

“这些印子是什么性质的，中士？”

“是窄胎，小型轻便车用的那种。”约翰逊中士说，那不动感情的年轻嗓音的干脆里微微添了点凝重。“其中两条轮胎——右前轮和左后轮上的——胎面磨得太狠，猜它们的厂牌要担风险。左前轮和右后轮上的是崭新的，两处印痕都清楚得像刻出来的。B2的印子比B1还要深，说明那车有一回停的时间远比另一回长。这个我们也试验了，但结果不够明确，没法言之凿凿地上报。”

“你怎么分得清哪组是B2？”

“有一处，B2清清楚楚叠印在B1上。”

“右后轮和左前轮的轮胎是什么牌子，中士？”

“右后轮是一条新埃阿斯胎；左前轮是一条几乎全新的锡尔弗敦帘线胎。”

“它们与本案迄今提到的任何一辆车相符吗？”

===Original===

“They corresponded exactly with the tires on Mr. Stephen Bellamy’s car when we inspected it on the afternoon of June twentieth.”

“No possibility of error?”

“Not a chance,” said Sergeant Johnson, succinctly and gravely.

“Exactly. Had the car been washed at the time you inspected it, Sergeant?”

“No, sir, it had not.”

“Was there mud on the tires?”

“Yes, but as it was of much the same character as the mud in Mr. Bellamy’s own drive, we attached no particular importance to it.”

“Was there any grease on the car?”

“No, sir; we made a very thorough inspection. There was no trace of grease.”

“Did you find anything else of consequence on the premises, sergeant?”

“I picked up a kind of lunch box in the shrubbery outside, and in the dining room, on a chair in the corner, I found a black cape﻿—chiffon, I expect you call it﻿—a black lace scarf and a little black silk bag with a shiny clasp that looked like diamonds.”

“Did you keep a list of the contents of the bag?”

“I did.”

“Have you it with you?”

“I have.”

“Let’s hear it, please?”

“ ‘Contents of black purse found in dining room of Thorne Cottage, June 20, 1926,’ ” read Sergeant Johnson briskly, “ ‘One vanity case, pale green enamel; one lipstick, same; one small green linen handkerchief, marked Mimi; leather frame enclosing snapshot of man in tennis clothes, inscribed “For My Mimi from Steve”; sample of blue chiffon with daisies; gold pencil; two theatre-ticket stubs to Vanities, June eighth; three letters, written on white bond paper, signed Pat.’ ”

“That’s all?”

“That’s all.”

“Are these the articles found in the dining room, sergeant?”

Sergeant Johnson eyed the contents of the box placed before him somewhat cursorily. “Those are the ones.”

“Just check over the contents of the bag, will you? Nothing missing?”

“Not a thing.”

“I ask to have these marked for identification and offer them in evidence, Your Honour.”

“No objections,” said Mr. Lambert unexpectedly.

===Chinese===

“与我们六月二十日下午查验的斯蒂芬·贝拉米先生车上的轮胎完全相符。”

“绝无出错的可能？”

“一丝可能都没有。”约翰逊中士言简意赅、郑重地说。

“很好。你们查验时那车洗过吗，中士？”

“没洗，先生。”

“轮胎上有泥吗？”

“有，但那泥跟贝拉米先生自家车道上的泥性质相仿，我们没特别当回事。”

“车上有油污吗？”

“没有，先生；我们查得非常彻底。油污的痕迹一点也没有。”

“宅子里你还发现别的要紧东西了吗，中士？”

“我在外面灌木丛里捡到一只像是午餐盒的东西；在餐厅角落的一把椅子上，我发现一件黑披肩——你们大概管它叫雪纺的——一条黑蕾丝纱巾，还有一只小小的黑绸手袋，搭扣亮闪闪的，像是钻石。”

“手袋里的东西你开了清单吗？”

“开了。”

“带在身边吗？”

“带着。”

“请念一念吧。”

“‘六月二十日于索恩小屋餐厅发现之黑色手袋内物件清单，’”约翰逊中士利落地念道，“‘粉饼盒一只，浅绿珐琅质；唇膏一支，同色；绿色亚麻小手绢一方，绣有“米米”字样；皮相框一个，内装一帧着网球装男子之快照，题有“给我的米米，斯蒂夫”；缀雏菊之蓝雪纺布样一方；金铅笔一支；《浮华世界》戏票存根两张，六月八日；白色高级书写纸信三封，署名“帕特”。’”

“就这些？”

“就这些。”

“这些就是餐厅里发现的物件吗，中士？”

约翰逊中士把摆到他面前的箱中之物草草看了一眼。“就是这些。”

“核对一下手袋里的东西，劳驾。没缺什么？”

“一样不缺。”

“庭上，我请求将这些物件编号备查，并列为证物。”

“无异议。”兰伯特先生出人意料地说。

===Original===

Mr. Farr eyed him incredulously for a moment, as though he doubted the evidence of his ears. Then, rather thoughtfully, he produced another object from the inexhaustible maw of his desk and poised it carefully on the ledge under the sergeant’s nose. It was a box﻿—a nice, shiny tin box, painted a cheerful but decorous maroon﻿—the kind of a box that good little boys carry triumphantly to school, bursting with cookies and apples and peanut-butter sandwiches. It had a neat handle and a large, beautiful, early English initial painted on the top.

“Did you recognize this, sergeant?”

“Yes. It’s a lunch box that I picked up back of the shrubbery to the left of the Orchards cottage.”

“Had it anything in it?”

“It was about three-quarters empty. There was a ham sandwich and some salted nuts and dates in it, and a couple of doughnuts.”

“What should you say that the initial on the cover represented?”

“I shouldn’t say,” remarked the sergeant frankly. “It’s got too many curlicues and doodads. It might be a D, or it might be P, or then again, it mightn’t be either.”

“So far as you know, it hasn’t been identified as anyone’s property?”

“No, sir.”

“It might have been left there at some previous date?”

“Well, it might have been; but the food seemed pretty fresh, and there were some new twigs broken off, as though someone had pressed way back into the shrubbery.”

“I offer this box in evidence, Your Honour, not as of any evidential value, but merely to keep the record straight as to what was turned over by the police.”

“No objections,” said Mr. Lambert with that same surprising promptitude, his eyes following the shiny box somewhat hungrily.

“Very well, sergeant, that’s all. Cross-examine.”

“Did you examine the portion of the drive to the rear of the cottage, sergeant?” inquired Mr. Lambert with genial interest.

“Yes, sir.”

“Find any traces of tires?”

“No, sir.”

“No further questions,” intoned Mr. Lambert mellifluously.

Mr. Farr turned briskly to an unhappy-looking young man crouching apprehensively in a far corner. “Now, Mr. Oliver, I’m going to get you just to read these three letters into the record. I’m unable to do it myself, as I’ve been subjected to considerable eye strain recently.”

“Do I start with the one on top?” inquired the wretched youth, who looked as though he were about to die at any moment.

===Chinese===

法尔先生难以置信地看了他片刻，仿佛怀疑自己耳朵的证据。然后，他若有所思地从办公桌那张无底洞似的大嘴里又取出一件东西，小心翼翼地架在中士鼻子底下的搁板上。那是一只盒子——一只漂亮、锃亮的铁皮盒，漆着一种喜庆而不失庄重的褐红色——就是乖孩子们得意洋洋拎去上学的那种盒子，里面塞满曲奇、苹果和花生酱三明治。盒子有个精巧的提手，盖上漆着一个硕大、漂亮的古英文字体花体首字母。

“你认得这个吗，中士？”

“认得。这是我在奥查兹小屋左侧灌木丛后面捡到的一只午餐盒。”

“里面有东西吗？”

“大概空了四分之三。里头有一块火腿三明治，一些咸果仁和枣子，还有两只炸面圈。”

“依你看，盒盖上那个首字母是什么？”

“我说不上。”中士坦率地说。“花体太多，缀饰太繁。兴许是D，兴许是P，再兴许，哪个都不是。”

“据你所知，还没人认领过它？”

“没有，先生。”

“它会不会是先前什么时候落下的？”

“嗯，兴许是；可食物相当新鲜，还有些新折断的树枝，像是有人一直挤到了灌木丛深处。”

“庭上，我请求将此盒列为证物——并非因为它有任何证据价值，只是把警方移交的物件记录齐全。”

“无异议。”兰伯特先生以同样出人意料的爽快说道，眼睛颇有些饥渴地追着那只亮盒子。

“很好，中士，没有问题了。盘问。”

“小屋后面那段车道你查验过吗，中士？”兰伯特先生饶有兴致、和蔼可亲地问。

“查过，先生。”

“发现轮胎痕迹了吗？”

“没有，先生。”

“没有其他问题了。”兰伯特先生甜腻腻地吟诵道。

法尔先生敏捷地转向缩在远处角落里提心吊胆的一副愁苦相的年轻人。“好了，奥利弗先生，我想请你把这三封信宣读入档。我自己没法念，近来我的眼睛过度疲劳。”

“从最上面那封开始吗？”这位可怜的青年问，那模样仿佛随时都会断气。

===Original===

“Start with the first in order of date,” suggested Mr. Farr benevolently. “May twenty-first, I think it is. And just raise your voice a little so we’ll all be able to hear you.”

“Darling, darling,” roared Mr. Oliver unbelievably, and paused, staring about him wildly, flame coloured far beyond the roots of his russet hair. “May twenty-first,” he added in a suffocated whisper.

Darling, darling:

I waited there for you for over an hour. I couldn’t believe that you weren’t coming﻿—not after you’d promised. And when I got back and found that hateful, stiff little note﻿—Mimi, how could you? You didn’t mean it to say, “I don’t love you”? It didn’t say that, did it? It sounded so horribly as though that was what it was trying to say that I kept both hands over my ears all the time that I was reading it. I won’t believe it. You do﻿—you must. You’re the only thing that I’ve ever loved in all my life, Mimi; I swear it. You’re the only thing that I’ll ever love, as long as I live.

You say that you’re frightened; that there’s been talk﻿—oh, darling, what of it? “They say? What say they? Let them say!” They’re a lot of wise, sensible, good-for-nothing idiots, who haven’t anything better to do in the world than wag their heads and their tongues, or else they’re a pack of young fools, frantic with jealousy because they can’t be beautiful like Mimi or lucky like Pat. If their talk gets really dangerous or ugly we can shut them all up in ten seconds by telling them that we’re planning to shake the dust of Rosemont from our heels any minute, and live happy ever after in some “cleaner, greener land.”

Do you want me to tell them that I’ve asked you fifteen thousand and three times to burn all our bridges and marry me, Mimi? Or didn’t you hear me? You always look then as though you were listening to someone else﻿—someone with a louder voice than mine, saying “Wait﻿—not yet. Think again﻿—you’ll be sorry. Be careful﻿—be careful.” Don’t listen to that liar, Mimi﻿—listen to Pat, who loves you.

===Chinese===

“按日期先后，从第一封开始吧。”法尔先生和颜悦色地建议。“我想是五月二十一日。嗓门稍微提一提，好让大家都听得见。”

“亲亲，亲亲。”奥利弗先生雷鸣般吼出这句，令人难以置信，随即停住，狂乱地环顾四周，红晕直烧过他那赤褐色头发的发根。“五月二十一日。”他憋着嗓子耳语般补了一句。

亲亲，亲亲：

我在那儿等了你一个多钟头。我不肯相信你不来了——你可是答应过的。等我回到家，看到那张可恨的、干巴巴的小字条——米米，你怎么能？你不是要写“我不爱你”吧？它没这么写，对不对？可它听上去偏偏像是拼了命要这么说，吓得我读的时候两只手一直摀着耳朵。我不信。你爱我——你一定爱。你是我这辈子爱过的唯一，米米；我发誓。只要我还活着一天，你就是我唯一会爱的。

你说你害怕；说人们闲话纷纷——哦，亲爱的，那又怎样？“他们说？说什么？随他们说去！”他们不过是一伙精明世故、百无一用的蠢货，世上没有更好的事可做，只会摇脑袋嚼舌头；再不然就是一群年轻的糊涂虫，妒火烧心，只因他们美不过米米、幸运不过帕特。他们的闲话真要闹得凶、闹得难看了，我们一句话就能封住他们的嘴：就说我们随时打算掸掉罗斯蒙特的尘土，从此到某个“更清爽、更青翠的地方”快快活活过日子。

你要不要我告诉他们，我求过你一万五千零三次，求你破釜沉舟嫁给我，米米？还是说你没听见？你那时总像在听另一个人说话——一个嗓门比我大的人，说着“等等——还不到时候。再想想——你会后悔的。小心——小心。”别听那个骗子的话，米米——听帕特的，帕特爱你。

===Original===

Tomorrow night, about nine, I’ll have the car at the back road. I’ll manage to get away somehow, and you must too. Wear that frilly thing that I love﻿—you know, the green one﻿—and the slippers with butterflies on them, and nothing on your hair. The wickedest thing that you ever do is to wear a hat. No, I’m wrong, you can wear something on your hair, after all. On the two curls right behind your ears﻿—the littlest curls﻿—my curls﻿—you can wear two drops of that stuff that smells like lilacs in the rain. And I’ll put you﻿—and your curls﻿—and your slippers﻿—and your sweetness﻿—and your magic﻿—into my car and we’ll drive twenty miles away from those wagging tongues. And, Mimi, I’ll teach you how beautiful it is to be alive and young and in love, in a world that’s full of spring and stars and lilacs. Oh, Mimi, come quickly and let me teach you!

Pat.

The halting voice laboured to an all too brief silence. Even the back of Mr. Oliver’s neck was incandescent﻿—perhaps he would not have flamed so hotly if he had realized how few eyes in the courtroom were resting on him. For across the crowded little room, Sue Ives, all her gay serenity gone, was staring at the figure by the window with terrified and incredulous eyes, black with tears.

“Oh, Pat﻿—oh, Pat,” cried those drowning eyes, “what is this that you have done to us? Never loved anyone else? Never in all your life? What is this that you have done?”

And as though in answer to that despairing cry, the man by the window half rose, shaking his head in fierce entreaty.

“Don’t listen! Don’t listen!” implored his frantic eyes.﻿ ﻿…

“Now the next one, Mr. Oliver,” said Mr. Farr.

Rosemont, June 8th.

Mimi darling, darling, darling:

It’s after four o’clock and the birds in the vines outside the window are making the most awful row. I haven’t closed my eyes yet, and now I’m going to stop trying. What’s the use of sleeping, when here’s another day with Mimi in it? Dawn﻿—I always thought it was the worst word in the English language, and here I am on my knees waiting for it, and ranting about it like any fool﻿—like any happy, happy fool.

===Chinese===

明晚九点上下，我把车停在后路上。我总会想法子脱身，你也必须脱身。穿那件我爱的、带褶边的衣裳——你知道，绿色的那件——穿那双缀蝴蝶的便鞋，头发上什么都不要戴。你这辈子干过的最缺德的事就是戴帽子。不，我说错了，你头发上终究可以戴点什么。在你耳后那两绺卷发上——最小的那两绺——我的卷发——你可以点两滴那种香露，闻起来像雨中的丁香。然后我要把你——和你的卷发——和你的便鞋——和你的甜——和你的魔法——统统装进我的车，我们开出二十英里，离开那些摇个不停的舌头。还有，米米，我要教你懂得，活着、年轻、恋爱着，在一个满是春天、星星和丁香的世界里，是多么美。哦，米米，快来吧，让我教你！

帕特

那磕磕绊绊的声音艰难地读完了，归于一片过于短促的寂静。连奥利弗先生的后脖颈都红透了——倘若他明白法庭里几乎没几双眼睛落在他身上，他兴许还烧不了这么热。因为在满屋人群的那一头，苏·艾夫斯——她那身快活的安详荡然无存——正用惊恐而难以置信的眼睛，望着窗边那个人影，双眼含泪，黑沉沉的。

“哦，帕特——哦，帕特。”那双溺水的眼睛哭喊道，“你对我们做了什么呀？从来没有爱过别人？这辈子从来没有？你做的这是什么呀？”

而仿佛是在回答那声绝望的呼喊，窗边的男人半站起来，猛力摇头恳求。

“别听！别听！”他那双狂乱的眼睛哀求道。……

“下一封，奥利弗先生。”法尔先生说。

罗斯蒙特，六月八日

米米，亲爱的，亲爱的，亲爱的：

已经四点多了，窗外藤蔓里的鸟吵得天翻地覆。我一直没合眼，如今索性不打算合了。反正明天又是一个有米米的日子，睡觉还有什么用？黎明——我一向以为这是英语里最恶毒的词，可如今我却跪着等它，为它絮絮叨叨像个傻子——像个幸福的、幸福的傻子。

===Original===

I’m so happy that it simply isn’t decent. I keep telling myself that we’re mad﻿—that there’s black trouble ahead of us﻿—that I haven’t any right in the world to let you do this﻿—that I’m older and ought to be wiser. And when I get all through, the only thing I can remember is that I feel like a kid waking up on his birthday to find the sun and the moon and the stars and the world and a little red wagon sitting in a row at the foot of his bed. Because I have you, Mimi, and you’re the sun and the moon and the stars and the world﻿—and a little red wagon too, my beautiful love.

Well, here’s the sun himself, and no one in Rosemont to pay any attention to him but the milkman and me. “The sun in splendour”﻿—what comes after that, do you remember? Not that it makes any difference; the only thing that makes any difference is that what will come after that in just a few minutes will be a clock striking five﻿—and then six and then seven and it will be another day﻿—another miraculous, incredible day getting under way in a world that holds Mimi in it. Lucky day, lucky world, lucky, lucky me, Mimi, who will be your worshipper while this world lasts.

Good morning, Beautiful.

Your Pat.

The eyes of the Court swung avidly back to the slim figure in the space before them, but for once that bright head was bowed. Sue Ives was no longer looking at Mimi’s worshipper.

“And the next?” murmured Farr.

Rosemont, June 9th.

My little heart:

I went to bed the minute I got home, just as I promised, but it didn’t do much good. I did go to sleep for a bit, but it was only to dream that you were leaning over me again with your hair swinging down like two lovely clouds of fire and saying over and over in that small, blessed voice﻿—that voice that I’d strain to hear from under three feet of sod﻿—“It’s not a dream, love, it’s not a dream﻿—it’s Mimi, who’s yours and who’s sweeter than all the dreams you’ll dream between here and heaven. Wake up. Wake up! She’s waiting for you. How can you sleep?” And I couldn’t sleep; no, it’s no use. Mimi, how can I ever sleep again, now that I have you?

===Chinese===

我幸福得简直有伤风化。我一遍遍告诉自己：我们疯了——前头有漆黑的大祸——我全无权利让你这么做——我年长些，理应更明白些。可等这一套全想完，我记得住的只有一件事：我像个生日早晨醒来的孩子，发现太阳、月亮、星星、全世界，还有一辆小小的红马车，一字排开坐在他的床脚。因为我有了你，米米，你就是太阳、月亮、星星和全世界——还是一辆小红马车，我美丽的爱人。

瞧，太阳他老人家露脸了，整个罗斯蒙特只有送奶工和我肯赏光看他。“光芒万丈的太阳”——接下来是哪句来着，你可记得？记不记得也无妨；要紧的只有一件事：几分钟之后接续上来的，将是一座钟敲响五点——然后六点、七点，于是又是新的一天——又一个奇迹般、不可思议的日子，在一个装着米米的世界里启程。幸运的日子，幸运的世界，幸运的、幸运的我，米米——只要这个世界还在，我就做你的膜拜者。

早安，美人。

你的帕特

满庭的目光贪婪地荡回他们面前那道窈窕的身影上，可这一回，那颗明亮的头颅低垂着。苏·艾夫斯不再看米米的膜拜者了。

“下一封？”法尔低声问。

罗斯蒙特，六月九日

我的小心肝：

我一到家就上了床，说到做到，可没什么用。我确实睡着了一小会儿，却只是梦见你又俯在我身上，你的头发垂下来，像两朵可爱的火云，用那个小小的、蒙福的嗓音——那嗓门，哪怕我埋在三尺土下也要侧耳去听——一遍遍地说：“这不是梦，亲爱的，这不是梦——是米米，是你的米米，比从这儿到天堂之间你将做的所有梦都甜。醒醒。醒醒呀！她在等你呢。你怎么还睡得着？”于是我就睡不着了；是啊，睡不着了。米米，如今我有了你，往后可怎么睡得着觉？

===Original===

It wasn’t just a dream that between those shining clouds that are your hair your eyes were bright with laughter and with tears, was it, Mimi? No, that was not a dream. To think that anyone in the world can cry and still be beautiful! It must be an awful temptation to do it all the time﻿—only I know that you won’t. Darling, don’t cry. Even when you look beautiful and on the edge of laughter, it makes me want to kill myself. It’s because you’re afraid, isn’t it﻿—afraid that we won’t be able to make a go of it? Don’t be afraid. If you will come to me﻿—really, forever, not in little snatched bits of heaven like this, but to belong to me all the days of my life﻿—if you will believe in me and trust me, I swear that I’ll make you happy. I swear it.

I know that at first it may be hideously hard. I know that giving up everything here and starting life all over somewhere with strangers will be hard to desperation. But it will be easier than trying to fight it out here, won’t it, Mimi? And in the end we’ll hold happiness in our hands﻿—you’ll see, my blessed. Don’t cry, don’t cry, my little girl﻿—not even in dreams, not even through laughter. Because, you see, like the Prince and Princess in the fairy tale, we’re going to live happy ever after.

Your Pat.

“That concludes the letters?” inquired Judge Carver, hopefully, his eyes on the bowed head beneath his throne.

“That concludes them,” said Mr. Farr, removing them deftly from the assistant prosecutor’s palsied fingers. “And as it is close to four, I would like to make a suggestion. The state is ready to rest its case with these letters, but an extremely unfortunate occurrence has deprived us so far of one of our witnesses, who is essential as a link in the chain of evidence that we have forged. This witness was stricken three weeks ago with appendicitis and rushed to a New York hospital. I was given every assurance that he would be able to be present by this date, but late last week unfavourable symptoms developed and he has been closely confined ever since.

“I have here the surgeon’s certificate that he is absolutely unable to take the stand today, but that it is entirely possible that he may do so by Monday. As this is Friday, therefore, I respectfully suggest that we adjourn to Monday, when the state will rest its case.”

“Have you any objections, Mr. Lambert?”

===Chinese===

那不只是梦吧——你那两朵火云般的头发之间，你的眼睛亮着笑意和泪水，是真的吧，米米？是的，那不是梦。想想看，这世上竟有人能一边哭一边还那么美！老是这么哭一定让人忍不住——可我知道你不会。亲爱的，别哭。哪怕你美美地站在笑的边缘上哭，也让我想杀死我自己。那是因为你害怕，对不对——怕我们成不了事？别怕。只要你肯来我这里——真正地、永远地，不是像这样一小片一小片偷来的天堂，而是在我余生的每一天都属于我——只要你肯信我、信得过我，我发誓我会让你幸福。我发誓。

我知道，起初可能苦得吓人。我知道，抛开这里的一切，到陌生的地方面对陌生的人从头来过，会难到绝望。可这总比在这儿硬撑下去容易些，对不对，米米？到头来我们会把幸福握在手里——你等着瞧，我的宝贝。别哭，别哭，我的小姑娘——连梦里也别哭，连笑声里也别哭。因为你要知道，就像童话里的王子和公主，我们从此要幸福地生活，直到永远。

你的帕特

“信到此就读完了？”卡弗法官满怀希望地询问，眼睛望着御座下那颗低垂的头。

“读完了。”法尔先生说着，灵巧地从副检察官瘫痪的手指间抽走信件。“现在快四点了，我想提个建议。控方本可在这三封信后结束举证，但一桩极其不幸的变故，使我们至今缺了一位证人——他是我们锻造的证据链上不可缺少的一环。这位证人三周前突发阑尾炎，被紧急送往纽约一家医院。各方都向我担保他能在此日期前出庭，但上星期末出现了不良症状，此后一直卧床静养。

“我这里有主治外科医生的证明，写明他今日绝无可能出庭，但到星期一完全有此可能。因此，今日既是星期五，我恭敬建议休庭至星期一，届时控方将结束举证。”

“你有异议吗，兰伯特先生？”

===Original===

“Every objection, Your Honour!” replied Mr. Lambert with passionate conviction. “I have two witnesses myself who have come here at great inconvenience to themselves and are obliged to return at the earliest possible moment. What about them? What about the unfortunate jury? What about the unfortunate defendants? I have most emphatic objections to delaying this trial one second longer.”

“Then I can only suggest that the trial proceed and that the state be permitted to produce its witness as soon as is humanly possible, in which case the defense would necessarily be permitted to produce what witnesses it saw fit in rebuttal.”

Mr. Lambert, still flown with some secret triumph, made an ample gesture of condescension.

“Very well, I consider it highly irregular, but leave it that way﻿—leave it that way by all means. Now, Your Honour﻿—”

“You say you have a certificate, Mr. Farr?”

“Yes, Your Honour.”

“May we have its contents?”

“Certainly.” Mr. Farr tendered it promptly. “It’s from the chief surgeon at St. Luke’s. As you see, it simply says that it would be against his express orders that Dr. Barretti should take the stand today, but that, if nothing unfavourable develops, he should be able to do so by Monday.”

“Yes. Well, Mr. Farr, if Mr. Lambert has no objections you may produce Dr. Barretti then. You have no further questions?”

“None, Your Honour.”

“Very well, the Court stands adjourned until tomorrow at ten.”

“What name did he say?” inquired the reporter in a curiously hushed voice. “Dr. What?”

“It sounded like Barretti,” said the redheaded girl, getting limply to her feet.

“The poor fool!” murmured the reporter in the same awestricken tones.

“What?”

“Lambert. Did you get that? The poor blithering fool doesn’t know who he is and where he’s heading.”

“Well, who is he?” inquired the redheaded girl over her shoulder despairingly. She felt that if anything else happened she would sit on the floor and cry, and she didn’t want to﻿—much.

“It’s Barretti﻿—Gabriel Barretti,” said the reporter. “The greatest fingerprint expert in the world. Lord, it means that he must have their﻿—What in the world’s the matter? D’you want a handkerchief?”

The redheaded girl, nodding feebly, clutched at the large white handkerchief with one hand and the large blue serge sleeve with the other. Anyway, she hadn’t sat on the floor.

===Chinese===

“异议大得很，庭上！”兰伯特先生满怀热烈的信念答道。“我自己也有两位证人，他们百忙之中赶来，还必须尽早返回。他们怎么办？不幸的陪审团怎么办？不幸的被告怎么办？我对这桩审判多拖一秒钟，都抱有最强烈的异议。”

“那我只能建议审判继续进行，准许控方在其证人可以出庭的第一时间传他到场；那样的话，辩方自然也将获准在反证时传召其认为合适的证人。”

兰伯特先生仍沉浸在某桩秘密胜利的得意里，宽宏地做了个挥洒的手势。

“很好，我认为这极不合规，但就这么办吧——务必就这么办。那么，庭上——”

“你说你有一份证明，法尔先生？”

“是的，庭上。”

“可否让大家听听内容？”

“当然。”法尔先生迅即递上。“是圣路加医院外科主任开的。如您所见，只是说：巴雷蒂医生今日出庭将违背他的明确医嘱；但若无不良变化，他到星期一应能出庭。”

“好。那么，法尔先生，既然兰伯特先生没有异议，届时你可传巴雷蒂医生出庭。你没有其他问题了？”

“没有了，庭上。”

“很好，本庭休庭，明晨十点继续。”

“他刚才说哪个名字？”记者用一种出奇低哑的声音问。“什么医生？”

“听着像巴雷蒂。”红头发姑娘说着，有气无力地站起身。

“这可怜的傻瓜！”记者以同样敬畏的声调咕哝。

“什么？”

“兰伯特。你听明白了吗？这可怜的胡涂虫还不知道他是谁、他要往哪儿走呢。”

“那他是谁？”红头发姑娘绝望地回头问。她觉得再出什么事她就要坐到地板上大哭了——而她并不想——不太想。

“是巴雷蒂——加布里埃尔·巴雷蒂。”记者说。“全世界最伟大的指纹专家。老天，这意味着他手上必定有他们的——你怎么了这是？要手帕吗？”

红头发姑娘虚弱地点着头，一手抓住那方大白手帕，一手抓住那条藏青哔叽大衣袖子。不管怎么说，她总算没坐到地板上。

===Original===

The fourth day of the Bellamy trial was over.

===Chinese===

贝拉米案审判的第四天结束了。

## V / 五
===Original===

“He couldn’t look so cocky and triumphant and absolutely sure of himself as that if he didn’t actually know that everything was all right,” explained the red-haired girl in a reasonable but tremulous whisper, keeping an eye in desperate need of reassurance on the portly and flamboyant Lambert, who was prowling up and down in front of the jury with an expression of lightly won victory on his rubicund countenance and a tie that boasted actual checks under a ruddy chin. Every now and then he uttered small, premonitory booms.

“He could look just exactly like that if he were a Godforsaken fool,” murmured the reporter gloomily. “And would, and undoubtedly does. Whom the gods destroy they first make mad. Look out, there he goes!”

“Your Honour,” intoned Mr. Lambert with unction, “gentlemen of the jury, I am not going to burden you with a lengthy dissertion at this moment. In my summing up at a later time I will attempt to analyze the fallacious and specious reasoning on which my brilliant opponent has constructed his case, but at present something else is in my mind; or perhaps I should be both more candid and more accurate if I say that something else is in my heart.

“We have heard a great deal of the beauty, the charm, the enchantment, and the tragedy of the young woman whose dreadful death has brought about this trial. Much stress has been laid on her appalling fate and on the pitiful horror of so much loveliness crushed out in such a fashion. It is very far from my desire to deny or to belittle any of this. Tragic and dreadful, indeed, was the fate of Madeleine Bellamy; not one of us can think of it unmoved.

“But, gentlemen, when its horror grips you most relentlessly, I ask you to think of another young woman whose fate, to my mind, has been bitterer still; who, many times in these past few days, would have been glad to change places with that dead girl, safe and quiet now, beyond the reach of the slings and arrows of outrageous fortune that have been raining about her own unprotected head. I ask you to turn your thoughts for one moment to the fate of Susan Ives, the prisoner at the bar.

===Chinese===

“他要是不是真知道一切都没事，绝不可能看上去那么趾高气扬、那么稳操胜券、那么有把握，”红头发姑娘压着嗓子解释，声音在理却发颤，一双急盼宽慰的眼睛盯住那肥硕张扬的兰伯特——他正在陪审团前来回踱步，红彤彤的脸上挂着轻松到手的胜利表情，红润下颏下系着一条货真价实的格纹领带，不时发出预示性的低沉嗡声。

“他要是被神抛弃的傻瓜，就正是那副长相，”记者阴郁地低语。“而且多半是，无疑就是。上帝欲灭之，必先迷其心。留神，他开始了！”

“法官阁下，”兰伯特先生抑扬顿挫、煞有介事地说，“陪审团的诸位先生，此刻我不打算用一篇冗长的宏论来劳烦诸位。日后总结陈词时，我会尝试剖析我那位才华横溢的对手构建其案情所依据的那套虚妄而似是而非的推理；但眼下，我心里装着别的事；或者说，若我说心里装着别的东西，或许既更坦白也更确切。

“关于这位年轻女子的美貌、魅力、魔力和悲剧——她的惨死酿成了这场审判——我们已经听得很多了。人们反复强调她那骇人的命运，强调那么多可爱竟以这种方式被碾灭的凄惨恐怖。我绝无半意否认或贬损这一切。玛德琳·贝拉米的命运，确实悲惨骇人；我们中没有谁能无动于衷地想到它。

“可是诸位先生，当这份恐怖最无情地攫住你们时，我请求你们想一想另一位年轻女子，在我看来，她的命运更为苦涩；过去这几天里，她多次巴不得同那个死去的姑娘换个位置——那姑娘如今安稳清静，够不着那些泼洒在她自己这颗无遮无护的头颅四周的命运明枪暗箭。我请诸位把思绪转向片刻：苏珊·艾夫斯的命运，这位站在被告席上的囚徒的命运。”

===Original===

“Not so many weeks ago there is not one of you who would not have thought her an object of profound envy. Sue Ives, the adored, the cherished, the protected; Sue Ives, moving safe and happy through a world of flowers and blue skies that held no single cloud; Sue Ives, the lucky and beloved, the darling of the gods. There she sits before you, gentlemen, betrayed by her husband, befouled by every idle tongue that wags, torn from her children and her home, pilloried in every journal in the land from the most lofty and impeccable sheet to the vilest rag in Christendom, branded before the world as that darkest, most dreadful and most abject of creatures﻿—a murderess.

“A murderess! This girl, so loyal and generous and honest that those who knew her believed her to be of somewhat finer clay than the rest of this workaday world; so proud, so sensitive and so fastidious that those who loved her would rather a thousand times have seen her dead in her grave than subjected to the ugly torture that has been her lot these past few days. What of her lot, gentlemen? What of her fate? What has brought her to this dreadful pass? Lightness or disloyalty or bad repute or reckless indiscretion or evil intent? Your own wife, your own daughter, your own mother, could not be freer of any taint of scandal or criticism.

“Accusations of this nature have been made in this court, but not by me and not against her. Of these sins, Madeleine Bellamy, the girl for whom all your pity has been invoked, has stood accused. She is dead. I, too, invoke your pity for her and such forgetfulness as you can mete out for the folly and dishonour that led to her death. For if she had not gone to that cottage to meet her lover, death would not have claimed her. She met death because she was there, alone and unprotected. Whether she was struck down by a thief, a blackmailer, an old lover or a new one, is not within my province to prove or in yours to decide. My intent is only to show you that so slight is the case against Susan Ives and Stephen Bellamy that a stronger one could be made out against half a dozen people that have been paraded before you in order to defame her.

===Chinese===

“就在不多几个星期前，你们中随便哪一位，都会把她当作深深艳羡的对象。苏·艾夫斯，被疼爱的、被珍视的、被呵护的；苏·艾夫斯，安然快活地穿行于一个鲜花蓝天、片云全无的世界；苏·艾夫斯，幸运而被人深爱的，众神的宠儿。而如今她就坐在你们面前，诸位先生：被丈夫出卖，被每一条嚼舌的闲口玷污，被从孩子和家中撕走，被这个国家从最清高无瑕的大报到基督教世界最下流的小报的每一份报刊示众，被当着全世界烙上那一最黑暗、最可怕、最卑贱的印记——女杀人犯。

“女杀人犯！这个姑娘，如此忠诚、如此慷慨、如此诚实，认识她的人都相信她比这劳碌尘世里的常人质地更精；如此骄傲、如此敏感、如此爱洁，爱她的人宁肯一千次看她躺进坟墓，也不忍她受这几十天来落在她头上的丑恶酷刑。她的这份遭遇算什么，诸位先生？她的命运算什么？是什么把她逼到了这个可怕的关口？轻浮？不忠？声名狼藉？鲁莽不慎？恶意？你们的妻子、你们的女儿、你们的母亲，也不能比她更干净地超然于任何污点与非议之外。

“这一类指控，在本庭上确曾有人提出过，但不是我，也不是针对她。这些罪愆，被控的是玛德琳·贝拉米——那个你们所有的怜悯都被唤去施与的姑娘。她死了。我也向你们为她的灵魂求一份怜悯，求你们在她招致死亡的糊涂与失贞上，量力施与一份宽忘。因为她若不去那间小屋会她的情人，死亡便索不到她。她之所以遇上死亡，是因为她在那里，孤身一人，无人护佑。她是死在窃贼手里、勒索者手里、旧情人手里还是新情人手里，证明之不在我的职权，裁断之也不在你们的职权。我的用意只是让你们看清：对苏珊·艾夫斯和斯蒂芬·贝拉米的指控是何等单薄——单薄到随便拼出半打人，都能立起一桩更硬的案子，就凭这一路为了抹黑她而被牵到你们面前的那些人。”

===Original===

“What is this case against her? I say against her, because if you decide that Mrs. Ives is not guilty, the case against Stephen Bellamy collapses automatically. It is not the contention of the state that he committed this crime. The evidence produced shows, according to the state, that he and Mrs. Ives were together throughout the evening, at her instigation. If she had nothing whatever to do with the crime, it follows inevitably that neither did her companion. I again, therefore, turn your attention to Mrs. Ives, and ask you once more what is this case against her?

“This: You are asked to believe that this girl﻿—many of you have daughters older than Susan Ives﻿—that this girl, gently born, gently bred and gentle-hearted, upon receiving information from a half-intoxicated and infatuated suitor of Mimi Bellamy’s that Mimi was carrying on an affair with her husband, Patrick Ives, dined peacefully at home, rose from the table, summoned Mrs. Bellamy’s adoring husband to meet her down a back lane, procured a knife from a table in her husband’s study and straightway sallied forth to remove the encumbrance that she had discovered in her smooth path by the simple and straightforward process of murdering her﻿—murder, you note, premeditated, preconceived, and prearranged. Roughly, an hour and a half elapsed between the time that Susan Ives set out and the moment that the scream fixes as that of the murder.

“Presumably some of that time was occupied in convincing Mr. Bellamy of the excellence of her scheme and some of it in idle conversation﻿—the time must have been occupied somehow; the actual rise and fall of a knife is no lengthy matter. Mr. Bellamy, we gather, was so entertained by the death of his idolized wife that he yielded to hearty laughter﻿—Mr. Thorne has told you of that laugh, I believe.

“The lamp has gone out, so in total darkness they proceed to collect the jewels and wait peacefully until Mr. Thorne has put his keys under the doormat﻿—the door is locked; they have thought of everything, you see﻿—when once more they venture forth, enter an automobile that has the convenient quality of becoming either visible or invisible as serves them best, and return promptly and speedily to the house of Mr. Stephen Bellamy.

===Chinese===

“这桩针对她的案子是什么？我说针对她，是因为只要你们裁定艾夫斯太太无罪，针对斯蒂芬·贝拉米的案子便自动崩塌。本州并非主张罪行是他犯下的。依本州的说法，其所出示的证据表明，当晚他始终与艾夫斯太太在一起，而且是她的主意。倘若她与罪行毫无干系，那么她的同伴不可避免地也毫无干系。因此我再次把诸位的注意引向艾夫斯太太，再问一遍：针对她的案子究竟是什么？

“是这个：你们被要求相信——你们中许多人的女儿都比苏珊·艾夫斯年长——相信这个姑娘，出身温良、教养温良、心地温良，在从来米·贝拉米的一个半醉的痴情追求者口中听得米米与她丈夫帕特里克·艾夫斯私通的消息之后，安安静静在家里吃了晚饭，离了餐桌，唤来贝拉米太太那位痴心的丈夫，约她在一条后巷相会，从丈夫书房的桌上弄到一把刀，随即跃跃出征，用杀人这条简单直捷的办法，除掉她在坦途上发现的障碍——请注意，是谋杀，预谋的、预念的、预置的谋杀。从苏珊·艾夫斯动身，到那声尖叫钉定的凶案时刻，中间大约过了一个半钟头。

“这段时间里，想必一部分用来向贝拉米先生兜售她方案的妙处，一部分用来闲谈——时间总得有东西填满；刀起刀落，本不是长久的事。而贝拉米先生呢，据推测，被他崇拜的妻子之死逗得纵声大笑——索恩先生已经向诸位讲过那声笑了，我相信。

“灯灭了，于是他们在一片漆黑里收拾珠宝，安安静静等索恩先生把钥匙压进门垫——门锁着；你们看，他们什么都想到了——然后再度出动，钻进一辆具有随需忽隐忽现之妙质的汽车，风驰电掣地折回斯蒂芬·贝拉米先生的家。”

===Original===

“Possibly you wonder why they do that. It is barely ten, and almost anyone might see them, thereby destroying their carefully concocted movie alibi, but possibly they thought that the Bellamy house would be a nice place to hide the pearls and talk things over. We are left a trifle in the dark as to their motives here, but undoubtedly the prosecutor will clear all that up perfectly. Ten minutes later they come out, and still together start off once more, presumably in the direction of Mrs. Ives’s home so that everyone there can get a good look at them together, while Mrs. Ives still has the knife and the bloodstained coat in her possession. There they part, Mrs. Ives to straighten up a little before she takes some fruit up to Mrs. Daniel Ives, Mr. Bellamy presumably to return to his own home and a night of well-earned repose.

“In the morning Mrs. Ives rises sufficiently early to pack up the blood drenched garments in a large box for the Salvation Army; she turns them over to a maid to turn over to a chauffeur, requests a fresh pair of gloves and sets forth to early church﻿—the service which she has attended every Sunday of her life since she was a mite of six, with eyes too big for her face, hair to her waist, skirts to her knees and little white cotton gloves that would fit a doll if it weren’t too big. The prosecutor leaves her there telling her God that last night she had had to kill a girl who was liable to make a nuisance of herself before she got through by cutting down Sue Ives’s monthly income considerably. Of course it all may seem a trifle incomprehensible to us, but it’s undoubtedly perfectly clear to God and the prosecutor.

“I think that that is a fair and accurate statement of the state’s case, though Mr. Farr undoubtedly can﻿—and will﻿—make it sound a great deal more plausible when he gets at it. But that’s what it boils down to, and all the specious reasoning and forensic and histrionic ability in the world won’t make it one atom less preposterous. That’s their case.

===Chinese===

“诸位也许纳闷他们这是干什么。才十点差几分，几乎谁都可能撞见他们，从而毁掉他们精心炮制的电影不在场证明；不过他们也许觉得，贝拉米宅是藏珍珠、商量事的好地方。他们此处的动机，我们还有些摸黑，但检察官无疑会把一切澄清得漂漂亮亮。十分钟后他们出来，仍旧一道，再度出发，想必是朝艾夫斯太太家的方向，好让那边人人看清他们双双同行——此时艾夫斯太太身上还带着刀和那件血衣。到了那里他们分手：艾夫斯太太进去稍稍收拾，然后给丹尼尔·艾夫斯太太送水果上楼；贝拉米先生呢，想必回自己家，安享一晚安枕。

“早晨，艾夫斯太太起得够早，把浸透血污的衣物打进一只大箱子，交给救世军；她把箱子交给女仆转交司机，要了一副新手套，出发上早礼拜——这礼拜她从六岁的小不点起每个星期天都做，那时她眼睛大得撑满了小脸，头发齐腰，裙子齐膝，一双小白棉手套，若不是娃娃太大，娃娃戴正合适。检察官就撒下她，由她告诉上帝：昨夜她不得不杀了一个姑娘，因为这姑娘到头来很可能大大削减苏·艾夫斯的月进，闹得人不得安生。这一切在我们看来自然有点费解，但在上帝和检察官那里，想必一清二楚。

“我认为，这是对本州案情的一个公允而准确的陈述，尽管法尔先生无疑能——也一定会——把它修饰得可信得多，等他着手的时候。可扒光了皮，它就是这个东西；天下所有似是而非的推理、所有的法庭雄辩和做戏的本事，也不能使它减少一星半点的荒谬。他们的案子就是这样。”

===Original===

“And on what evidence are we asked to believe this incredible farrago? I’ll tell you. We have the word of a hysterical and morbidly sensitive girl with a supposed grievance that she overheard a telephone conversation; we have the word of a vindictive young vixen who is leading nothing more nor less than a life of sin that she planted a note and failed to find it again; we have the disjointed narrative of an unfortunate fellow so far gone in drink, and love that he was half out of his senses at the time that he is supposed to be reporting these crucial events and has since blown his brains out; we have the word of an ex-jailbird who might well have more reasons than one for directing the finger of suspicion at a convenient victim; we have a trooper, eager for credit and prominence, swearing to you that he can as clearly recognize and identify a scrap of earth bearing the imprint of a bit of tire as though it were the upturned countenance of his favourite child﻿—a bit of tire, gentlemen, which undoubtedly has some hundreds of millions of twins in this capacious country of ours.

“It is on this evidence, fantastic though it may sound, that my distinguished adversary is asking you to condemn to death a gentle lady and an honest gentleman. On the testimony of a neurotic, a love thief, a jailbird, and a drunkard! These are plain words to describe plain truths. I propose to produce witnesses of unimpeachable record to substantiate every one of them.

“It is, frankly, a great temptation to me to rest the case for the defense here and now; because in all honesty I cannot see how it would take any twelve sane men in this country five consecutive minutes to reach and return a verdict of not guilty. Remember, it does not devolve on me to prove that Susan Ives and Stephen Bellamy are innocent, but on the state to prove that they are guilty. If they have proved that these two are guilty, then they have proved that I am. I believe absolutely that one is not more absurd than the other.

===Chinese===

“而我们被要求凭什么证据来相信这堆难以置信的大杂烩？我来告诉你们。我们有一位歇斯底里、病态敏感、自认受了委屈的姑娘的话，说她无意间听到一段电话通话；我们有一位狠毒的年轻泼妇的话——她过的那种日子，不折不扣就是罪恶的日子——说是她栽了一张条子、后来又没能再找到它；我们有一个可怜虫的语无伦次的叙述，此人酒与爱陷得那样深，在他据称目击这桩桩关键时刻的当儿已半失神志，事后又崩了自己的脑袋；我们有一位坐过牢的家伙的话，此人把嫌疑之指拨向一个现成的替罪羊，理由很可能不止一条；我们有一位骑警，急着邀功扬名，向你们赌咒说，他认一块带着一条轮胎印的碎土，能像认他宝贝孩子的脸一样清楚——一条轮胎印，诸位先生，在我们这个幅员辽阔的国家里，无疑有着千千万万个一模一样的孪生兄弟。

“凭的就是这样的证据——听着离奇，可千真万确——我那位杰出的对手就要请你们把一位淑女和一位正人君子判处死刑。凭一个神经质、一个窃爱者、一个囚犯、一个酒鬼的证词！这些是直话，说的是实事。我准备传召记录清白无瑕的证人，把每一句都坐实。

“坦白说，此刻就地把辩方案子收场，于我是莫大的诱惑；因为我老实想不出，这个国家随便哪十二个心智健全的人，要花整整连续五分钟，才能达成并宣判一个无罪裁决。记住，证明苏珊·艾夫斯和斯蒂芬·贝拉米无辜的责任不在我；证明他们有罪的责任在州。他们若证明得了这两人有罪，也就证明得了我有罪。我绝对相信，这两件事一样荒唐。”

===Original===

“On that profound conviction I could, I say, rest this case. But there is a bare possibility that some minor aspects of the case are not so clear to you as they are to me﻿—there is a passionate desire on my part to leave not one stone unturned in behalf of either of my clients﻿—and there is also, I confess, a very human desire to confront and confound some of the glib crew who have mounted the steps to that stand day after day somewhat too greatly concerned to swear away two human lives. It will not be a lengthy and exhausting performance, I promise. Four or five honest men and women will suffice, and you will find, I believe, that truth travels as fast as light.

“Nor shall I produce the hundreds upon hundreds of character witnesses that I could bring before you to tell you that of all the fine and true and gallant souls that have crossed their paths, the most gallant, the finest and the truest is the girl that this very sovereign state is asking you to brand as a murderess. In the case of *the People versus Susan Ives* I shall call only one character witness into that box﻿—Susan Ives herself. And if, after you have listened to her, after you have seen her, after you have heard her tell her story, you do not believe that society and the law and the people themselves, clamouring for a victim, have made a frightful and shocking error, it will be because I am not only a bad lawyer but a bad prophet as well. Gentlemen, it is my profound and solemn conviction that whatever I may be as a lawyer, I am in very truth a good prophet!”

“I don’t believe he’s a bad lawyer,” said the redheaded girl breathlessly. “He’s a good lawyer. He is! He makes everyone see just how ridiculous the case against them is. That’s being a good lawyer, isn’t it. That’s making a good speech, isn’t it? That’s﻿—”

“He’s a pompous old jackass,” said the reporter unkindly. “But he loves his Sue, and he did just a little better than he knows how. Not so good at that either. You don’t make a case ridiculous by jeering at it. If﻿—”

“Call Mrs. Platz!” boomed the oblivious object of his strictures.

“Mrs. Adolph Platz!”

Mrs. Platz, minute and meek, with straw-coloured hair and straw-coloured lashes and a small pink nose in a small white face, advanced toward the witness stand with no assurance whatever.

“Mrs. Platz, what was your position on June 19, 1926?”

===Chinese===

“凭这一深沉的信念，我说，我本可以就此收场。但存在一线小小的可能：案子里某些细处，在诸位眼中不如在我眼中清楚——我怀着一腔热望，要为我的两位当事人力求滴水不漏——而且我承认，还有一桩很合人情的愿望：会一会、煞一煞那伙油嘴滑舌的家伙，他们连日来一级级登上那个证人席，过于起劲地发誓送掉两条人命。我保证，这不会是一场冗长劳神的表演。四五位诚实的先生太太就够了，而且我相信，诸位将会看到，真理快如光。

“我也不打算把成百上千位品格证人带到诸位面前——我原是可以的——让他们告诉你们：在他们平生遇见的一切高尚、真诚、勇敢的灵魂里，最高尚、最真诚、最勇敢的，正是这个主权州正请你们烙上女杀人犯印记的姑娘。在人民对苏珊·艾夫斯一案中，我只传一位品格证人进那个席位——苏珊·艾夫斯本人。倘若你们听了她、见了她、听了她讲完她的故事之后，仍然不相信社会、法律和喧嚣着索要牺牲品的人民大众铸成了骇人听闻的大错，那只能是因为我不仅是个坏律师，还是个坏先知。诸位先生，我深沉而庄严地确信：无论我做律师算什么水准，做先知，我是真正在行的！”

“我不信他是个坏律师，”红头发姑娘气都喘不匀地说。“他是个好律师。就是！他让每个人都看清强加给他们的案子有多可笑。这就是好律师，对不对？这就是好演讲，对不对？这就是——”

“他是头浮夸的老蠢驴，”记者刻薄地说。“不过他疼他的苏，而且这一回发挥得比他自己会的略好一点。其实也没多好。把一个案子吼可笑，不等于把它驳可笑。要是——”

“传普拉茨太太！”挨骂的当事人在台上浑然不觉，嗡声大作。

“阿道夫·普拉茨太太！”

普拉茨太太，小小的，怯怯的，草黄头发，草黄睫毛，小白脸上一只小粉鼻，毫无把握地朝证人席挪过去。

“普拉茨太太，一九二六年六月十九日，您做什么营生？”

===Original===

“I was chambermaid-waitress with Mrs. Alfred Bond at Oyster Bay.”

“Had you been formerly in the employ of Mrs. Patrick Ives?”

“Yes, sir, I was, for about six months in 1925. I just did chamber work there, though.”

“Was your husband there at the time?”
