“这可非常有意思，”兰伯特先生眉开眼笑——“确实非常有意思。可要是那本书里当真有字条，您就会发现它，对吧？”

“嗯，只要不是胡涂到家，这命题相当稳当。”

“而您要是发现了它，就会去赴约，对吧？”

“我肯定会尽一切努力去。”

“退掉牌局？”

“想必。”

“抄田野近道？”

“我怎么去说不好。这就有点纸上谈兵了，不是吗？”

“而在那间园丁小屋里等着您的，就会是那位不幸的姑娘，带着那些您务必弄到手的信？”

“您怎么不问问他，那时他口袋里是不是还揣着那把刀？”法尔先生温和地插话。“怎么不问问他，他会拿刀干什么？这么要紧的您可别漏了。”

兰伯特得意扬扬的征程被人如此粗鲁地拦截，鼓着眼睛、嗓着一嗓子被冒犯的抗议，直奔卡弗法官那张无动于衷的脸。

“庭上，在律师席上效力多年，我还从未遇见过如此明目张胆的失礼——”

卡弗法官厉声切断这些尖利的训斥：“兰伯特先生，在律师席上效力多年，本庭也还从未遇见过有谁像您这样主持一场询问——而本庭，由于控辩双方均未抗议，也纵容了您。法尔先生的异议方式虽不合规矩，其本身却完全正当。您向证人提出的这些问题纯属假设情形，证人完全有权拒绝回答。请继续。”

“我来答。”帕特·艾夫斯说。“我要是发现了字条，就会去小屋，把钱给米米，拿回信，我们谁也不必这几个星期琢磨换个环境地狱该多美好舒心了。我求老天爷让我发现它。这是您想知道的吗？”

===Original===

It was very far indeed from what Mr. Lambert wanted to know. However, he turned a wary eye on the jury, who were contemplating soberly and not too sympathetically the bitter, insolent face of the young gentleman in the witness box. Flippancy was obviously an evil stench in their nostrils. Mr. Lambert rattled the letters still clenched in his hand reminiscently.

“There are two or three things in these letters that I’d like to have you reconcile with the statement that they were written in 1916. First, what does it mean, Mr. Ives, when you say: ‘I keep telling myself that we’re mad﻿—that there’s black trouble ahead of us﻿—that I haven’t any right in the world to let you do this’﻿—do what, Mr. Ives?”

“Carry on the highly indiscreet affair that we were indulging in,” said Pat Ives, his white face a shade whiter. “We’d both completely lost our heads. She wasn’t willing to marry me because she was afraid that I hadn’t it in me to make good. There was a lot of ugly gossip going on, and it had upset her.”

“Quite so,” smiled Mr. Lambert dreadfully; “oh, quite so. Now in the one that begins: ‘Mimi darling, darling, darling, it’s after four o’clock and﻿—’ ”

“Are you going through those letters again?” inquired Patrick Ives, his hands clenched on the edge of the box.

“Just one or two little things that I’d like cleared up, and I’m sure that these gentlemen would too. It goes on: ‘Dawn﻿—I always thought that was the worst word in the English language and here I am on my knees waiting for it, and ranting like﻿—’

“You needn’t go on,” said Patrick Ives, “if what you’re really after is when they were written. The sun that rose at 4:30 that morning in June in 1916 would have kept me waiting exactly one hour and six minutes longer in 1926. You and Mimi and I had forgotten just one thing, Mr. Lambert﻿—we’d forgotten that in 1916 there was no such thing as daylight saving.”

===Chinese===

这离兰伯特先生想知道的，实在相去十万八千里。不过他还是警惕地瞥了一眼陪审团——他们正神情肃穆、不无反感地打量着证人席里那位年轻绅士那张尖刻狂放的脸。轻浮在他们鼻子里显然是一股恶臭。兰伯特先生回味地抖了抖still攥在手里的那沓信。

“这些信里有两三处，我想请您跟‘写于一九一六年’的说法对对榫。第一，艾夫斯先生，您写道：‘我一遍遍告诉自己：我们疯了——前头有漆黑的大祸——我全无权利让您这么做’——做什么，艾夫斯先生？”

“继续我们沉濯其中的那桩极不检点的私情。”帕特·艾夫斯说，白脸又白了一分。“我们俩都完全昏了头。她不肯嫁我，是怕我没本事成事。当时闲话闹得很凶，把她搅乱了。”

“正是如此。”兰伯特先生可怕地微笑着。“哦，正是如此。那么在开头是‘米米，亲爱的，亲爱的，亲爱的，已经四点多了，而且——’的那封里——”

“你们还要把这些信再过一遍？”帕特里克·艾夫斯质问，双手扣紧了证人席的边沿。

“只清理一两件小事，我相信这几位先生也想弄明白。信里接着写：‘黎明——我一向以为这是英语里最恶毒的词，可如今我却跪着等它，像个——’

“您不必念下去了。”帕特里克·艾夫斯说，“如果您真正想问的是写信的时间。一九一六年六月那天早晨四点半升起的太阳，到一九二六年，会让我多等整整一小时零六分。您和米米和我，兰伯特先生，恰恰忘了一件事——我们忘了，一九一六年还没有夏令时这回事。”

===Original===

And through the staggered silence that invaded some three hundred-odd people who had forgotten precisely the same thing, there rose a little laugh﻿—a gay, excited, triumphant little laugh, as though somewhere a small girl had suddenly received a beautiful and unexpected present. It came from just behind Mr. Lambert’s sagging shoulders﻿—it came from﻿—The startled eyes of those in the courtroom jerked in that direction, staring unbelievingly at the quiet figure, so quiet, so cool, so gravely aloof. But the redheaded girl felt idiotic tears sting swiftly beneath her lids. Under the lowered barrier of Sue Ives’s lashes there still danced the echo of that joyous truant, shameless and unafraid. It was she who had laughed, after all.

Mr. Lambert was not laughing. “You are a little late in recalling this,” he remarked heavily.

“Oh, a good deal late,” agreed Patrick Ives. “But, you see, I hadn’t been going in for watching the sun rise for some time previous to the murder. Since then I have. And when I heard that letter read in court the other day, something clicked in my head. Not five o’clock, and the sun was up! Something wrong there. I went back to New York and looked it up in the public library. On Friday, June 9, 1916, the sun rose at four twenty-two a.m. On Wednesday, June 9, 1926, the sun rose at five twenty-eight. So that’s that.”

“Have you a certified statement to that effect?” inquired Lambert, forlornly pompous.

“No,” said Mr. Ives. “But I can lend you a World Almanac.”

“You seem to find a trial for murder a very amusing affair,” remarked Lambert heavily, his eyes once more on the jury.

“You’re wrong,” said Patrick Ives briefly. “I don’t.”

“I do not believe that your attitude makes farther examination desirable,” commented Lambert judicially. “Cross-examine.”

Farr rose casually from his chair, his hands in his pockets, his head cocked a trifle to one side. “Mr. Ives,” he said leisurely, “I’m going to ask you the one question that Mr. Lambert didn’t. Did you murder Madeleine Bellamy?”

After a pause that seemed interminable, Pat Ives lifted his eyes from their scrutiny of his hands, locked at the edge of the witness box. “No,” he said tonelessly.

“No further questions,” remarked Mr. Farr, still more leisurely resuming his seat.

===Chinese===

愕然的寂静漫过那三百来个忘掉同一件事的人；寂静中升起一小声笑——欢快、激动、得意的一小声笑，仿佛什么地方有个小姑娘忽然收到了一件漂亮又意外的礼物。笑声来自兰伯特垮下来的双肩后——来自——满庭惊愕的眼睛倏地扭向那边，难以置信地盯着那道安静的影子，那么安静，那么冷静，那么端庄地疏离。可红头发姑娘觉得眼皮底下傻傻地刺痛起来。在苏·艾夫斯低垂的睫毛这道理性的屏障下，仍荡着那个偷欢孩子的回声，无耻而无畏。笑的，终究是她。

兰伯特先生可没笑。“您想起这件事，未免迟了些。”他沉沉地说。

“哦，迟得不止一些。”帕特里克·艾夫斯同意道。“可是您瞧，案发前我有段日子没去看日出。案发后我看了。前几天在法庭上听人念那封信时，我脑子里咔哒一响。还不到五点，太阳就出来了！这里头有毛病。我回纽约去，在公共图书馆查了。一九一六年六月九日，星期五，日出为凌晨四点二十二分。一九二六年六月九日，星期三，日出为五点二十八分。就是这样。”

“您有这方面的公证文书吗？”兰伯特问，徒然摆着架子。

“没有。”艾夫斯先生说。“不过我可以借您一本《世界年鉴》。”

“您倒把一桩谋杀审判当成很乐的事。”兰伯特沉甸甸地说，眼睛又转向陪审团。

“您错了。”帕特里克·艾夫斯简短地说。“我不觉得乐。”

“我认为，您这副态度，已使继续询问失去意义。”兰伯特以法官的口吻评论道。“盘问。”

法尔随随便便从椅子上站起，双手插兜，脑袋微微一侧。“艾夫斯先生，”他从容地说，“我要问您一个兰伯特先生没问的问题。玛德琳·贝拉米是您杀的吗？”

隔了仿佛没有尽头的一停，帕特·艾夫斯把眼睛从紧扣于证人席边沿的双手上抬起来。“不是。”他毫无声调地说。

“没有其他问题了。”法尔先生说，以更胜一筹的从容重新落座。

===Original===

Lambert glared﻿—swallowed﻿—glared again, and turned on his heel. “Mrs. Ives, will you be good enough to take the stand?”

She was on her feet before the words were off his lips, brushing by him with her light, swift step and a look of contemptuous anger that was bright and terrible as a sword.

“Looks as though his precious Sue was going to give Uncle Dudley a bad half hour,” murmured the reporter exultantly.

“Why?” whispered the redheaded girl. “Why did she look like that?”

“Because I rather fancy that Lambert has just a scrap exceeded his authority in his efforts to speed Pat Ives to the gallows. The old walrus made out a fairly damaging case against him, even if he did snort himself purple. If﻿—”

“Mrs. Ives, I’m going to ask you to tell us in your own words just what occurred on the evening of the nineteenth of June, from the time that Mr. Farwell spoke to you at the club. I won’t interrupt unless I feel that something is not quite clear. At what time did the conversation with Mr. Farwell take place?”

She looked so small, sitting there﻿—so small and young and fearless, with her dark, bright eyes and her lifted chin and the pale gold wings of her hair folded under the curve of the little russet hat. She had no colour at all﻿—not in her cheeks, not in her lips.

“It was a little after five,” said Sue Ives, and the redheaded girl gave a sigh of sheer delight. Once or twice in a lifetime a voice like that falls on our lucky ears﻿—a voice clear and fresh as running water, alive and beautiful and effortless. The girl in the box did not have to lift it a half tone to have it penetrate to the farthest corner of the gallery. “We got in from the links just at five, and Elliot came up and asked me if he could bring me something to drink. I said yes, and when he came back he suggested that we go over and sit on the steps, as he had a splitting headache, and everyone was making a good deal of a racket. We hadn’t been there more than five minutes before he told me.”

“Before he told you what?” prompted Lambert helpfully.

===Chinese===

兰伯特瞪眼——干室——再瞪眼，转身就走。“艾夫斯太太，劳驾您上证人席，好吗？”

话音未落她已起身，迈着轻快碎步擦过他身旁，那神情里的愤恚明亮而可怕，如一柄出鞘的剑。

“瞧这架势，他宝贝的苏要给达德利大叔好瞧的半个钟头了。”记者幸灾乐祸地低语。

“为什么？”红头发姑娘悄声问。“她为什么是那副神情？”

“因为我相当怀疑，兰伯特为了把帕特·艾夫斯快点送上绞架，稍稍越了那么一点权限。这头老海象给他罗织了一份相当致命的案情，哪怕他自己哼得满面发紫。如果——”

“艾夫斯太太，我要请您用自己的话，讲讲六月十九日晚间的事，从法威尔先生在俱乐部跟您说话讲起。除非我觉得哪里不清楚，我不会打断您。与法威尔先生的谈话，发生在什么时间？”

她坐在那儿显得那么小——那么小、那么年轻、那么无畏，深色的亮眼，扬起的下巴，淡金色的头发像一对翅膀拢在小赭色帽檐下。她脸上没有一点血色——颊上没有，唇上也没有。

“五点刚过。”苏·艾夫斯说；红头发姑娘发出一声纯粹的赞叹。这样的嗓音，一生里只有一两次落进我们有幸的耳朵——清亮新鲜如流水，鲜活、美丽、毫不费力。证人席上的这位姑娘不必抬高半个调门，声音便能传到旁听席最深远的角落。“我们五点整从球场回来，埃利奥特迎上来，问我要不要喝点什么。我说好，他回来时提议我们过去坐台阶上，因为他头疼欲裂，而大家闹得凶。我们坐下不到五分钟，他就告诉我了。”

“告诉你什么之前？”兰伯特热心地提词。

===Original===

“Before he told me that Pat was having an affair with Mimi Bellamy.” She did not vouchsafe him even a glance, but kept the clear, stern little face turned squarely to the twelve attentive ones lifted to hers. “At first I thought that it was simply preposterous nonsense﻿—I told him so. Everyone knew that Elliot was absolutely out of his head over Mimi, and I thought that he really was going a little mad. I could see that he’d been drinking, of course, and I wasn’t even as angry as I ought to have been, because he was so unhappy﻿—dreadfully unhappy. And then he said that he’d spied on them﻿—that he’d seen them go to the cottage together. Well, that﻿—that was different. That didn’t sound like the kind of thing that you’d invent or imagine, no matter how unbalanced you were.”

“You believed it?”

“No, not at first﻿—not quite. But it bothered me dreadfully all the way home from the club﻿—all the time that we were standing around in our living room waiting for the cocktails. I couldn’t get it out of my head. And then Pat came in.”

She paused, frowning a little at the memory of that sick perplexity.

“You say that Mr. Ives came in?”

“Yes. He was looking dreadfully tired and﻿—excited. No, that’s not the word. Keyed up﻿—different. Or perhaps it was just that I expected him to look different. I don’t know. Anyway, Elliot started to go then, and I went into the hall after him, because he’d been drinking a good deal more, and I was afraid that he’d talk as indiscreetly to someone else as he had to me. I couldn’t think very clearly yet, but I was quite sure that that ought to be stopped. So I asked him to be careful, and he said that he would.”

“Did you notice Melanie Cordier in the library?”

===Chinese===

“在他告诉我帕特和米米·贝拉米有私情之前。”她连一眼都懒得赏他，只把那张清朗端肃的小脸端端正正对着那十二张仰起的专注面孔。“起初我以为那纯属荒唐胡扯——我就这么跟他说的。谁都知道埃利奥特色迷米米迷得神魂颠倒，我以为他真是有点疯了。当然，我看得出他喝过酒，而我连本该有的那点气都没生出来，因为他太不幸了——不幸极了。后来他说他盯过他们的梢——说亲眼见他们一起去过那间小屋。这就——这就不同了。那听起来不像是编得出来、想得出来的事，不管人怎么失措都编不出。”

“您信了？”

“不，起初没信——没全信。可从俱乐部回家的路上，它一路可怕地繺着我——大家站在客厅里等鸡尾酒的时候，它还繺着我。我甩不开它。后来帕特进来了。”

她停住，为那段恶心的惶惑微微皱眉。

“您是说，艾夫斯先生进来了？”

“是的。他看上去累极了——而且亢奋。不，这个词不对。绷着——反常。也可能只是我盼着他显得反常。我说不清。反正那时埃利奥特要走了，我跟着他到门厅，因为他酒喝得更多了，我怕他跟别人也像跟我一样口无遮拦。我还想不很清楚，但我确信那必须拦住。于是我嘱咐他小心，他说会的。”

“您在书房里看见梅拉妮·科迪埃了吗？”

===Original===

“No. I was watching Elliot. He looked so wretchedly unhappy that I was really worried about him. Well, anyway, he went off without even saying goodbye, and I went back toward the living room. Just as I came up to it I heard George Dallas say, ‘We can count on you for the poker party tonight, can’t we?’ And Pat said, ‘I’ll surely try to make it, but don’t count on me.’ Something inside my head went click, and all the pieces in the puzzle fell into place. I walked straight into the room and up to where he was standing. He’d gone over to the table and was pouring out another of those new cocktails. Everyone was making a dreadful racket, laughing and talking. I said, ‘Nell Conroy wanted us to go to the movies tonight. Don’t you think that it would be rather fun?’ And he said, ‘Sorry, but I told George that I’d run over for a poker game. Tell Nell that you’ll go, and then I won’t worry about you being lonely.’ I said, ‘That’s a good idea.’ And Pat said, ‘Be back in a minute. I have some papers I want to get rid of.’

“He went across the hall; I could hear his steps. I felt just exactly as though I’d taken poison and I stood there waiting for it to begin to work. Someone came up to me to say goodbye﻿—I think it was the Conroys, and then everyone else began to go, too, the way they always do. I started to go out to the porch with them, and while I was passing through the hall I saw Pat standing by the desk. He was looking at some papers in his hand. I went on toward the porch, calling back over my shoulder that everyone was leaving. In a minute, he came out too. I looked to see whether he still had the papers in his hand, but he hadn’t. While we were both standing there watching them drive off, Melanie came out, announced dinner, and we went in. Pat stopped behind in the study for a moment, but he didn’t go near the desk drawer﻿—I could see it from my place at the table.”

“Could you have seen him take a book from the corner shelf?”

“No﻿—the screen between the rooms cut off that corner.”

“Nothing unusual occurred at dinner?”

“No. That made it worse. Nothing unusual occurred at all. Pat talked and laughed a good deal, but that’s what he always did.”

“And after dinner?”

===Chinese===

“没有。我在看着埃利奥特。他那副愁苦相让我真为他担心。反正，他连再见都没说就走了，我折回客厅那边。刚走近，我就听见乔治·达拉斯说：‘今晚的牌局能算上你一个吧？’帕特说：‘我一定尽量来，不过别把宝押我身上。’我脑子里咔哒一声，谜里所有的拼图块各就各位。我径直走进房间，走到他站的地方。他已走到桌边，正斟又一轮那种新式鸡尾酒。大家闹得凶，又说又笑。我说：‘内尔·康罗伊约咱们今晚看电影去。你不觉得挺有意思吗？’他说：‘抱歉，我跟乔治说了要过去打牌。跟内尔说你去，这样我也不担心你一个人闷。’我说：‘好主意。’帕特说：‘马上回来。我有些文件要处理掉。’

“他穿过门厅去了；我听得见他的脚步。我的感觉就像吞了毒药，站在那儿等它发作。有人过来道别——我记得是康罗伊夫妇，接着其他人也照例陆续告辞。我跟他们往外走到门廊，穿过门厅时，我看见帕特站在书桌旁，正看手里的一些文件。我继续往门廊走，回头招呼说大家都要走了。不一会儿他也出来了。我看他手里还拿没拿文件——没有了。我们俩正站在那儿目送他们开车离开，梅拉妮出来开饭，我们便进去了。帕特在书房耽搁了片刻，但他没走近书桌抽屉——我坐在餐桌前看得见那儿。”

“您可能看见他从角落书架上取书吗？”

“不可能——两屋之间的屏风挡住了那个角落。”

“晚餐时没有异常？”

“没有。这反而更糟。压根什么事都没有。帕特谈笑了不少，可他一向如此。”

“晚饭后呢？”

===Original===

“After dinner Mother Ives went out into the garden, and Pat asked me to come into the study to look at the clipper ship that he’d been making for Pete. All the time that I was supposed to be looking at it, I couldn’t take my eyes off the desk, wondering what he’d done with those papers﻿—wondering what they were. There had been quite a little pile of them. After a while I couldn’t stand it any longer, and I said, ‘If you want to say good night to Pete and Polly, you’ll have to hurry. They ought to be asleep by now.’ He said, ‘Lord, that’s true!’ He snatched up the boat and started for the door, and I called after him, ‘I’m not coming. I kissed them good night before dinner.’ I waited until I heard his footsteps on the stairs﻿—”

She paused for a moment, pushing the bright hair back from her brow as though she found it suddenly heavy.

“And then, Mrs. Ives?”

“Then,” said Sue Ives steadily, “I did something disgusting. I searched the desk, I pushed the door to, so that none of the servants could see me if they passed through the hall, and I hurried like mad. I don’t know exactly what I expected to find, but I thought that maybe those papers were letters from Mimi, and then I knew that Pat kept his checkbook there, too, and I thought that there might be entries of some kind that would tell me something; I could bear anything but not knowing. It was like a﻿—like a frenzy. Oh, it was worse! The top drawer on the left-hand side of the desk was locked.”

She paused again for a moment, staring down as curiously and intently at the upturned faces below her as they stared up at her; then, with a quick, impatient shake of her head she went on: “But that didn’t make any difference, because I knew where the key was. I used the top right-hand drawer myself for my household accounts and bills and loose silver, and I kept it locked because, whenever Pat brought home gold pieces from his directors’ meetings, we used to put them there. We saved them up until we had enough to get a present for the house, something beautiful and﻿—No, that doesn’t make any difference. We called the drawer the bank, and Pat showed me where he kept the key so that I could always get into it.”

“Where did he keep this key?”

===Chinese===

“晚饭后，艾夫斯妈妈到花园去了，帕特叫我进书房看他给皮特做的那艘快速帆船。表面上我在看船，实则眼睛离不开那张书桌，琢廑他把那些文件怎么处置了——琢廑它们是什么。那一沓文件还不少呢。过了一阵我再也忍不住，便说：‘你要是想跟皮特和波莉道晚安，就得抓紧。他们这会儿该睡了。’他说：‘天哪，可不是！’他一把抓起船就往门口走，我在他身后喊：‘我就不去了，晚饭前我吻过他们晚安了。’我一直等到听见他的脚步上了楼梯——”

她停了片刻，把明亮的头发从额前掠开，仿佛它忽然重了起来。

“然后呢，艾夫斯太太？”

“然后，”苏·艾夫斯平平稳稳地说，“我干了一件恶心事。我搜了那张书桌。我把门掩上，免得仆人路过门厅看见我，然后疯了一样地翻。我并不确切知道想找什么，只是想，那些文件也许会是米米的信；后来又想起帕特的支票簿也放在那儿，想着也许有什么账目能告诉我点什么；什么都受得了，唯独受不了不知情。那像一种——一种癱狂。哦，比癱狂更糟！书桌左手最上层的抽屉锁着。”

她又停了片刻，好奇而专注地俯视下方一张张仰起的脸，正如他们专注地仰望她；随即她不耐烦地一甩头，继续说：“可那不要紧，我知道钥匙在哪儿。右手最上层的抽屉归我自己用，放家用账目、单据和零碎银币；我一直锁着它，因为帕特每次开完董事会带金币回家，我们都搁在那儿。我们攒哪攒，攒够了就给家里添一件礼物，添件漂亮的东西，还有——不，这些都不要紧。我们把那抽屉叫作‘银行’，帕特告诉过我钥匙搁哪儿，好让我随时能打开。”

“钥匙他搁在哪儿？”

===Original===

“In a tobacco jar on top of the bookcase. I found it and opened the drawer, and there were the papers, quite a thick packet of them, pushed way back in the drawer. They were bonds﻿—eighty-five thousand Liberty, fifteen thousand municipal. I counted them twice to make sure.” For the first time since she had mounted the stand she turned her dark and shining eyes on the perturbed Lambert. “You were very anxious to know whether anyone but Pat had seen that money, weren’t you? Well, I saw it. And I was just as sure that Pat had taken it out of our safe-deposit box in order to run away with Mimi Bellamy as I was that I was standing there counting it﻿—just as sure as that. I put it back and locked the drawer and dropped the key back into the tobacco jar and went to the flower room to telephone to Stephen Bellamy. The clock in the hall said five minutes past eight. I hadn’t been in the study for more than ten minutes.” Once more she lifted her hands to that bright hair. “Do you want me to repeat the telephone conversation?”

“Was it substantially the same as Miss Page gave it?”

“Exactly the same, word for word.”

“Then I hardly think that that will be necessary. Just tell us what you did after you finished telephoning.”

“I went to the foot of the nursery stairs and called up to ask Pat if he had absolutely decided to go to the poker game. He called back yes, and asked if he couldn’t drop me at the Conroys’. I told him that I’d rather walk. I got that flannel coat out of the closet and started off for the gate at the back of the house that led to the back road. I was almost running.”

“Had you planned any course of action?”

===Chinese===

“在书柜顶上的一只烟草罐里。我找到钥匙，打开抽屉，那些文件就在那儿，厚厚的一沓，塞在抽屉最里头。是债券——八万五自由公债，一万五市政公债。我数了两遍，确凿无疑。”自登上证人席以来，她头一回把那双深色的、发亮的眼睛转向心乱如麻的兰伯特。“您方才不是十万火急地想知道，帕特之外还有没人见过那笔钱吗？喏，我见过。而且我当时就笃定，帕特把它从我们的保管箱里取出来，是为了跟米米·贝拉米私奔——笃定得就像我笃定自己正站在那儿数它一样——就那么笃定。我把债券放回去，锁上抽屉，把钥匙丢回烟草罐，便去花房给斯蒂芬·贝拉米打电话。门厅的钟指着八点五分。我在书房里前后没超过十分钟。”她又抬手拢了拢那头亮发。“电话里的谈话，要我再复述一遍吗？”

“跟佩奇小姐复述的大体相同？”

“一字不差，完全相同。”

“那我看就不必了。只讲讲您打完电话后做了什么。”

===Original===

“No, I hadn’t any definite plan, but I knew that I had to get to Stephen and make him stop Mimi, and that every minute was precious. Just as I got to the gate, I noticed that a wind had sprung up﻿—quite a cold wind﻿—and I remembered that Mother Ives had told me at dinner that Polly’s ear had been hurting her, and that she slept right by the window where that wind would blow on her, so I turned back to the house to tell Miss Page to be sure to put a screen around the head of her crib. I saw Mother Ives at the far end of the rose garden, but I thought that it would take as long to call her and explain as it would to do it myself. So I ran on to the house, and I was halfway up the nursery stairs before I heard Pat’s voice. I thought he was talking to the babies, and I hurried up the last few steps. I was almost at the nursery door when I heard another voice﻿—Kathleen Page’s. It wasn’t coming from the nursery; it was coming from her room. She was saying, ‘Don’t let her send me away from you﻿—don’t, don’t! All I want﻿—’ ”

“Your Honour﻿—”

Farr’s warning voice was hardly swifter than Judge Carver’s: “I am afraid that you cannot tell us what you heard, Mrs. Ives.”

“I cannot tell you what I heard Kathleen Page saying?”

The wonder in the clear, incredulous voice penetrated the farthest corner of the courtroom.

“No. Simply confine yourself to what you did.”

“Did? I did nothing whatever. I could no more have moved a step nearer to the door than if I had been nailed to the floor. She was crying dreadfully, in horrid little pants and gasps. It was absolutely sickening. Pat said, ‘Keep quiet, you little lunatic. Do you want﻿—’ ”

“Mrs. Ives, the Court has already warned you that you are not able to tell us what was said.”

“Why am I not able to tell you what was said? I told you what we said downstairs.”

Judge Carver leaned toward her, his black sleeves flowing majestically over the edge of the rail. “No objection was raised as to that conversation. Mr. Farr objects to this and the Court sustains him. For your own sake, the Court requests you to conform promptly to its rulings.”

For a moment the two pairs of dark eyes met in an exchange of glances more eloquent than words; a look of grave warning and one of fearless rebellion.

“I do not understand your rules. What am I permitted to tell of the things that I am asked to explain?”

===Chinese===

“我走到育儿室楼梯脚下，朝上喊帕特，问他是不是铁了心要去打牌。他应声说是，又问要不要捎我去康罗伊家。我说我宁愿走路。我从衣橱里取出那件法兰绒外套，朝宅子后头通后路的那扇后门走去。我几乎是在跑。”

“您筹划好行动方案了吗？”

“没有，没什么明确的计划，可我知道必须找到斯蒂芬，让他拦住米米，而且每一分钟都金贵。刚走到后门，我发觉起了风——风很冷——想起晚饭时艾夫斯妈妈说过，波莉的耳朵一直疼，而她睡觉的床正挨着窗，风会直吹到她。我便折回宅子，想嘱咐佩奇小姐务必在小床头围一道屏风。我看见艾夫斯妈妈在玫瑰园远头，可我想，喊住她、再解释清楚，跟我自己去办花的时间差不多。于是我径直跑回宅子，还没上完育儿室楼梯的一半，就听见了帕特的声音。我以为他在跟孩子们说话，便紧走几步。快到育儿室门口时，我听见了另一个声音——凯瑟琳·佩奇的。那声音不是从育儿室来的，是从她房间来的。她正说：‘别让她把我从您身边赶走——别，别！我只要——’

“庭上——”

法尔的警示之声几乎不比卡弗法官更快：“恐怕您不能向我们转述您听到的话，艾夫斯太太。”

“我不能告诉你们我听见凯瑟琳·佩奇说了什么？”

那清亮嗓音里的惊愕难以置信，直透法庭最深远的角落。

“不能。请只限于您做了什么。”

“做了什么？我什么也没做。哪怕钉在地上，我也一步都挪不近那扇门。她哭得凶，一声声抽噎倒气，难听极了，简直叫人恶心。帕特说：‘安静，小疯子。你想——’

“艾夫斯太太，本庭已警告过您，说过的话您不能转述。”

“为什么说过的话我不能转述？我在楼下说的话，我不都告诉你们了吗。”

卡弗法官朝她俯过身来，黑袍的两袖雍容地漫过栏杆边沿。“对那段谈话，无人提出异议。对这一段，法尔先生有异议，本庭予以支持。为您自己着想，本庭请求您即刻遵从裁定。”

有那么一瞬，两双深色的眼睛相遇，交换的眼色比言语更雄辩：一方是庄重的警告，一方是无畏的叛逆。

“我不懂你们的规矩。我被要求解释的事情，到底允许我讲些什么？”

===Original===

“Simply tell us what you did after you heard the voices in the room.”

“Very well; I will try again. I stood there for a moment, staring at the door to the day nursery. The key was on the outside so that the babies couldn’t lock themselves in. I don’t remember moving, but I must have moved, because suddenly I had the doorknob in my hand. I jerked it toward me and slammed the door so hard that it nearly threw me off my feet. The key﻿—”

“Yes, yes,” cut in Lambert, his face suffused with a sudden and terrifying premonition. “We needn’t go too much into all these details, you know. We want to stick to our story as closely as possible. You didn’t say anything, did you?”

“No.”

“Just went on downstairs to meet Stephen Bellamy, didn’t you?”

“No.”

“You did not?” Mr. Lambert’s blank query was enough to wring commiseration from a stone. Sue Ives did not look particularly merciful, however. She had turned in her chair so that she faced her devoted adversary squarely. She leaned forward a little now, her lovely mouth schooled to disdain, her eyes under their level brows bright with anger.

“No, not then. I was telling you what I did. I turned the key in the lock and put it in my pocket. You didn’t want me to say that, did you, Uncle Dudley? You wanted everyone to believe that it was Pat who murdered Mimi, didn’t you?”

“Mrs. Ives﻿—Mrs. Ives﻿—”

“Silence! Silence!”

“Mrs. Ives!”

Over the outraged clamour of the law, her voice rose, clear and triumphant: “He didn’t murder her, because he was locked in those rooms until quarter to eleven that night, and I had the key in my pocket. Now, you can all strike that out of the record!”

“Mrs. Ives!” Over the last crash of the gavel, Judge Carver’s voice was shaken with something deeper than anger. “Mrs. Ives, if you are not immediately silent, the Court will be obliged to have you removed.”

“Removed?” She was on her feet in an instant, poised and light. “You wish me to go?”

“I wish you to get yourself in hand immediately. You are doing yourself untold injury by pursuing this line of conduct. The rules that you are refusing to obey were made largely for your own protection.”

“I don’t want to be protected. I want to tell the truth. Apparently no one wants to hear it.”

“On the contrary, you are permitted to take the stand for that express purpose.”

===Chinese===

“只讲您听见屋里人声之后做了什么。”

“好吧；我再试试。我在那儿站了片刻，盯着日间育儿室的门。钥匙挂在外面，免得孩子们把自己反锁进去。我不记得自己动过，可我一定动过，因为突然间门把手已在我手里。我朝自己一拽，把门摔得那么重，差点把我带个趔趄。钥匙——”

“是，是，”兰伯特打断她，脸上漫过一阵突然的、可怕的不祥预感。“这些细节我们不必钻得太深，您知道。我们要尽量贴着正题走。您什么也没说，对吧？”

“没说。”

“就径直下楼去见斯蒂芬·贝拉米了，对吧？”

“不。”

“您没有？”兰伯特先生那茫然的一问，足以从石头里榨出怜悯。可苏·艾夫斯看上去并不特别心慈。她已在椅子里转过身，与这位忠心耿耿的对手正面相对。此刻她微微前倾，那张可爱的嘴训练有素地瞣着轻蔑，平直眉毛下的眼睛亮着怒火。

“不，那时没有。我在讲我做了什么。我在锁里转了两圈钥匙，把它放进了口袋。您不想让我说这个，对吧，达德利大叔？您想让所有人都相信，杀米米的是帕特，对吧？”

“艾夫斯太太——艾夫斯太太——”

“肃静！肃静！”

“艾夫斯太太！”

越过法律界愤慨的喧嚷，她的嗓音拔地而起，清亮而得胜：“他没有杀她，因为那晚直到十点三刻，他都被锁在那几间屋里，而钥匙在我口袋里。现在，你们大可以把这条从记录里划掉！”

“艾夫斯太太！”压过法槌最后一声轰响，卡弗法官的声音震颤着，那已比愤怒更深。“艾夫斯太太，您若不立即安静，本庭将不得不命人将您带出。”

“带出？”她立刻站起身，轻盈而镇定。“您要我走？”

“我要您立即管住自己。您这样一意孤行，正在给自己造成难以估量的伤害。您拒绝遵守的这些规则，多半正是为您自己的保护而设。”

“我不要被保护。我要讲真话。可显然没一个人想听。”

“恰恰相反，准许您登上证人席，为的正是这个目的。”

===Original===

“For that purpose? To tell the truth?” The scorn in her voice was almost gay.

“Precisely. The limits that are imposed are for your benefit, and you are injuring your co-defendant as well as yourself by refusing to abide by them.”

“Stephen?” She paused at that, considering gravely. “I don’t want to do that, of course. Very well, I will try to go on.” She turned back to her chair, and a long sigh of incredulous relief trembled through the courtroom.

“I have forgotten where I stopped.”

“You were about to tell us what you did after you came down the nursery stairs?” Lambert’s shaken voice was hardly audible.

“Yes. Well, then﻿—then we did exactly what Stephen said we did. We drove through the back road to the River Road, where we turned to the left and went into Lakedale in order to get more gasoline. I distinctly remember the time, because we had been discussing whether the movies would be out by the time that we got back. It was twenty-five minutes past nine. After that we retraced our steps﻿—down the River Road to the back road, down to the place in the back road where I had met Stephen, past our house into the main street of the village, past the movie house, which was dark, and up the main street, which runs into the Perrytown Highway﻿—up the Perrytown Highway to the Bellamy house.

“I was absolutely sure that I saw a light over the garage, but it certainly wasn’t there a minute or so afterwards, and I decided that I might as well go in anyway. I was beyond bothering much about any minor conventions, and I thought that if Mimi were actually there, it would be a heavenly relief to put all the cards on the table and have it out with her once and forever. Mimi wasn’t there, of course; it was then that Steve called up the Conroys. When he found that she wasn’t there, I was really terrified at his condition. He was as quiet as usual, but he didn’t seem to understand anything at all that was said to him. He didn’t even bother to listen. He had some kind of a chill, and he just sat there shivering, while I reassured and argued and explained.

===Chinese===

“为这个目的？讲真话？”她声音里的轻蔑几乎欢快起来。

“正是。那些限制为的是您的利益；您拒绝遵守，既在伤害您自己，也在伤害您的共同被告。”

“斯蒂芬？”听到这里她顿住，郑重掂量。“我当然不想伤到他。好吧，我尽力往下讲。”她转回椅子里，满庭响起一声长长的、难以置信的宽慰之叹。

“我忘了讲到哪儿了。”

“您正要告诉我们，下了育儿室楼梯之后您做了什么？”兰伯特发颤的声音低得几乎听不见。

“是。那么，后来——后来我们做的，跟斯蒂芬说的分毫不差。我们驱车穿过后路到河路，左转进了雷克代尔，为的是再加油。时间我记得清清楚楚，因为我们一直在讨论，等我们回去电影散场了没有。是九点二十五分。之后我们原路折返——沿河路回后路，回到后路上我遇见斯蒂芬的地方，经过我们家进村主街，经过黑着灯的电影院，再沿主街而上——主街通到佩里敦公路——沿佩里敦公路直到贝拉米家。

“我绝对肯定看见车库上方有一盏灯，可一两分钟后那里确实什么也没有了，我想索性进去吧。什么小节小规，我已顾不得许多；而且我想，米米若真在那儿，把牌全摊到桌面上，跟她一次性彻底了断，倒是天大的解脱。米米当然不在；就是那时，斯蒂夫给康罗伊家打了电话。发现她不在之后，他的状态真把我吓坏了。他还是一如既往地安静，可别人对他说什么都好像全然进不去。他连听都懒得听。他发着某种寒战，就那么坐在那儿抖，而我在一旁宽慰、争辩、解释。

===Original===

“I could have saved my breath. He didn’t even hear me. He did finally rouse himself to telephone the police and the hospital; the rest of the time he just sat there staring and shivering. He wanted me to call up Pat and the Dallases, and of course I knew that that wouldn’t do any good﻿—Pat was locked up two stories away from a telephone. Finally I asked, ‘Did you see what direction she was going in when she left?’ He shook his head. I said, ‘But she told you that she was going toward the Conroys’?’ He nodded. I said, ‘Well, maybe she turned her ankle and fainted somewhere along the side of the road﻿—she always wears such dreadfully high heels. We might take the car and turn the headlights along the edge of the road and see if we can get any trace of her. Come on!’

“I knew that that was perfect nonsense, but I was desperate, and I thought that there was just a chance that it might rouse him. It did. It was exactly as though you’d put a galvanic shock through him. He jerked out of his chair. He was out in the hall without even waiting to look back at me, and I had to run to get to the car before he started it.

“We got off with such a jerk that it nearly threw me out of the car, and I was really afraid that he was going to dash us against one of the gateposts. I said, ‘If we’re going to find Mimi, Steve, we must go slowly, mustn’t we? We must look carefully.’ He said, ‘That’s right!’ And after that we literally crept, all the way to the Conroys’.”

“How far was that?”

“Oh, not far﻿—not half a mile﻿—just a little way. It wasn’t until after we got past their entrance that we decided that﻿—” She paused for a moment, her eyes dilated strangely in her small pale face; then she wrung her hands together more closely as though in that hard contact she found comfort, and continued steadily in her low voice. “We decided that we might as well go on.”

Lambert, paler than she, said just as steadily, “Might as well go on where, Mrs. Ives?”

“Go on to the gardener’s cottage at Orchards,” said Susan Ives.

===Chinese===

“我真是白费口舌。他连听都没听见。最后他总算振作起来，给警察和医院打了电话；其余时间就那么坐着，呆望、发抖。他要我给帕特和达拉斯家打电话，我当然知道那没用——帕特被锁在离电话隔两层楼的楼上。最后我问：‘她走的时候，您看见她朝哪个方向去了吗？’他摇头。我说：‘可她告诉过您，她往康罗伊家那边去了呀？’他点头。我说：‘那，会不会是她崴了脚，晕倒在路边什么地方了——她老是穿那种高得吓人的高跟鞋。咱们何不开车，用车灯沿路边照过去，看看能不能找到她的踪迹。走！’

“我知道这纯属胡说，可我已走投无路，心想兴许有一线希望能把他唤醒。还真唤醒了。那情形就像给他通了电。他从椅子里猛地弹起来，连回头看我一眼都等不得，人已到了门厅；我只得一路跑，才赶在他发动汽车前上了车。

“车子起步一蹿，差点把我甩出车外；我真怕他会把我们撞在大门柱上。我说：‘要找米米，斯蒂夫，咱们就得慢慢开，对不对？得仔细看。’他说：‘对！’打那以后，我们简直是爬着去的，一路爬到康罗伊家。”

“有多远？”

“哦，不远——不到半英里——就一小段路。是在过了他们家入口之后，我们才决定——”她停了片刻，小脸苍白，眼睛怪异地睁大；随即她把双手攥得更紧，仿佛那硬实的相握能给她安慰，又用低沉的嗓音平平稳稳地说下去。“我们决定，索性继续往前走。”

兰伯特的脸色比她还白，同样平稳地说：“索性继续往前，去哪儿，艾夫斯太太？”

“索性继续往前，去奥查兹的园丁小屋。”苏珊·艾夫斯说。

===Original===

In the gray light of the courtroom, the faces of the occupants looked gray, too﻿—sharpened, fearful, full of an ominous unease. More than one of them glanced swiftly over a hunched shoulder at the blue-coated guardians of the door, and then back again, with somewhat pinched and rueful countenance, at the slight occupant of the witness box. The figure sat so quietly there in the gathering shadows; to many who watched it seemed that there slanted across her lifted face another shadow still﻿—the shadow of the block, of the gallows, of the chair.﻿ ﻿…

“Is she confessing?” asked the redheaded girl in a small colourless voice.

“Wait!” said the reporter. “God knows what she’s doing.”

Judge Carver leaned suddenly toward Lambert.

“Mr. Lambert, it is already considerably past four. Is this testimony likely to continue for some time?”

“For some time, Your Honour.”

“In that case,” said Judge Carver gravely, “the Court considers it advisable to adjourn until ten tomorrow. Court is dismissed.”

The small figure moved lightly down from the witness stand into the deeper shadows﻿—deeper still﻿—she was gone. The sixth day of the Bellamy trial was over.

===Chinese===

法庭灰蒙蒙的光线里，满庭人的脸也显出灰色——绷紧了，怯惧着，盛满不祥的不安。不止一人从缩起的肩膀上飞快瞥一眼门口蓝制服的守卫，又扭回来看证人席里那纤瘦的人影，神情多少有些紧巴、懊丧。那身影在渐浓的阴影里坐得那么安静；在许多注视者看来，另一道影子似乎正斜掠过她仰起的脸——断头台的影子，绞架的影子，电椅的影子。……

“她这是在招供吗？”红头发姑娘用小小的、没有血色的声音问。

“等着！”记者说。“天知道她在干什么。”

卡弗法官忽然朝兰伯特俯过身去。

“兰伯特先生，已过四点不少了。这份证词还要持续一阵吗？”

“还要一阵，庭上。”

“那样的话，”卡弗法官庄重地说，“本庭认为宜休庭至明晨十点。退庭。”

那纤小的身影轻盈地走下证人席，没入更深的阴影——更深——她不见了。贝拉米案审判的第六天结束了。

## VII / 七
===Original===

The reporter cast an anxious eye at the redheaded girl.

“You’ve been crying,” he said accusingly.

The redheaded girl looked unrepentant.

“Of all the little idiots! What’s Sue Ives to you?”

“Never mind,” said the redheaded girl with dignity. “I can cry if I want to. I can cry all night if I want to. Keep quiet. Here she is!”

---

“Mrs. Ives, what made you decide to go on to the cottage?” Lambert’s voice was very gentle.

“I think that it was Stephen’s idea, but I’m not absolutely sure. I was at my wit’s end by this time, you see. But I believe that it was Steve who suggested that maybe she had been taken ill or perhaps even fallen asleep at the cottage. I remember agreeing that it was stupid of us not to have thought of that before. At any rate, we both agreed to go on to the cottage.”

She stopped again and sat for a moment locking and unlocking her fingers, her eyes fixed on something far beyond the courtroom door.

“What time did you arrive at the cottage?”

“At about quarter past ten, I believe﻿—twenty minutes past perhaps. It isn’t more than a five-minute drive. We drove the car up through the lodge gates and then turned off the little dirt road to the cottage. We drove it right up to the front steps, and then I said, ‘It’s no good; there’s no light in the place. She isn’t here.’ Steve said, ‘Maybe she left a note saying where she was going,’ and I said, ‘That’s perfectly possible. Let’s go in and see.’ He helped me out, and just as we got to the door, I said, ‘Well, we’ll never know. The place will be locked, of course.’ Steve had his hand on the doorknob, and he pushed it a little. He said, ‘No, it’s open. That’s queer.’ I said, ‘Probably she thought that he might come later.’ And he opened the door and we went in.”

She sat staring with that curious, intent rigidity at that far-off spot beyond the other closed door, and the courtroom followed her glance with uneasy eyes.

“And then?”

===Chinese===

记者朝红头发姑娘投去焦急的一瞥。

“你哭过了，”他控诉似的说。

红头发姑娘一脸毫无悔意。

“天底下头一号小傻瓜！苏·艾夫斯与你何干？”

“不劳你管，”红头发姑娘端着架子说。“我想哭就能哭。想哭一整夜也行。安静。她来了！”

---

“艾夫斯太太，是什么让您决定继续去小屋？”兰伯特的嗓音极轻。

“我想那是斯蒂芬的主意，不过我没有绝对把握。您知道，那时我已是山穷水尽。可我相信是斯蒂夫提议，说不定她病倒在小屋里，兴许还睡着了。我记得我还附和说，咱们早先竟没想到这一层，真够蠢的。总之，我们一致同意继续去小屋。”

她又停了下来，坐了片刻，手指扣紧又松开，眼睛盯着法庭门外很远很远的什么东西。

“你们几点到的？”，我想十点一刻上下——兴许二十分。开车不过五分钟。我们把车从门房大门开进去，拐上通小屋的那条土路，一直开到前台阶下。我说：‘没用的；屋里没有灯。她不在。’斯蒂夫说：‘说不定她留了条子，写明去哪儿了。’我说：‘很有可能。进去看看。’他扶我下车，刚到门口，我说：‘算了，永远不会知道了。门自然锁着。’斯蒂夫的手已搭上门钮，轻轻一推。他说：‘没锁，开着。怪事。’我说：‘多半是她想着他兴许晚些来。’他便推开门，我们走了进去。”

她端坐着，带着那种奇异的、专注的僵直，盯着另一扇关着的门外的那个远点；整个法庭怀着不安的眼睛，跟着她的目光望去。

“然后呢？”

===Original===

“Yes. And then when we got in there wasn’t any light, of course. Steve asked, ‘Do you know where the switch is?’ And I told him, ‘There isn’t any switch. Douglas has always been talking about putting electricity in these cottages, but he never has.’ Steve said, ‘Well, there must be a light somewhere,’ and I said, ‘Oh, of course there is. There always used to be an old brass lamp here in the corner by the front door﻿—let’s see.’ It was right there on the same table. There were matches there, too, and I struck one of them and lit it. Steve had stepped by me into the room; he was standing by the door, and he stood aside to let me pass. There was a little breeze from the open door, and I had put up one hand to shield the light and keep it from flickering. I was looking at the piano, because I’d never remembered seeing a piano there before. I was halfway across the room before I﻿—before I﻿—” The voice shuddered slowly away to silence.

After a long pause, Lambert asked, “Before you did what, Mrs. Ives?”

She gave a convulsive start, as though someone had let fall a heavy hand across the nightmare. “Before I﻿—saw her.”

The voice was hardly a whisper, but there was no one in the room beyond the reach of its stilled horror.

“It was Mrs. Bellamy that you saw?”

“Yes, I﻿—” She swallowed﻿—tried to speak﻿—swallowed again, and lifted a hand to her throat. “I’m sorry. Might I have a glass of water? Is that all right?”

In all that room no one stirred save the clerk of the Court, who poured a glass of water with careful gravity and handed it up to her over the edge of the box. She drank it slowly, as though she found in this brief respite life itself. When she had finished it, she put it down gently and said, “Thank you,” in a voice once more clear and steady.

“You were telling us that you saw Mrs. Bellamy.”

===Chinese===

“是。进去之后，屋里当然没有灯。斯蒂夫问：‘开关在哪儿你知道吗？’我告诉他：‘没有开关。道格拉斯一直念叨着给这些小屋拉电，可始终没拉。’斯蒂夫说：‘那总该有个灯在什么地方。’我说：‘哦，当然有。前门旁边这个墙角，从前老摆着一盏黄铜灯——我想想。’它就在那张老桌子上。桌上还有火柴，我划了一根，点着了它。斯蒂夫已跨到我前头进了屋；他站在门边，侧身让我过去。敞开的门口透进一点风，我抬起一只手护着灯火，不让它摇。我望着那架钢琴，因为我从不记得那里有过钢琴。我走到屋子半中间，才——才——”那嗓音战栗着，慢慢淡成了寂静。

长长的停顿之后，兰伯特问：“才怎么，艾夫斯太太？”

她痉挛似的抖了一下，仿佛有谁在噩夢上头沉沉落下一只手。“才——看见了她。”

那嗓音比耳语高不了多少，可那凝固的恐怖，屋子里没有一个人躲得开。

“您看见的，是贝拉米太太？”

“是，我——”她咽了一口——想说话——又咽了一口，抬手按住喉咙。“对不起。可以给我一杯水吗？可以吗？”

满屋没有一个人动，只有法庭书记官，郑重其事地倒了一杯水，从围栏边上递上去。她慢慢喝了，仿佛在这短短的喘息里喝到了生命本身。喝完，她轻轻放下杯子，说声“谢谢”，嗓音又清又稳了。

“您方才讲到，您看见了贝拉米太太。”

===Original===

“Yes.﻿ ﻿… I must have dropped the lamp immediately; all I remember was that we were standing there in the dark. I heard Stephen say, ‘Don’t move. Where are the matches?’ He needn’t have told me not to move. If I could have escaped death itself by stepping aside one inch I could not have moved that inch. I said, ‘I have them here﻿—in my pocket.’ He said, ‘Strike one.’ I tried three times. The third time it lit, and he went by me and knelt down beside her. He touched her wrist and said, ‘Mimi, did it hurt? Did it hurt, darling?’ The match went out and I started to strike another. He said, ‘Never mind. She’s dead.’ I said, ‘I know it. Dead people can’t close their eyes, can they?’ He said, ‘I have closed them. She’s been murdered. I got you into this, Sue, and I’ll get you out of it. Where are you?’ I tried to say, ‘Here,’ but I couldn’t. And then I thought that I heard something move﻿—outside﻿—in the bushes﻿—and I screamed.

“I’d never done that before in my life. It didn’t sound like me at all. It sounded like someone quite different. Steve whispered, ‘For God’s sake, be still.’ I said, ‘I heard someone moving.’ He said ‘It was I, coming toward you. Give me your hand.’ His was so cold on my wrist that it was horrible.

“I put my hand over my mouth to keep from screaming again, and he pulled me through the hall and on to the porch. I said, ‘Steve, we can’t leave her there like that﻿—we can’t.’ He said, ‘She doesn’t need us anymore. Get in the car.’ I pulled back, and he said, ‘Listen to me, Sue. It doesn’t make any difference how innocent we are, if it is ever known that we were in that room this evening, we’ll never be able to make one human being in God’s world believe that we aren’t guilty﻿—and we’ll have to make twelve of them believe. I’ve got to get you home. Get into the car.’ So I got in, and he drove me home.”

===Chinese===

“是。……灯想必立刻就脱了手；我只记得我们站在黑暗里。我听见斯蒂芬说：‘别动。火柴在哪儿？’他用不着叫我别动。哪怕往旁边挪一寸就能逃开死亡，那一寸我也挪不动。我说：‘在我这儿——兜里。’他说：‘划一根。’我划了三回。第三回着了，他走过我身边，跪到她旁边。他碰了碰她的手腕，说：‘米米，疼吗？疼不疼，亲爱的？’火柴灭了，我又去划。他说：‘不用了。她死了。’我说：‘我知道。死人闭不上眼睛，对不对？’他说：‘我替她闭上了。她是被人杀的。是我把你拖进来的，苏，我也会把你摘出去。你在哪儿？’我想答‘在这儿’，说不出。跟着我好像听见什么动了一下——外头——树丛里——我叫了出来。

“我这辈子从没那样叫过。那声音一点也不像我。像另外一个人。斯蒂夫低声说：‘看在上帝分上，别出声。’我说：‘我听见有人动。’他说：‘是我，朝你走过来。把手给我。’他的手搭上我腕子，冷得吓人。

“我捫住嘴，免得再叫，他拉着我穿过门厅，走上门廊。我说：‘斯蒂夫，我们不能把她那样撂下——不能。’他说：‘她不再需要我们了。上车。’我往后缩，他说：‘听我说，苏。咱们多清白都没用；只要叫人知道咱今晚进过那间屋，天底下便再没有一个人肯信咱们无罪——而咱们得叫十二个人信。我必须送你回家。上车。’我上了车，他送我回了家。”

===Original===

She was silent, and the courtroom was silent too. To the redheaded girl, it seemed as though for a space everyone had foregone even the habit of breath and held it suspended until that voice should finish its dreadful tale. She could see Patrick Ives in his corner by the window. A long time ago he had buried his black head in his hands, and he did not lift it now. His mother had placed one small gloved hand on his knee. It rested there lightly, but she was not looking at him; her eyes had never wavered from Sue Ives’s white face. Long ago the winter roses had faded in her own, but it was as gravely and graciously composed as on that first day.

“Did you drive straight home, Mrs. Ives?”

“Straight home. Stephen spoke two or three times; I don’t remember saying anything at all. He told me to say that we’d driven over to Lakedale, and then he said that everything would be all right, because no one would know that Elliot had spoken to me, and no one could possibly know that we had gone to the cottage. I remember nodding, and then we were at our gate. Stephen said, ‘You might as well give me that signal that we decided on before to let me know whether Pat’s there; will you, Sue?’ I said, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘You might ask him whether he heard from her this evening.’ I said, ‘Steve, it isn’t us that this is happening to, is it? It isn’t us﻿—not Pat and you and I and Mimi?’ He said, ‘Yes, it’s us. I’ll wait right here. Hurry, will you?’

“I went into the house. All the lights were out except one in the hall, but I went out through the study and the dining room to the pantry. It connects with the servants’ quarters, and I wanted to make sure that none of them were about, as I had to go up and unlock the day nursery, and I was afraid that Kathleen Page might make a scene. It was all dark and quiet; there wasn’t anyone there. I passed the ice box as I came back, and I could see the fruit through the glass door. I remembered that Pat couldn’t have taken it to Mother Ives, and I put some on a plate and went upstairs. Her door was open; she always left it open so that we could say good night if we came in before eleven.”

“Were you with her long?”

===Chinese===

她沉默着，法庭也沉默着。在红头发姑娘听来，有一阵子，人人连呼吸的老习惯都戒了，屏着它，等这嗓音讲完那个可怕的故事。她看得见帕特里克·艾夫斯在他窗边的角落里。许久以前他已把那颗黑脑袋埋进双手，此刻也没有抬起来。他母亲把一只戴手套的小手放在他膝上，轻轻掿着，却没有看他；她的眼睛从没离开过苏·艾夫斯那张苍白的脸。她自己脸上的冬玫瑰早已谢了，可那张脸依旧像第一天那样庄重而温文地镇定着。

“您直接开车回家了吗，艾夫斯太太？”

“直接回家。斯蒂芬开了两三次口；我不记得自己说过一个字。他叫我就说咱们开车去了雷克代尔；又说一切都会没事，因为没人知道埃利奥特跟我说过话，也没人可能知道咱们去过小屋。我记得我点了点头，然后就到了我家门口。斯蒂芬说：‘不如把咱们先前商定的那个信号给我，让我知道帕特在不在；好吗，苏？’我说：‘好。’他说：‘你可以问问他，今晚有没有她的消息。’我说：‘斯蒂夫，这不是发生在咱们身上的事，对不对？不是咱们——不是帕特和你、我和米米？’他说：‘是，就是咱们。我就在这儿等。快去，好吗？’

“我进了宅子。除了门厅一盏，灯都熄了；我穿过书房和饭厅走到配膳室。它通佣人住的那一侧，我必须上楼去开日间育儿室的锁，怕凯瑟琳·佩奇闹出动静，想先确认他们都不在。一路又黑又静；没有人。回来时经过冰箱，隔着玻璃门看得见水果。我想起帕特没法给艾夫斯妈妈送水果了，便装了一盘，端上楼。她的门开着；她总留着门，好让十一点前回来的人道声晚安。”

“您陪了她很久？”

===Original===

“Oh, no, only a minute. I told her that Steve and I had driven over to Lakedale instead of going to the movies, and kissed her good night. Then I went around the gallery and on up to the nursery wing. I unlocked the door and pushed it open, but I didn’t go in. Pat was sitting by the table, reading. The door to Miss Page’s room was closed. He sat there looking at me for a moment, and then he stood up and came into the hall, pulling the nursery door to behind him. He said, ‘I didn’t know that you had it in you to play an ugly trick like that, Sue.’ I said, ‘I didn’t know it either.’ I went down to the study and lit the light﻿—twice. I waited until I heard the car start, and then I went up to my room and took off my clothes and went to bed. There were several lights in the room, and I kept every one of them burning until after the sun was up. In the morning I got up and dressed and went to church, and it was just a little while after I got home that we heard that Mimi’s body had been found. And Monday evening both Stephen and I were put under arrest.”

She was silent for a moment, and then said in a small, exhausted voice, “That’s all. Must I wait?”

Lambert said gravely and gently, “I’m afraid so. When was the first time that you told this story, Mrs. Ives?”

“Night before last﻿—to you﻿—after they found my fingerprint, you know.”

“It is the full and entire account of how you spent the evening of the nineteenth of June, 1926?”

“Yes.”

“To the best of your knowledge, you have omitted nothing?”

“Nothing.”

“Thank you; that will be all. Cross-examine.”

Mr. Farr advanced leisurely toward the witness box and stood staring thoughtfully for a long moment at its pale occupant. Under those speculative eyes, the sagging shoulders straightened, the chin lifted.

“You were perfectly familiar with the gardener’s cottage, were you not, Mrs. Ives?”

“Perfectly.”

“You remembered even where the lamp stood in the hall?”

“Yes. I used to go there often as a child.”

“Nothing had been changed since then?”

“I don’t know. I was only there for a few seconds.”

“Not long enough to notice a change of any kind whatever?”

“There was the piano; I remember that.”

She sat very straight, watching him with those wide, bright eyes as though he were some strange and dangerous beast.

“Were you familiar with the back entrance from the River Road﻿—to the Thorne estate, Mrs. Ives?”

===Chinese===

“哦，不，只一分钟。我告诉她，我和斯蒂夫没看电影，开车去了雷克代尔，吻了她道晚安。然后我绕过走廊，上育儿室那翼去。我开了锁，推开门，没有进去。帕特坐在桌边看书。佩奇小姐房间的门关着。他坐在那儿看了我片刻，随即站起来走到门厅里，随手把育儿室的门带上。他说：‘没想到你身上还有这么一手阴招，苏。’我说：‘我也没想到。’我下楼进了书房，把灯开了——两下。我等着听见汽车发动，才回自己房间，脱衣上床。屋里有好几盏灯，我把它们一盏不落全点到太阳升起之后。早晨我起床穿衣，去了教堂；回家刚一会儿，就听说米米的尸体被发现了。星期一晚上，斯蒂芬和我双双被捕。”

她静了片刻，然后用一个小小的、耗尽了的嗓音说：“完了。我必须等在这儿吗？”

兰伯特庄重而温和地说：“恐怕是的。这个故事您头一回讲，是什么时候，艾夫斯太太？”

“前天夜里——讲给你——你知道，在他们发现我的指纹之后。”

“这是一九二六年六月十九日晚间您所作所为的完整全部经过？”

“是。”

“据您所知，没有一点遗漏？”

“没有。”

“谢谢；问完了。交叉询问。”

法尔先生从容地走向证人席，对着席中那位苍白的人若有所思地端详了许久。在那双盘算的眼睛底下，那对塌陷的肩膀挺了起来，下巴抬了起来。

“您对那间花匠小屋熟得很，是吧，艾夫斯太太？”

“熟得很。”

“连门厅里灯摆在哪儿都记得？”

“记得。我小时候常去。”

“从那以后什么都没变过？”

“不知道。我那次只待了几秒钟。”

“短得来不及察觉任何变化？”

“有那架钢琴；这个我记得。”

她坐得笔直，用那双睁大的亮眼睛盯着他，仿佛他是一头陌生而危险的野兽。

“您熟悉河滨路通索恩庄园的后门吧，艾夫斯太太？”

===Original===

“Yes.”

“You could have found it at night quite easily?”

“You mean by the lights of the automobile?”

“Exactly.”

“Yes.”

“Were you aware that it was a shorter way to reach Orchards than going back by way of Rosemont?”

“Oh, yes; it was about three miles shorter.”

“Why didn’t you take it?”

“Because when we were in Lakedale we had no idea of going to the cottage. We didn’t think of it until long after we had returned to Rosemont.”

“But why didn’t you think of it before? You knew that in all probability Mrs. Bellamy was waiting for your husband at the cottage, didn’t you?”

The question was asked in tones of the gentlest consideration, but the sentinel watching from the dark eyes was suddenly alert.

“No, I didn’t know that at all. In the first place, I wasn’t sure that she had gone there; in the second place, I wasn’t sure that she had waited, even if she had gone.”

“There was no harm in making sure, was there?”

“I thought there was. My idea in seeing Stephen was to get him to talk to Mimi; I hadn’t the faintest desire to take part in the humiliating and painful scene that would have been inevitable if I had confronted her.”

“I see. Still, you were willing to confront her in her own home, weren’t you?”

“Yes.” She bit her lip in an effort to concentrate on that. “But that wouldn’t have been tracking her down and spying on her, and by then﻿—”

“ ‘Yes’ is an answer, Mrs. Ives.”

“You mean that it’s all the answer that you want?”

“Exactly.”

“You didn’t really want to know why I did it?”

Under the level irony of her glance the prosecutor’s eyes hardened. “For your own good, Mrs. Ives, I suggest that you do not attempt to bandy language with me. You were not only willing to see her in her home but not long after you went to seek her in the cottage, did you not?”

“Yes. By that time we were both desperately worried and I put my own wishes aside.”

“You wish us to understand that you went there on an errand of mercy?”

“I am not asking you to understand anything. I was simply telling you why we went.”

“Exactly. Now, when you got to the cottage, Mrs. Ives, you say there was no light?”

“There was no light.”

“But you fortunately remembered that this lamp was in the hall?”

“Fortunately?” repeated Susan Ives slowly, “I remembered that there was a lamp in the hall.”

“How long has it been since you were at Orchards?”

===Chinese===

“熟悉。”

“夜里您也找得到，不费事？”

“您是说借汽车的车灯？”

“正是。”

“找得到。”

“您知道那是去果园庄更近的路，比绕罗斯蒙特近？”

“哦，知道；大约近三英里。”

“为什么没走？”

“因为在雷克代尔时，我们压根没有去小屋的念头。回到罗斯蒙特很久之后，才想到这一层。”

“可为什么没早想到？您知道十有八九贝拉米太太正在小屋里等您丈夫，不是吗？”

这个问题问得再温和体贴不过，可黑眼睛里放哨的那个卫兵驟然警觉了。

“不，我根本不知道。第一，我不确定她去了那儿；第二，就算去了，我也不确定她等了。”

“去确认一下，并无害处，对吧？”

“我看有害。我见斯蒂芬的用意，是要他去跟米米谈；我丝毫不想参与那种必然难堪痛楚的场面，倘若我当面撞上她。”

“明白了。不过，在她自己家里撞上她，您倒是愿意的，对吧？”

“是。”她咬住嘴唇，用力想着这一点。“可那不算跟踪她、窥伺她，而且到那时——”

“‘是’就够了，艾夫斯太太。”

“您是说，这就是您要的全部回答？”

“正是。”

“您并不真想知道我为什么那样做？”

在她那平平的讥诣之下，检察官的眼睛硬了起来。“为您自己好，艾夫斯太太，我劝您别跟我斗嘴。您不仅愿意在她家里见她，而且不久之后就去小屋找她了，对不对？”

“对。到那时我们两个都急疯了，我把自己的意愿搁在了一边。”

“您要我们理解为，您去那里是一趟善举？”

“我没有求你们理解什么。我只是告诉你们我们为什么去。”

“正是。那么，你们到小屋时，艾夫斯太太，您说没有灯？”

“没有灯。”

“可您有幸记得门厅里有这盏灯？”

“有幸？”苏珊·艾夫斯缓缓地重复，“我记得门厅里有灯。”

“您上一次到果园庄，是多少年前？”

===Original===

“I have not been there since my marriage﻿—not for seven years.”

“How long since you were in the cottage?”

“I’m not sure﻿—possibly a year or so before that.”

“Were you a child nine years ago?”

“A child? I was over twenty.”

“I thought you told us that it was as a child that you went to the cottage.”

“I went occasionally after I was older. I was very fond of the old gardener and his wife. They were German and very sensitive after the outbreak of the war. We all used to go down from time to time to try to cheer them up.”

“Very considerate indeed﻿—another errand of mercy. But about this lamp, now, that you remembered so providentially after nine years. You are quite sure that it wasn’t in the front parlour?”

“Absolutely sure.”

“It couldn’t have been standing on the little table that was overturned by Mimi Bellamy’s fall?”

“How could it possibly have been standing there?”

“I was asking you. You are perfectly sure that it wasn’t standing on that table, lighted, when you came in?”

“I see.” The unwavering eyes burned brighter with that clear disdain. “I didn’t quite understand. You mean am I lying, don’t you? I have told you the truth; the lamp was on the table in the hall.”

“Your Honour, I ask to have that reply stricken from the record as unresponsive.”

“It may be stricken from the record to the point where the witness says, ‘The lamp was on the table in the hall.’ ” Judge Carver stared down with stern, troubled eyes at the clear, unflinching face lifted to his. “Mrs. Ives, the Court again assures you that you do yourself no service by such replies and that they are entirely out of order. It requests that you refrain from them.”

“I will try to, Your Honour.”

“Mrs. Ives, you have told us that when you were standing in darkness you heard a sound that frightened you. Was it someone trying the door?”

“Oh, no; the door was open. It wasn’t anything as clear as that. I thought first that it was someone moving in the bushes, but it was probably simply my imagination.”

“You didn’t hear anyone whistling?”

“No.”

“You are quite sure that neither of you locked the door?”

“Absolutely. Why should we lock the door?”

“I must remind you again, Mrs. Ives, that it is I who am examining you. Now, you say that you went into the room ahead of Mr. Bellamy?”

“Yes.”

“How far were you from the body when you first saw it?”

===Original===

In the paper-white face the eyes dilated, suddenly, dreadfully. “I don’t know. Quite near﻿—three feet﻿—four feet.”

“You suspected that she was dead?”

“I knew that she was dead. Her eyes were wide open.”

“You did not go nearer to her than those three or four feet?”

“No.” She forced the word through her lips with a dreadful effort.

“You did not touch her?”

“No﻿—no.”

“Then how did the bloodstains get on your coat?”

At the sharp clang of that triumphant cry she shuddered and turned and came back to him slowly from the small, haunted room. “Bloodstains? There were no bloodstains on my coat.”

“Do you still claim that the coat that you smuggled out of your house Sunday morning was stained with grease from Mr. Bellamy’s car?”

“No﻿—no, I don’t claim that.”

“That’s prudent of you, as Sergeant Johnson has testified that there was no grease whatever on the car.”

“I meant to explain that before,” said Sue Ives simply. “Only there were so many other things that I forgot. It was kerosene from the lamp﻿—the coat was covered with it. I didn’t know how to explain it, so I thought that I had better get rid of it.”

“I see,” said the prosecutor grimly, “You’re a very resourceful young woman, aren’t you?”

“No,” said the clear, grave voice. “I don’t think that I’m particularly resourceful.”

“I differ from you.﻿ ﻿… Mrs. Ives, you didn’t intend to tell this jury that you had been in the gardener’s cottage on the night of the nineteenth of June, did you?”

“Not if I could avoid doing so without perjuring myself.”

“You decided to do so only when you were literally forced to it by information that you found was in the state’s possession?”

“It is hard for me to answer that by yes or no,” said Susan Ives. “But I suppose that the fairest answer to it is yes.”

“You had decided to withhold this vitally important information because you and Stephen Bellamy had together reached the conclusion that no twelve sane men could be found to accept the fantastic coincidence that you and he were in the room in which this murder was committed within a few minutes of this crime, and yet had nothing whatever to do with it?”

“I think that again the answer should be yes.”

“You are still of that opinion?”

“I no longer have any opinion.”

“Why should you have changed your opinion that twelve sane men could not possibly believe your story?”

纸白的脸上，眼睛驟然睁大，大得吓人。“不知道。很近——三英尺——四英尺。”

“您疑心她死了？”

“我知道她死了。她眼睛睁着。”

“您没有走近到三四英尺以内？”

“没有。”这个词是从她唇间以可怕的力气挤出来的。

“您没有碰她？”

“没——没有。”

“那您外套上的血迹是哪来的？”

那一声得胜的锿锵之下，她一颤，转过身，从那间闹鬼的小屋里慢慢回到他面前。“血迹？我的外套上没有血迹。”

“您还坚称，星期天早晨您从家里偷偷运出的那件外套，沾的是贝拉米先生车上的油污？”

“您倒识相，约翰逊巡佐已经作证，车上一点油污也没有。”

“那件事我本想先解释的，”苏·艾夫斯直统统地说。“只是别的事太多，忘了。那是灯里的煤油——外套浸透了它。我不知道怎么解释，便想还是把它处理掉为好。”

“明白了，”检察官冷冷地说。“您是位很有点子的年轻女性，对吧？”

“不，”那清亮庄重的嗓音说。“我不觉得自己特别有点子。”

“我可不敢苟同。……艾夫斯太太，您本不打算告诉这个陪审团，六月十九日夜里您到过花匠小屋，对吧？”

“只要不作伪证也能瞒住，就不打算说。”

“直到发现州方掌握着那情报、被逼到墙角，您才决定说？”

“这个问题很难只答是或不是，”苏珊·艾夫斯说。“不过我想最公道的回答是：是。”

“您决定隐瞒这攸关重要的情报，是因为您和斯蒂芬·贝拉米得出了同一个结论：找不出十二个心智健全的人，会接受这个离奇的巧合——你们二人在案发后几分钟置身于凶案现场，却与之毫无干系？”

“我想这一问的答案也该是：是。”

“您现在仍持这个看法？”

“我如今没有任何看法了。”

“十二个心智健全的人绝不可能相信你们的故事——这个看法为什么会变？”

===Original===

“I do not know whether they will believe me or not,” said Sue Ives, her eyes, fearless and unswerving, on the twelve stolid, inscrutable countenances raised to hers. “You see, I don’t know how true truth sounds.”

“I should imagine not,” said the prosecutor, his voice cruelly smooth. “No further questions.”

And at that Parthian shot the white lips in the white face before him curved suddenly and amazingly into the lovely irony of a smile, a last salute over the drawn swords before they were sheathed.

“That will be all,” said Lambert’s voice gently. “You may stand down.”

For a moment she did not move, but sat staring down with dark eyes to which the smile had not quite reached, at the twelve enigmatic countenances before her﻿—at the slack, careless young one on the far end; the grim elderly one next to it; the small, deep-set eyes above the heavy jowls of that flushed one in the centre; the sleek attentive pallor of the one next to the door. She opened her lips as though to speak again, closed them with a small shake of her head, swept up gloves, bag and fur with one swift gesture, and without a backward glance was gone, moving across the cluttered space between her chair and the box with that light, sure step that seemed always to move across green grass, through sunlight and a little wind. She did not even look at Stephen Bellamy, but in the little space between their chairs their hands met once and clenched in greeting and swung free.

“Your Honour,” said Lambert, in the quiet, tired voice so many leagues removed from the old boom, “in view of Mrs. Ives’s evidence, I would like to have Mr. Bellamy take the stand once more. I have only one or two questions to put to him.”

“He may take the stand,” said Judge Carver impassively.

He took it steadily, the white face of horror that he had turned from the day before schooled once more to the old courtesy and quiet.

“Mr. Bellamy, you have heard Mrs. Ives’s evidence as to the circumstance that led up to your visit to the gardener’s cottage and of the visit to the cottage itself. Is her description is accord with your own recollection?”

“In complete accord.”

“You would not change it in any particular?”

“No. It is absolutely accurate.”

“Nor add to it?”

“Yes. There is something that I believe that I should add. Mrs. Ives was not aware of the fact that I returned to the cottage again that night.”

===Chinese===

“我不知道他们信不信我，”苏·艾夫斯说，她的眼睛——无畏而不游移——望着朝她仰起的十二张木然莫测的脸。“您瞧，我不知道纯粹的实话，听起来是什么样子。”

“我想您不知道，”检察官说，嗓音残忍地圆滑。“没有别的问题了。”

这一记回马枪过后，他面前那张白脸上的白嘴唇，驟然而惊人地弯成一个可爱的讥诣微笑——收剑入鞘前，隔着出鞘的剑最后一次致意。

“问完了，”兰伯特的嗓音温和地说。“您可以下来了。”

有一会儿她没有动，坐着，用那双笑意未及抵达的黑眼睛，俯视面前十二张谜一样的脸——最边上那张松散潦草的年轻的脸；挨着它的那张严苛的老脸；正中那张红脸膛、厚下巴、小而深陷的眼睛；门边那位油亮专注的苍白先生。她张开嘴似乎还要说什么，随即抿住，轻轻摇了摇头，一个利落的动作抄起手套、提包和毛领，头也不回地走了，以那种轻盈笃定的步子穿过她的座位与证人席之间堆杂的空地——那步子仿佛永远走在青草地上，穿过阳光和一点微风。她连斯蒂芬·贝拉米看都没看，可在两把椅子间的小小空隙里，他们的手相遇了一次，紧握为礼，又荡开。

“法官阁下，”兰伯特说，用那副离昔日的嗡鸣已隔了万水千山的安静而疲倦的嗓音，“鉴于艾夫斯太太的证词，我想请贝拉米先生再上证人席。我只有一两个问题问他。”

“可以上，”卡弗法官不动声色地说。

他稳稳地登上去，昨日从他脸上扭开的那副恐怖的白脸，重新调回了旧日的谦和与安静。

“贝拉米先生，您听到了艾夫斯太太关于你们去花匠小屋的缘由和小屋之行本身的证词。她的叙述与您的记忆一致吗？”

“完全一致。”

“任何细节您都不会改动？”

“不会。它绝对准确。”

“也不会增补？”

“会。有一件事我想我应当补上。艾夫斯太太并不知道，那天夜里我又回过一次小屋。”

===Original===

If Lambert also was not aware of it, he gave no sign. “For what purpose?”

“I had no definite purpose﻿—I did not wish to leave my wife alone in the cottage.”

“At what time did you return?”

“Very shortly after I left Mrs. Ives at her home. I actually didn’t know what I was doing. I took the wrong turn in the back road and drove around for a bit before I got straightened out, but it couldn’t have been for very long.”

“How long did you stay?”

“Until it began to get light; I didn’t look at the time.”

“You did not disturb the contents of the cottage in any way?”

“No; I left everything exactly as it was.”

“Nor remove anything?”

“Nothing﻿—nothing whatever.”

“Thank you, Mr. Bellamy. That will be all, unless Mr. Farr has any questions.”

“As a matter of fact, I have one or two questions,” remarked Mr. Farr, leisurely but grim. “You, too, are highly resourceful, Mr. Bellamy, aren’t you?”

“I should hardly say that I had proved myself so.”

“Well, you can reassure yourself. That extra set of automobile tires had to be accounted for, hadn’t they?”

“I should have accounted for them in any case.”

“Should you, indeed? That’s very interesting, but hardly a responsive answer to my question. I’ll be grateful if you don’t make it necessary for me to pull you up on that again. Now, you say that you didn’t touch anything in the cottage?”

“I said that I did not disturb anything.”

“Oh, you touched something, did you?”

“Yes.”

“What?”

“I touched her hand.”

“I see. You were looking for the rings?”

“No. I didn’t think of the rings.”

“They were still there?”

“Until you asked me this minute I had not thought of them. I do not believe that they were there.”

“Mr. Bellamy, I put it to you that you returned to that cottage with the express purpose of removing those rings, the necklace, and any traces that you or Mrs. Ives may have left behind you in your previous flight?”

“You are wrong; I did not return for any of those purposes.”

“Then for what purpose?”

“Because I did not wish to leave my wife alone.”

“You consider that a plausible explanation?”

“Oh, no; simply a true one.”

“She was dead, wasn’t she?”

“She was dead.”

“You knew that?”

“Yes.”

“You knew that you couldn’t do anything for her, didn’t you?”

“I wasn’t sure.” The voice was as quiet as ever, but once more the ripple of the clenched teeth showed in the cheek. “She was afraid of the dark.”

===Chinese===

兰伯特若也不知道，他脸上毫无表示。“为什么回去？”

“我没有明确的目的——我不愿把妻子一个人留在小屋里。”

“几点回去的？”

“送艾夫斯太太到家之后不久。说实话我不知道自己在干什么。后路上拐错了弯，瞎绕了一阵才摆正方向，为时想必不长。”

“待了多久？”

“待到天开始亮；我没看时间。”

“您没有动过小屋里的任何东西？”

“没有；一切原样未动。”

“也没带走任何东西？”

“没有——什么也没有。”

“谢谢您，贝拉米先生。问完了，除非法尔先生有问题。”

“正好，我有一两个问题，”法尔先生说，从容而森然。“您也是位很有点子的先生，贝拉米先生，对吧？”

“我很难说我已经证明了这一点。”

“嗯，您可以放心了。那多出的一套车胎，总得有人交代吧？”

“无论如何我都会交代的。”

“是吗？有意思，可惜不是对我问题的回答。劳驾别再逼我揪您这个毛病。好，您说您没碰小屋里的任何东西？”

“我说的是我没有搅动任何东西。”

“哦，您碰了些什么，是吧？”

“是。”

“碰了什么？”

“我碰了她的手。”

“明白了。您在找那些戒指？”

“不。戒指我没想到。”

“戒指还在？”

“到您此刻问起，我一直没想到过它们。我想它们不在了。”

“贝拉米先生，我且这么说：您重返那间小屋，正是为了取走那些戒指、那条项链，以及你们先前逃走时可能留下的任何痕迹？”

“您错了；我回去不为任何一种那种目的。”

“那为什么？”

“因为我不愿把妻子一个人留下。”

“您认为这算个可信的解释？”

“哦，不；只是个真实的解释。”

“她已经死了，对吧？”

“她死了。”

“这您知道？”

===Original===

“Of the dark?”

“Yes; she was afraid to be alone in the dark.”

“She was dead, wasn’t she?”

“Yes﻿—yes, she was dead.”

“You ask us to believe that you spent hours in momentary danger of arrest for murder because a woman who was stone dead had been afraid of the dark when she was alive?”

“No. I don’t ask you to believe anything,” said Stephen Bellamy gently. “I was simply telling you what happened.”

“You say that you didn’t touch anything else in the cottage?”

“Nothing else.”

“How could you find your way about without a light?”

“I had a light; I took the flashlight from my car.”

“So that you could make a thorough search of the premises for anything that had been left behind?”

“We had left nothing behind.”

“But you couldn’t have been sure of that, could you? A knife, perhaps? A knife’s an easy thing to lose.”

“We had no knife.”

Mr. Farr greeted this statement with an expression of profound skepticism. “Now, before I ask you to step down, Mr. Bellamy, I want to make sure that you haven’t one final installment to add for our benefit. That’s all that you have to tell us?”

“That is all.”

“Sure?”

“Quite sure.”

“This continued story that you have been presenting to us from day to day has reached its absolutely ultimate installment?”

“I have already said that I have nothing to add to my statement.”

“And this is the same story that you were so sure that no twelve sane men in the world would believe, isn’t it?”

“Yes. It isn’t necessary to prove to me that I have been the fool of the world,” said Stephen Bellamy quietly. “I willingly admit it. My deepest regret is that my folly has involved Mrs. Ives too.”

“You have had no cause to revise your opinion as to the skepticism that your account of that night’s doings would arouse in any twelve sane men, have you?”

“Oh, yes, I have had excellent reason completely to revise it.”

The low, pleasant voice seemed to jar on the prosecutor as violently as a bomb. “And what reason, may I ask?”

“At the time that I arrived at that conclusion I had naturally had no opportunity to hear Mrs. Ives on the witness stand. Now that I have, it seems absolutely impossible to me that anyone could fail to believe her.”

“That must be extremely reassuring for you,” remarked Mr. Farr in a voice so heavily charged with irony that it came close to cracking under the strain. “That will be all, thank you, Mr. Bellamy.”

===Chinese===

“知道。”

“您也知道您救不了她了，对不对？”

“我没有把握。”嗓音还是那么平静，可腮上又一次掠过咬牙的颤动。“她怕黑。”

“怕黑？”

“是；她怕一个人待在黑暗里。”

“她已经死了，对不对？”

“是——是，她死了。”

“您要我们相信：您在随时可能因杀人被捕的险境里守了几个钟头，就因为一个死透了的女人活着的时候怕黑？”

“不。我不要求你们相信什么，”斯蒂芬·贝拉米温和地说。“我只是告诉你们发生了什么。”

“您说小屋里别的东西您没碰？”

“别的没碰。”

“没有灯，您怎么行动？”

“我有灯；我从车上取了手电。”

“好把屋里彻底搜一遍，看落下了什么吧？”

“我们什么也没落下。”

“可这点您没法拿准，对吧？比如，一把刀？刀这东西，最容易丢。”

“我们没有刀。”

法尔先生用一脸深沉的怀疑迎接这句话。“那么，请您下台之前，贝拉米先生，我要确认您没有最后一集要为我们奉上。您要说的就这些？”

“就这些。”

“拿得准？”

“拿得准。”

“您连日呈给我们的这部连载，已经到了绝对的大结局？”

“我说过了，我的陈述再无可补充。”

“而这同一个故事，您当初可是笃定天下没有十二个心智健全的人会信，是不是？”

“是。不必向我证明我是天字第一号傻瓜，”斯蒂芬·贝拉米静静地说。“我欣然承认。我最深的悔恨，是我的愚蠢连累了艾夫斯太太。”

“您对那一夜行止的叙述将在任何十二个心智健全者心中激起何等怀疑——这个看法，您没有理由修正吧？”

“哦，有，我有极好的理由彻底修正它。”

那低沉悦耳的嗓音，震得检察官如同挨了颗炸弹。“请问，什么理由？”

“我得出那个结论时，自然还没机会听到艾夫斯太太在证人席上讲话。如今听过了，在我看来，竟有人不信她，是绝对不可能的事。”

“这对您必定是极大的安慰，”法尔先生说，嗓音里讥讽浓得几乎压断了弦。“问完了，谢谢您，贝拉米先生。”

===Original===

Mr. Lambert rose slowly to his feet. “The defense rests,” he said.

The redheaded girl watched them filing out through the door at the back without comment, and without comment she accepted the cake of chocolate and the large red apple. She consumed them in the same gloomy silence, broken only by an occasional furtive sniff and the application of a minute and inadequate handkerchief.

“You promised me last night,” said the reporter accusingly, “that if I’d go home you’d stop crying and be reasonable and sensible and﻿—”

“I’m not crying,” said the redheaded girl﻿—“not so that anybody would notice anything at all if they weren’t practically spying on me. It’s simply that I’m a little tired and not exactly cheerful.”

“Oh, it’s simply that, is it? Would you like my handkerchief too?” The redheaded girl accepted it ungratefully.

“The worst thing about a murder trial,” she said, “is that it practically ruins everybody’s life. It’s absolutely horrible. They’re all going along peacefully and quietly, and the first thing they know they’re jerked out of their homes and into the witness box, and things that they thought were safe and hidden and sacred are blazoned out in letters three inches tall in every paper in the﻿ ﻿… That poor little Platz thing, and that wretched Farwell man, and poor little Mrs. Ives with her runaway husband, and Orsini with his jail sentence﻿—it isn’t decent! What have they done?”

The reporter said, “What, indeed?” in the tone of one who has not heard anything but the last three words. After a moment he inquired thoughtfully. “Have you ever thought about getting married?”

The redheaded girl felt her heart miss two beats and then race away like a wild thing. She said candidly, “Oh, often﻿—practically all the time. All nice girls do.”

“Do they?” inquired the reporter in a tone of genuine surprise. “Men don’t﻿—hardly ever.” He continued to look at her abstractedly for quite a long time before he added, “Only about once in their lives.”

He was looking at her still when the door behind the witness box opened.

---

兰伯特先生缓缓起立。“辩方举证完毕。”他说。

红头发姑娘看着他们从后门鱼贯而出，没有评论；那块巧克力和那个大红苹果，她也无言地收下了。她同样阴郁地默默吃了，只偶尔一声偷偷的抽噎、一方小而不顶用的手帕打断。

“你昨晚答应过我，”记者控诉道，“只要我回家，你就不哭了，就讲道理、懂事了——”

“我没哭，”红头发姑娘说——“不至于叫谁看出来，除非他存心监视我。我只是有点累，不太快活罢了。”

“哦，只是这样，是吗？我的手帕也要不要？”红头发姑娘不领情地接了。

“凶案审判最糟的一点，”她说，“就是它实实在在毁掉每个人的生活。可怕透了。人家本来太太平平、安安静静地过着日子，一转眼就被从家里拽出来，塞进证人席，那些他们以为稳妥、藏严、神圣的事情，用三英寸高的大字登遍每份报纸的……可怜的小普拉茨，倒霉的法韦尔，可怜的小艾夫斯太太和她出走的丈夫，还有蹲过班房的奥尔西尼——这不成体统！他们做了什么？”

记者说：“是啊，做了什么？”那口气，像是只听见了最后三个字。过了一会儿，他若有所思地问：“你考虑过结婚吗？”

红头发姑娘觉得心脏漏跳了两拍，随即像头小兽似的狂奔起来。她坦白地说：“哦，常想——差不多一直想。好姑娘都这样。”

“是吗？”记者的口气透着真心的惊讶。“男人就不——几乎从不。”他继续心不在焉地望了她好一阵，才又加一句：“一辈子只想一回。”

他还在望着她，证人席后头那扇门开了。

===Original===

“Your Honour”﻿—the lines in Mr. Lambert’s face stood out relentlessly, but his voice was fresh and strong﻿—“gentlemen of the jury, it is not my intention to take a great amount of your time, in spite of the fact that there devolves on me as solemn a task as falls to the lot of any man﻿—that of pleading with you for the precious gift of human life. I do not believe that the solemnity of that plea is enhanced by undue prolixity, by legal hairsplitting or by a confusion of issues essentially and profoundly simple. The evidence in the case has been intricate enough. I shall not presume to analyze it for you. It is your task, and yours alone, to scrutinize, weigh, and dispose of it. On the other hand, the case presents almost no legal intricacies; any that are present will be expounded to you by Judge Carver when the time comes.

“When all is said and all is done, gentlemen, it is a very simple question that you have to decide﻿—as simple as it is grave and terrible. The question is this: Do you believe the story that Stephen Bellamy and Susan Ives have told you in this courtroom? Is their story of what happened on that dreadful night a reasonable, a convincing and an honest explanation as to how they became involved in the tragic series of events that has blown through their peaceful homes like a malignant whirlwind, wrecking all their dearest hopes and their dearest realities? I believe that there can be but one answer to that question, and that not so long from now you will have given that answer, and that every heart in this courtroom will be the lighter for having heard it.

“These two have told you precisely the same story. That Stephen Bellamy did not go quite to the end with it in the first instance is a circumstance that I deplore as deeply as any one of you, but I do not believe that you will hold it against him. He did not, remember, utter one syllable that was not strictly and accurately truthful. It had been agreed between them that if it were necessary to swerve one hairbreadth from the truth, they would not swerve that hairbreadth.

===Chinese===

“法官阁下”——兰伯特先生脸上的皱纹毫不留情地凸着，可他的嗓音清新而有力——“陪审团的诸位先生，我不打算占用你们太多时间，尽管落在我肩上的，是人间最庄重的任务之一——为那无价的礼物、人的生命，向你们恳求。我不认为这个恳求的庄重，需要靠不必要的冗长、法律字眼的吹毛求疵、或把本质上极其简单的问题搅得纷乱来增益。本案的证据已经够缠夹了。我不会僭越替诸位剖析它。审视它、掂量它、处置它，是你们的任务，也只归你们。另一方面，本案几乎不含任何法律疑难；偶有一二，到时候卡弗法官自会向诸位讲解。

“千言万语说完、千事万事做完，诸位先生，你们要裁决的其实是一个极简单的问题——简单得一如它严酷而可怕。问题是这个：斯蒂芬·贝拉米和苏珊·艾夫斯在这座法庭上向你们讲的故事，你们信不信？他们讲的那一夜的经历，对他们如何被卷入这连串悲剧——那悲剧像一场恶旋风刮进他们安宁的家宅，摧毁了他们最珍贵的希望与最珍贵的实在——是不是一个合理、可信、诚实的解释？我相信这个问题只有一个答案，而且不久之后你们就会给出它，而这座法庭里的每一颗心，都将因为听见它而轻快。

“这两个人向你们讲的，是分毫不差的同一个故事。斯蒂芬·贝拉米起初没有一口气讲到底，这一节我同诸位一样深感遗憾，但我不信你们会记恨他。请记住，他没有说过一个音节不是严格而精确的实话。他们之间有约：若需要偏离实话一根头发丝，他们便不偏那一根。

===Original===

“In persuading Mrs. Ives that her only safety lay in not admitting that she had been in the cottage that night, Mr. Bellamy made a grave mistake in judgment, but it was the mistake of a chivalrous and distraught soul, literally overwhelmed at the ghastly situation into which the two of them had been so incredibly precipitated.

“As for Susan Ives, she was so shaken with horror to the very roots of her being﻿—so stunned, so confused and confounded﻿—that she was literally moving through a nightmare during the few days that preceded her arrest; and, gentlemen, in a nightmare the best of us do not think with our accustomed clarity and cogency. She did what she was told to do, and she was told that it would make my task easier if I did not know that she had been near the cottage that night. That, alas, settled it for her once and for all. She has always sought to make my tasks easier.

“Stephen Bellamy undoubtedly remembered the old precept that it takes two to tell the truth﻿—one to speak it and one to hear it. Possibly he believed that if there were two to speak it and twelve to hear it, it would be a more dangerous business. I do not agree with him. I believe that twelve attentive and intelligent listeners﻿—as you have amply proved yourselves to be﻿—make the best of all forums at which to present the truth, the whole truth and nothing but the truth. That is my belief, that was my considered advice, and it is my profound conviction that before many hours have passed I shall be justified of my belief.

===Chinese===

“贝拉米先生劝艾夫斯太太，说她唯一的生路在于不承认那晚到过小屋——这个判断他铸成了大错，可那是一个侠义而心神俱乱之魂的错误：两个人被如此匪夷所思地抛进那毛骨悚然的境地，他当真是被压垮了。

“至于苏珊·艾夫斯，她被恐怖震动到了生命的根须——那样震惊、茫然、昏乱——被捕前的几天，她当真是在一场噩梦中间走动；而诸位先生，在噩梦当中，我们最好的人也用不惯平日的清明与条理。人家叫她怎么做她就怎么做，人家告诉她：若我不知道她那晚到过小屋附近，我的差事会好办些。唉，这一句就替她永远定了案。她一贯设法让我的差事好办些。

“斯蒂芬·贝拉米无疑记得那句古训：实话说成，须得两人——一个说，一个听。或许他还以为，若要两个说、十二个听，那便更危险了。我不同意他。我相信十二位专注而聪慧的听众——诸位已经充分证明了自己正是这样的人——是呈递真相、全部真相、唯有真相的最佳法庭。这是我的信念，这是我深思后的忠告，而且我深信，不出几个钟头，我的信念就将获得昭雪。”

===Original===

“Perhaps you have guessed that my relations to Susan Ives are not the ordinary relations of counsel to client. Such, at any rate, is the case, and I do not shirk one of its implications. There is no tie of blood between us, but I am bound to her by every other tie of affection and admiration. I can say that I believe she is as dear to me as any daughter﻿—dearer, perhaps, than any daughter, because she is what most men only dream that their daughter may be. For the first time in my life I have offended her since I came to this court﻿—offended her because she believed that I was more loyal to her welfare than her wishes. But she will forgive me even for that, because she knows that I am only a stupid old man who would give every hope that he has of happiness to see hers fulfilled, and who, when he pleads for her life today, is pleading for something infinitely dearer to him than his own.

“If, later, you say to one another and to yourselves, ‘The old man is prejudiced in her favour; we must take that into account,’ I say to you, ‘And so you must﻿—and so you must﻿—well into account.’ I am prejudiced because I have known her since she was so small that she did not come to my knee; because I have watched her with unvarying wonder and devotion from the days that she used to cling to me, weeping because her black kitten had hurt its paw, or radiant because there was a new daisy in her garden; because I have watched her from those bright, joyous days to these dark and terrible ones, and never once have I found a trace of alloy in her gold. I have found united in her the traits we seek in many different forms﻿—all the gallantry and honesty of a little boy, all the gaiety and grace of a little girl, all the loyalty and courage of a man, all the tenderness and beauty of a woman. If you think I am prejudiced in her favour you will be right, gentlemen. And if that fact prejudices me in your eyes, make the most of it.

“Of Stephen Bellamy I will say only this: If I had a daughter I would ask nothing more of destiny than that such a man should seek her for his wife﻿—and you may make the most of that too.

===Chinese===

“诸位或许已经猜到，我与苏珊·艾夫斯的关系，不是寻常的律师与当事人。事实如此，其中的含义我一概不避。我们之间没有血亲，但除了血亲之外的每一种情爱与敬慕的纽带，都把我同她系在一起。我可以说，她在我心里亲如女儿——只怕比女儿还亲，因为她正是多数人只在梦里指望女儿成为的那种人。我平生头一回惹恼了她，就在踏进这座法庭之后——惹恼她，是因为她认定我对她的幸福比她的意愿更忠。可连这个她也会宽恕我，因为她知道我不过是个愚蠢的老人，情愿把自己对幸福的全部指望拱手相让，只为看见她的实现；今天我在这里为她的生命恳求时，所恳求的，于我远比自己的生命珍贵。

“倘若稍后你们彼此说、心里想：‘这老头偏袒她；这一点必须记进账里。’那我便对你们说：‘自然必须——必须——重重记进账里。’我偏袒她，因为我认识她时，她还小得够不到我的膝盖；因为我怀着始终如一的惊叹与深情注视着她，从她搂着我哭黑猫咪咪伤了爪子的日子，为她园里开了一朵新雏菊而欢喜放光的日子；因为我从那些明亮的欢快日子一路注视到这些黑暗可怕的日子，从未在她那金子里发现一星杂质。我在她身上见到了我们要在许多不同人身上分头寻觅的品质——小男孩的全部骁勇与诚实，小姑娘的全部欢快与灵秀，男人的全部忠义与胆气，女人的全部温柔与美好。诸位若认为我偏袒她，你们没有说错。这件事若使我在你们眼中也成了有偏之人，那就请尽管利用。”

“关于斯蒂芬·贝拉米，我只说一句：我若有一个女儿，我对命运别无所求，只求这样的男子来聘她为妻——这一句，也请你们尽管利用。”

===Original===

“On this subject I will not touch again, I promise. It is not part and parcel of the speech of counsel for the defense to the jury in a murder trial to touch on his feeling toward his clients. I am grateful for the indulgence of both the Court and the prosecution in permitting me to dwell on them at some length. During the course of Mrs. Ives’s examination something as to our relation was inadvertently disclosed. In any case, I should have considered it my duty to inform you of it, as well as of every other fact in this case. I have now done so.

“A few days ago I said to you that Susan Ives was rich in many things. When I said that I was not thinking of money; I was referring to things that are the treasured possessions, the precious heritage, of many a humble and modest soul. Love, peace, beauty, security, serenity, health﻿—these the least of us may have. As I have said, I am pretty close to being an old man now, and in my time I have heard much talk of class feeling and class hatred. I have even been told that it is difficult to get justice for the rich from the poor or mercy for the poor from the rich. I believe both these statements to be equally vile and baseless slanders.

“In this great country of which you and I are proud and privileged citizens, we are all rich﻿—rich in opportunity and in liberty﻿—and there is no room in our hearts for grudging envy, for warped malice. We do not say, ‘This woman is rich; she has breeding; she has intelligence and culture and position, therefore she is guilty.’ We do not say, ‘This man is a graduate of one of our greatest universities. Five generations of his ancestors have owned land in this country, and have lived on it honourably and decently, gentlefolk of repute and power in their communities; he is the possessor of a distinguished name and a distinguished record, therefore he is a murderer!’ We do not say that. No; you and I and the man in the street say, ‘It is impossible that two people with this life behind them and a richer and finer one before them should stoop to so low and foul a weapon as an assassin’s knife and a coward’s blow in the dark.’

===Chinese===

“这个题目我答应不再触碰。凶案审判中辩方律师对陪审团的陈词，本不该谈他对当事人的感情。我感激本庭与检察官的宽宥，容我在此流连了这许久。艾夫斯太太受审之际，关于我们关系的只言片语无意间泄露了出来。无论如何，我认为都有义务让诸位知情，一如对本案其余每一项事实。现在我已经做到了。

“几天前我对你们说，苏珊·艾夫斯在许多事情上富有。我说这话时想的不是钱；我指的是那些东西——许多谦卑朴素的灵魂珍藏的家当、宝贵的遗产。爱、安宁、美、安全、静穆、健康——这些，我们中最低微的人也可能拥有。我说过，我如今差不多是个老人了，我这一辈子听多了关于阶级情感与阶级仇怨的言论。甚至有人告诉我，富人难从穷人那里得到公道，穷人难从富人那里得到怜悯。这两句话，我认为是同样恶毒、同样无根的诽谤。

“在你我引以为荣、引以为权的这个伟大国家里，我们人人都是富人——富有机遇与自由——我们心里容不下酸溜溜的嫉妒，容不下扭曲的恶意。我们不说：‘这女人有钱；她有教养；她有才智、学识、地位，所以她有罪。’我们不说：‘这人毕业于我们最伟大的大学之一；他的先人五代在这片土地上置产，体面正派地生活在上面，是乡里有名有势的绅士；他拥有显赫的姓氏与显赫的履历，所以他是杀人犯！’我们不说这话。不；你我与街头巷尾的平常人说的是：‘有这般过去、又有这般更丰厚更美好的将来在前的两个人，绝不可能堕落到操刺客之刀、行暗夜之怯击那种卑鄙下作的家伙。’”

===Original===

“But even in the strictly material sense of wealth, Mrs. Ives is not a wealthy woman. I should like, in the simple interests of truth, to dispel the legends of a marble heart moving through marble halls that has been growing about her. She has lived for several happy years in what you have heard described to you as a farm house﻿—a simple, unpretentious place that she made lovely with bright hangings and open fires and books and prints and flowers. If you had rung her doorbell before that fatal day in June, no powdered flunky would have opened it to you. It might have been opened by Mrs. Ives herself, or by Mr. Ives’s mother, or by a little maid in a neat dark frock and a white apron. Whoever had opened it to you, you would have found within a charming and friendly simplicity that might well cause you a little legitimate envy; you would have found nothing more.

“Sue Ives had what all your wives have, I hope﻿—flowers in her garden, babies in her nursery, sunshine in her windows. With these any woman is rich, and so was she. As for Stephen Bellamy, he had no more than any good clerk or mechanic﻿—a little house, a little car, a little maid of all work to help his pretty wife. That much for the legend of pride and pomp and power and uncounted millions that has grown up about these two. In the public press this legend has flourished extravagantly; it is of little concern to you or to any of us, save in so far as the preservation of truth is the concern of every one of us.

“The story that you have heard from the lips of Mrs. Ives and Mr. Bellamy is a refutation of every charge that has been brought against them. It is a fearless, straightforward, circumstantial and coherent account of their every action on the evening of that terrible and momentous night. Granted that every witness produced by the state here in order to confound and confuse them has spoken the absolute and exact truth﻿—a somewhat extravagant claim, some of you may feel﻿—granted even that, however, still you will find not one word of their testimony that is not perfectly consistent with the explanation of their actions that evening offered you by the defendants.

===Chinese===

“可即便单论财富的物质意义，艾夫斯太太也算不得富婆。为了实话实说，我很想驱散围绕她生长起来的那个传说：大理石厅堂里一颗大理石的心。她在你们听人描述为农舍的房子里幸福地住了好些年——一座朴实无华的宅子，她用鲜亮的帘幔、敞开的炉火、书籍、版画和鲜花把它装点得可爱。在那致命的六月之日以前，你若按响她的门铃，给你开门的绝不会是扑粉的仆役。开门的可能是艾夫斯太太本人，可能是艾夫斯先生的母亲，可能是个穿整洁深色衣裙、系白围裙的小女仆。无论谁给你开门，你在屋里见到的那份迷人而友好的简朴，或许会叫你生出一点情有可原的艳羡；此外你什么也见不到。

“苏·艾夫斯拥有的，我希望也就是你们每位太太拥有的——园中的花，育儿室里的娃娃，窗口的阳光。有这些，任何女人都是富的，她也是。至于斯蒂芬·贝拉米，他的家当不多于任何一个好店员、好技工——一座小房子，一辆小车，一个帮他漂亮太太干活的小打杂女仆。关于在这两位身上生长起来的那个骄奢、煊赫、权势与亿万身家的传说，就说到这里。在公开报刊上，这个传说枝繁叶茂；它于你们、于我们都无足轻重，只是——保全真相，原是我们每一个人分内的事。

“你们从艾夫斯太太与贝拉米先生唇间听到的故事，是对针对他们的每一项指控的驳斥。它对他们那个可怕而重大的夜晚的每一项行动，做了一个无畏、直捷、详明而连贯的交代。就算本州为搅乱他们而传的每一位证人都讲了绝对精确的实话——诸位或许觉得，这要求未免奢——就算如此，你们在他们证词里也找不出一个字，同两位被告对当晚行止的解释有任何不相合。”

===Original===

“Not only does the state’s testimony not conflict with ours﻿—it corroborates it. The overheard telephone conversation, the knife from the study, the stained flannel coat, the visit to Stephen Bellamy’s house, the tire tracks in the mud outside the cottage, the fingerprints on the lamp within﻿—there is the state’s case, and there also, gentlemen, is ours. These sinister facts, impressive and terrible weapons in the state’s hands, under the clear white light of truth become a very simple, reasonable and inevitable set of circumstances, fully explained and fully accounted for.
